### ASSISTANT
# The Coffin Corner — Full Analysis & Teaching Breakdown### *How icing and storms eat your speed margin, and how to fly the trap*

---

## 1. What You Just Showed Me (Decoding Every Element)

This is the **"Coffin Corner"** teaching chart from *Legally Armed America* (sourced from FAA AC 61‑107B, *High‑Altitude Operations*, and *Aviation Week*). The caption's own caveat matters: **"Illustrative, not to scale"** and **"Learjet 60 limits per script."**

| Element | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **X‑axis** | Speed in Mach (M0.50 → M0.90) |
| **Y‑axis** | Altitude (25,000 → 45,000 ft) |
| **Red line (left/lower boundary)** | The **STALL boundary** — it slopes up-and-right: stall Mach *increases* with altitude. Label: "TOO SLOW: STALL" |
| **Orange vertical line (right boundary)** | The **overspeed boundary** (VMO/MMO). It is vertical because VMO is a *fixed Mach number* at any altitude. Label: "TOO FAST: OVERSPEED" |
| **Green shaded region** | The **SAFE GAP** — every Mach number in here is simultaneously above the stall and below the overspeed limit |
| **White dot @ ~35,000 ft, M0.76** | "Normal cruise M0.76" — the aircraft's actual position |
| **Orange dashed line @ 35,000 ft** | "Where they **were**" |
| **Blue dashed line @ 37,000 ft** | "Where they were **asked to go**" |
| **Step at 37,000 ft** | "Limit M0.81" below 37,000 → **"Limit M0.78 above 37,000"** — a *stepped* AFM limit, not a constant MMO |
| **Red rectangle** | The emphasis box: the thin sliver (~M0.78–0.82 / 31,000–39,500 ft) where the gap is nearly closed |
| **Banner** | "Up high, the gap between too slow and too fast shrinks" |
| **Peak ~43,000 ft** | Where the stall line *touches* the M0.78 limit — **the band has zero width** |

**The one‑line message:** At altitude your speed envelope has a *floor* (the stall) and a *ceiling* (the overspeed limit), and as you climb the floor rises toward the ceiling. Flying at cruise is therefore not "pick a speed" — it's "stay inside a gap that is actively closing."

---

## 2. The Physics: *Why* the Corner Exists (This Is the Part to Memorize)

### The governing relationship

A stall is a **dynamic‑pressure (q) event**, not a speed event:

$$q = \tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^2 \quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{Stall occurs at a near-constant IAS}$$

For a given weight and configuration, **Vs (IAS) is essentially constant** (~215 KIAS for the aircraft implied by this chart — I back‑calculated it and it holds to within a few knots from 25,000 to 43,500 ft).

Now convert that constant IAS into Mach:

$$M_{stall} = \frac{V_{IAS}}{\sqrt{\sigma}\cdot a} \approx \frac{V_{IAS}}{a\sqrt{\sigma}}$$

- **a** (speed of sound) **falls** with altitude (288.6 kt at SL → ~555 kt at 43,000 ft)
- **σ** (density ratio) **falls** with altitude (1.000 → 0.225)

So **1/(a·√σ) rises with altitude → Mstall rises with altitude**, while **VMO/MMO stays flat.** The two lines converge. That's the coffin.

### Proof by numbers (standard atmosphere, 215 KIAS stall, chart's limits)

| Altitude | a (kt) | σ | **Mstall** | **Limit** | Band (Mach) | Band (KIAS) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25,000 | 602 | 0.448 | 0.534 | 0.81 | 0.276 | ~150 kt |
| 30,000 | 590 | 0.374 | 0.596 | 0.81 | 0.214 | ~120 kt |
| **35,000** | 577 | 0.310 | **0.670** | **0.81** | **0.140** | **~45 kt** |
| **37,000** | 571 | 0.286 | **0.704** | **0.78** | **0.076** | **~24 kt** |
| 39,000 | 566 | 0.265 | 0.740 | 0.78 | 0.040 | ~12 kt |
| 41,000 | 561 | 0.244 | 0.776 | 0.78 | 0.004 | **~1 kt** |
| 43,000 | 556 | 0.225 | 0.814 | 0.78 | **0.000** | **ZERO** |

> **The headline numbers: At 37,000 ft the entire legal speed band is about 24 knots wide. At 39,000 ft it's 12 knots. At 41,000 ft it's 1 knot.**
>
> A 5‑knot pitot icing error, a 1‑knot trim error, or a small altimeter error is a large fraction of that.

### The scenario the chart is teaching

The aircraft was at **35,000 ft / M0.76** and was asked to climb to **37,000 ft**. Watch what 2,000 ft of climb did — it attacked the band **from both sides simultaneously**:

| | At 35,000 ft | At 37,000 ft | Change |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stall Mach | 0.670 | 0.704 | **+0.034** (floor rises ~19 kt TAS) |
| Max Mach | 0.81 | **0.78** | **−0.030** (ceiling drops ~17 kt TAS) |
| Usable band (TAS) | 81 kt | 44 kt | **−46%** |
| Margin to overspeed at M0.76 | 0.05 (~26 kt) | 0.02 (**~11 kt**) | **−58%** |

**Two thousand feet of climb cut the usable speed band nearly in half — and left the crew with roughly 11 knots of overspeed margin.** That is the trap. The climb didn't look dangerous; the paperwork made it look reasonable.

---

## 3. ICING: How It Devours the Margin### 3.1 What ice does to the numbers

FAA/manufacturer guidance (In‑Flight Icing Training Aid; *Pilot's Handbook*, AC 91‑74B; manufacturer FCOM icing notes):

- Ice on the leading edge **destroys the wing's leading‑edge shape**, dropping the **critical angle of attack by 5–10° or more**
- **Stall speed rises 5–15%** in icing; **severe icing can raise it by 20% or more**
- The stall may arrive with **little or no warning and no normal buffet** (because the stall AoA is lower, you never reach the buffet onset speed)
- **Aileron effectiveness degrades** — roll control may be lost *before* the wing stalls
- **Pitch authority shrinks** as ice on the horizontal stabilizer reduces tail lift (tailplane stall risk)

### 3.2 Apply it to the chart's scenario

Using the chart's own 215 KIAS clean stall at37,000 ft (band = 215 → 238.6 KIAS, only ~24 kt wide):

| Condition | New stall speed | Stall Mach @ FL370 | Band at M0.76 | Result |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clean | 215 KIAS | 0.704 | 0.076 Mach (~24 kt) | Flyable, tight |
| **Light ice (+5%)** | 226 KIAS | 0.740 | 0.020 Mach (~6 kt) | Practically unflyable |
| **Moderate ice (+10%)** | 236.5 KIAS | 0.775 | **BELOW STALL** | **You are stalled at normal cruise Mach** |
| **Severe ice (+20%)** | 258 KIAS | 0.845 | **NEGATIVE** | **No valid Mach exists** |

> ### ⚠️ The single most important sentence in this session:
> **At 37,000 ft, a 10% stall-speed penalty from moderate icing is enough to put a normally safe M0.76 *below* the stall.**
>
> The margin that ATC's climb request nearly erased is the *same* margin that icing will erase on its own. **You do not need both to happen — but in a storm you often get both.**

### 3.3 Where icing and the coffin corner overlap (this is why the two topics belong in one lesson)

Icing is concentrated between the **freezing level (0 °C)** and about **−20 °C**. In mid‑latitude summer the 0 °C level sits around **FL250–FL320** and the −20 °C level around **FL370–FL400**.

**So the most severe icing band (roughly −5 to −15 °C, high LWC) typically sits right where the coffin corner is narrowest.** The two hazards are *altitude-coincident*, not merely related.

Additional icing facts that make it worse up high:

- **Supercooled Large Droplets (SLD) / freezing drizzle** can produce icing at temperatures as warm as **−10 °C to −2 °C**, sometimes *above* the standard icing envelope, and is notorious for accreting **aft of the boot** where ice detection and boots don't reach
- **Ice crystal icing** in very cold air (below ~−40 °C) can still accrete rime at **FL380–FL450** — precisely where your band is1 knot wide
- **Wet hail / sleet** can be encountered at the freezing level with significant airframe damage risk at Mach
- **Anti‑ice/heat is thrust‑limited:** turning on engine anti‑ice and wing heat at cruise altitude costs thrust; combined with a Mach‑limited climb this can make the aircraft *unable to hold the requested altitude* — so the icing creates a **forced descent** into the weather you were trying to avoid

---

## 4. STORMS: How They Compound Everything

### 4.1 Convective weather mechanics that hit the corner

- **Vertical gusts** of ±2,000–4,000 ft/min in and around cells. A 3,000 ft/min updraft is an **instantaneous AoA excursion** — the wing sees an angle of attack your airspeed doesn't reflect. The airspeed indicator says you're at M0.76; the wing may be stalled.
- **Turbulence reduces the lift available** at a given AoA and can put you on the **back side of the drag curve** in a thin margin.
- **Icing + turbulence + the corner** = the classic triple trap: reduced stall AoA (ice), increased AoA excursion (gust), and no margin to absorb either (corner).
- **Turbulence penetration speed may not exist.** Recommended penetration speed is often VMO/VMO‑ish. At FL370, the *highest* Mach you may legally select is M0.78 — **you may not be able to select the recommended turbulence speed at all.** You are limited by the AFM, not by the aerodynamics.
- **Anvil/overhang cloud** near the tropopause contains supercooled droplets at low liquid water content — subtle, easy‑to‑miss icing exactly at FL350–450.

### 4.2 The paradox you must be able to resolve

The instinctive recovery from icing at altitude is **descend** (cold air + margin). But the descent path from FL370 runs **down through the icing layer**, and in a convective environment it can run directly into **a cell, a downdraft, or a microburst.**

**Resolution — the descent must be decisive and vertical:**
1. **Continue through the icing layer; do not level off in it.** Use full anti‑ice, wings level, slightly above stall speed, and *complete* the descent to clear air. Leveling off in the icing layer is the worst option.
2. **Turn around / deviate laterally** if the cell is ahead and the turn is survivable — 180° at 25° bank at FL370 takes 2–4 minutes.
3. **Don't descend below the altimeter setting (29.92)** unless terrain or an approach requires it — altimeter error at altitude is significant and the terrain is the thing that kills you in the descent.
4. **Get below the freezing level, then continue to a level where the band is wide again** (FL250 or below =100+ knots of margin).

> **The rule that beats every technique: weather avoidance is the only permanent cure.** Coffin‑corner management is what you do when avoidance failed. If you're maneuvering in the corner, you're already in the failure chain.

### 4.3 The classic storm‑related accident chain in the corner

`Icing accretes → stall speed rises → crew slows (or fails to accelerate) to stay under MMO → AoA increases → reduced stall AoA → stall break, often asymmetric → ice‑contaminated wing stalls, roll may be uncommandable → altitude loss → CFIT/terrain or spin`

Notice the cruel part: **the crew's correct instinct ("slow down, I'm close to the limit") is exactly the wrong one**, because slowing down moves you *toward* the ice‑raised stall speed.

---

## 5. How To Actually Avoid Underspeed & Stall (The Procedure)

### 5.1 Before you leave: build your band card

Do this on the ground for every altitude you'll fly, and carry it in the flight bag.

**For each level, write four numbers:**
1. **Mstall** at your *actual* weight (AMM/AFM chart; scales ≈ √W, so +5,000 lb ≈ +4% stall speed)
2. **MMO/VMO** for that altitude — *check for stepped limits* (this chart's M0.81 → M0.78 at 37,000 ft is exactly the kind of trap)
3. **Target Mach** in the middle of the band
4. **Your escape altitude** — the altitude at which you would descend to get clean air

**Also on the ground:** know the **0 °C and −20 °C levels** for your route. If your planned cruise altitude sits inside the icing band *and* inside the coffin corner, you have designed a trap. Change the cruise altitude.

### 5.2 In flight: manage the *margin*, not the number

- **Recompute the band at every level change.** The stall Mach rises continuously with altitude even if your Mach doesn't change. Every 1,000 ft you climb, the floor moves up.
- **Maintain ≥10–15 kt above the stall** (or the operator's 10% icing allowance) and **≥10 kt / 0.02 Mach below the overspeed limit**. If the band can't hold both, the answer is *altitude*, not airspeed.
- **Never accept a level change that puts you above your MMO.** If the level you're given has a lower Mach limit, understand *why* (structural, flutter, empennage) — it is not a number to be "tested."
- **Watch the thrust, not just the speed.** A climb that can't be held means the margin is eroding from below as you climb. Anti‑ice on = less thrust = a Mach‑limited climb is even harder.
- **Trust but verify the airspeed.** Pitot icing gives a *low* airspeed reading → you keep accelerating toward overspeed. Static icing gives a *high* altitude reading → you're lower than you think, with less stall margin than you computed. Above FL410, IAS indication error can exceed 20 kt (AC 61‑107B) — **use the Mach indication and a GPS TAS cross‑check.**
- **Know your FMA/FMC speed limit behavior.** On Airbus, an MACH SPD target above the limit produces ACCEL DECEL with a MACH speed limit; on Boeing the FMC clamps the commanded speed to VMO/MMO. Don't let the automation surprise you.
- **Mach tuck:** at high Mach, pulling back on the yoke pitches the nose *up* (elevator effectiveness falls with increasing Mach). In icing this is fatal — pulling is the wrong input.

### 5.3 If you suspect icing (in the corner or not)

1. **Confirm:** "ICE" indication, windshield deposits, wing/strut ice, AOA change, roll trim change, loss of performance.
2. **Activate anti‑ice per SOP** and monitor the ice detector.
3. **Do not just maintain altitude and hope.** Decide: climb out of the layer, descend out of the layer, or exit the weather laterally.
4. **Descend decisively if the layer is above you** and the ground allows.
5. **Do not accelerate toward the MMO to "build margin."** If the MMO is your ceiling, speed is not a remedy — only altitude and ice shedding are.
6. **Do not descend into a cell.** If the descent path is convective, turn around instead.

### 5.4 Stall recovery in icing (memorize this)

- **Reduce AoA, full nose-up, maximum thrust, wings level, accept the altitude loss.** Do not pull to hold altitude — that's how ice-contaminated stalls become unrecoverable.
- **Expect an abrupt, possibly asymmetric break**, and expect **no prior buffet.**
- **If it's a tailplane stall** (high Mach + ice + AoA excursion): the nose will feel light and the trim may pitch nose‑down. **The recovery is nose‑down (reduce AoA) — pulling makes it worse.** A wing stall and a tailplane stall are recovered in *opposite* directions.
- **After recovery:** level off, maintain power, clean up the ice slowly, and **do not exceed the overspeed limit while cleaning up** (iced surfaces + high speed = flutter risk).

### 5.5 Self‑check questions (use these to drill the class)

1. Why does stall speed in *Mach* increase with altitude even though stall speed in *knots IAS* doesn't change?
2. At 37,000 ft with a 24‑knot total band, what is your reaction to a 5‑knot airspeed error?
3. Your aircraft is at 215 KIAS clean stall. A forecaster says "light icing." Recompute your band at FL370.
4. What are the two ways ice and the coffin corner attack your margin from *opposite* directions? (Answer: ice raises the floor; the MMO caps the ceiling — and the ceiling is *fixed*.)
5. You're in icing at FL370 and your autopilot is in Mach mode with a target of M0.77. The limit is M0.78. What can you actually do to gain margin?
6. Why is "descend" a potentially dangerous icing maneuver above FL350, and what are the two mitigations?
7. Where is the 0 °C level on your route today, and is your planned cruise altitude above or below it?
8. If ATC offers you FL370 and you're at FL350 in icing, what's your first calculation?

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ALT Mstall LIMIT    BAND      KIAS BAND           │
│  25,000   0.53     0.81     0.276     ~150 kt            │
│  30,000   0.60     0.81     0.214     ~120 kt            │
│  35,000   0.67     0.81     0.140      ~45 kt            │
│  37,000   0.70     0.78     0.076      ~24 kt   ← DANGER  │
│  39,000   0.74     0.78     0.040      ~12 kt            │
│  41,000   0.78     0.78     0.004       ~1 kt   ← CLOSED  │
│  43,000   0.81     0.78     0.000 0      ← NO BAND  │
│                                                          │
│  ICE PENALTY: +5% → 5kt of band +10% → band gone    │
│               +20% → no valid Mach                       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

## 7. Key Terminology to Add to Your Vocabulary

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Coffin corner** | The altitude band where the stall speed (Mach) and the overspeed limit converge, leaving a very narrow usable speed range |
| **Mstall** | Stall speed expressed as a Mach number — rises with altitude because both sound speed and density fall |
| **VMO / MMO** | Maximum operating speed, expressed as a Mach number — constant with altitude (a vertical line on the chart) |
| **Low‑speed buffet / buffet onset** | The precursor to stall; in icing it may never appear before the stall |
| **Mach tuck** | The nose‑up pitching tendency when pulling at high Mach, due to reduced elevator effectiveness with increasing Mach |
| **Tailplane stall** | Loss of horizontal stabilizer effectiveness at high Mach and high AoA; caused or worsened by tail ice; **recovered nose‑down** |
| **Thrust decay** | Thrust falls off with altitude; small thrust reductions turn a marginal climb into a descent |
| **Rime vs. clear ice** | Rime is rough, porous, aerodynamically destructive; clear ice is smooth, dense, and de‑icing‑resistant |
| **SLD / freezing drizzle** | Supercooled large droplets — icing at surprisingly warm temperatures, often behind the boots |
| **RVSM airspace** | FL290–FL410 in the U.S.; where most jet cruise happens and where the corner bites |

---

# POST‑SUMMARY — The 10 Things That Matter Most

**1. The coffin corner is a *trap*, not a wall.** You are not choosing "a speed" — you are choosing a *position inside a gap that is closing*. The only real cure is to **widen the gap**, not to manage your position inside it.

**2. Why the floor rises:** the stall is a dynamic‑pressure event at a constant IAS; because sound speed and air density both fall with altitude, the *same* IAS becomes a *higher* Mach. Mstall ≈ Vs(IAS) / (a·√σ).

**3. Why the ceiling is flat:** VMO/MMO is a fixed Mach number, so it draws a vertical line — and the two lines intersect at ~43,000 ft for this aircraft.

**4. The killer stat: at 37,000 ft the entire legal speed band is ~24 knots wide.** At 39,000 ft it's 12 knots; at 41,000 ft it's **1 knot**. A 5‑knot airspeed error is a fifth of your entire margin.

**5. A 2,000‑ft climb can halve your band.** Climbing 35,000 → 37,000 raised the stall Mach 0.034 *and* dropped the max Mach 0.030, leaving ~11 knots of overspeed margin at normal cruise. **Always recompute the band after a level change.**

**6. Watch for *stepped* limits.** This aircraft's max Mach is M0.81 below 37,000 and **M0.78 above** it. Never accept a level change without checking the AFM for that level's actual limit.

**7. Icing attacks the *same* margin ATC just took away.** Ice raises stall speed 5–15% (20%+ in severe icing) and lowers stall AoA so the buffet warning disappears. **At 37,000 ft, moderate icing alone puts M0.76 below the stall.**

**8. The severe icing band and the corner are altitude‑coincident.** The worst icing (−5 to −15 °C, high LWC) typically sits at FL330–FL400 — exactly where your band collapses. These are one problem, not two.

**9. In the corner, slowing down is the wrong instinct.** The MMO caps your ceiling, so speed is not a remedy; **altitude and shedding ice are.** But descending runs you through the icing layer and possibly into a cell — so **descend decisively and vertically through the layer, or turn around.** Never level off in the icing.

**10. Recovery facts to have automatic:** Wing stall → full nose‑up, max power, level wings, **accept altitude loss**. Tailplane stall (high Mach + ice) → **nose‑down**. And never pull to hold altitude in icing.

**Bonus — the three‑second pre‑acceptance check before you take a level change:**
> ① What is my stall Mach at the *new* altitude? ② What is the max Mach at the *new* altitude? ③ Where is the freezing level relative to both — and where do I go if ice appears?

### ASSISTANT
# Case Study: N722BD — Learjet 60, Palm Beach County (16 Feb 2025)

### *A forensic rebuild of the coffin corner, icing, and the descent — plus a fact-check of the video's claims*

---

## PART A — Ground Truth First

### A.1 What the transcript actually contains

| Segment | Content | Type |
|---|---|---|
| Ch. 1 | Framing: "Mount Everest to a Florida street in about 80 seconds"; 9 fatalities; crater5.5 miles from Mar-a-Lago; Trump flew out 42 min prior | Narrative/emotive + insinuation |
| Ch. 2 | **Aircraft:2004 Learjet 60, N722BD, Austin TX → Nassau.**9 on board. FO reported as Natalia Gadella, 20, ~1,300 hrs. At FL350, crew reported **600–700 ft altitude swings** ("moderate turbulence") and **"light clear ice that is manageable… minus 47 °C outside."** Crew **requested a climb to 37,000** because they were "picking up precipitation." Miami Center approved: *"Final maintain flight level 370."* | **Primary — these are the load-bearing facts** |
| Ch. 3 | Coffin corner explanation (M0.76 cruise vs M0.81 limit); aircraft "flew into a heavy storm cell right as that climb started"; ADS-B: GS 437→505 kt while descending20,000 fpm; Mayday at 22,000 fpm descent, 60° bank; "Mach tuck"; FAA upset guidance quoted | Technical interpretation |
| Ch. 4 | Impact near-vertical over Military Trail; no ground casualties; ~600 residents relocated; DeSantis "looked like a missile" | Aftermath |
| Ch. 5–8 | Missile/TFR/Stinger/Avenger/F-16 speculation, **then largely walked back** by the host; ownership (LR60271LLC, Wilmington DE — company named for model + serial); Aurora Aviation charter certificate; FO qualifications discussion; host's own conclusion: *"Everything kind of looks at and confirms the weather story"* | Speculation, self-corrected |

**Important framing:** This is **commentary/entertainment content** (note the suppressor ad read at 7:03 and the book promo at 15:14), not an investigative product. It has a thesis and it pushes it. Treat it as a hypothesis generator, not a finding.

### A.2 The critical timeline (from the video's own ADS-B data)

```
FL350, M0.76, ~437 kt GS — "light clear ice",600–700 ft turbulence        ↓  crew asks for FL370 ("picking up precipitation") → APPROVED
        ↓  aircraft turns toward / climbs into the cell
FL370  ←── the trap is set
        ↓  descent begins (~20,000 fpm, GS 505 kt)
        ↓  "We have an overspeed condition" / Mayday
        ↓  "We're descending 22,000 [ft] and we're banking 60"
8,750 ft, VS >32,000 fpm (saturated readout)
        ↓  impact, near vertical, Military Trail
```

### A.3 The speculation problem — settled, briefly and firmly

The host spends ~5 minutes on missiles, then **disproves his own theory with the geometry** and states there is no evidence of any launch. The arithmetic:

- **TFR ceiling:** inner ring (10 nm) topped out at **17,999 ft**. The aircraft was at **35,000–37,000 ft** — it was *above the restricted airspace entirely* and legally so.
- **Stinger/Avenger envelope:** ~12,500 ft ceiling, 4–5 miles engagement range. The aircraft began its uncontrolled descent at **35,400 ft, 10.5 miles west of Mar-a-Lago** — nearly 3× too high, 2× too far.
- **It was already in a runaway descent** before it ever got inside any conceivable engagement envelope.
- **NORAD and the Secret Service have said nothing.** No launch imagery, no corroboration, no F-16 scramble record.

> **For your training: extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence.** There is none here. The flight-deck evidence (radio, ADS-B, kinematics) fully supports the weather-and-handling explanation. Probable cause is the NTSB's determination to be made on the record — not from a forum thread, and not from insinuation about the occupant of Mar-a-Lago. Nine families deserve a serious investigation, not a spectator sport.

---

## PART B — The Aerodynamics, Reconstructed

### B.1 The band at FL350 vs. FL370 (clean, using the chart's implied ~215 KIAS stall)

| | FL350 | FL370 (the requested climb) |
|---|---|---|
| Speed of sound *a* | 577 kt | 571 kt |
| Density ratio σ | 0.310 | 0.286 |
| **Mstall (clean)** | **0.670** | **0.704** |
| **Max Mach (limit)** | **0.81** | **0.78** ← limit *drops* |
| Total band | 0.140 Mach | 0.076 Mach |
| **Band in KIAS** | 215 → 260 (**45 kt**) | 215 → 238.6 (**23.6 kt**) |
| Aircraft's position (M0.76) | 244 KIAS | 232.4 KIAS |
| Margin to stall | 29 kt | 17.4 kt |
| **Margin to overspeed** | 16 kt | **6.2 kt** |

### B.2 Now add the ice — **THE SMOKING GUN CALCULATION**

FAA/manufacturer guidance: ice raises stall speed **5–15%**, and **20%+** in severe icing.

**Climbing FL350 → FL370 at M0.76, in moderate icing (+10% stall speed = 236.5 KIAS):**

```
Aircraft IAS at FL350, M0.76 ......... 244.0 KIAS
  (climbing at constant Mach loses ~5.5 KIAS per 1,000 ft of altitude)
Aircraft IAS at FL370, M0.76 ......... 232.4 KIAS
Icing-adjusted stall speed ............ 236.5 KIAS
                                        ─────────────
AT THE TOP OF THE CLIMB, THE AIRCRAFT WAS~4 KNOTS BELOW ITS OWN ICING-ADJUSTED STALL SPEED.

And the overspeed limit at FL370 (M0.78) was 238.6 KIAS — 6 kt above them.

TOTAL USABLE BAND IN ICE AT FL370: 236.5 → 238.6 KIAS  ≈  2 KNOTS
```

> ### The entire legal speed envelope for that aircraft, in that condition, at that altitude, was about **two knots wide.**
>
> There was no correct Mach number to fly. The band was not "narrow" — it was **closed.** The aircraft was, in the physical sense, **between the stall and the overspeed limit simultaneously.**
>
> (Model caveat: uses the chart's implied ~215 KIAS stall and ISA. The real numbers come from the AFM and actual weight. The *structure* of the calculation is what matters, and it is solid.)

### B.3 The three separate errors in that climb request

The crew's reason for climbing was *"we're picking up precipitation"* — i.e., **they were already in cloud, already in icing, and already in600–700 ft of turbulence, and their instinct was to go UP.** That single decision attacked the margin from three directions simultaneously:

1. **It raised the floor.** Stall Mach0.670 → 0.704 (the stall speed in Mach rises because *a* and σ fall).
2. **It dropped the ceiling.** M0.81 → M0.78 (a stepped AFM limit — the very thing the chart in your first message highlights).
3. **It moved them toward the cell** — into the worst icing, the worst turbulence, and the coldest air, with **no escape level above and a contaminated wing.**

And note the trap inside the trap: **climbing at constant Mach in a climb *reduces* IAS by ~5.5 kt per 1,000 ft** (because *a* falls). So the crew's chosen Mach was quietly bleeding them into the stall while they were busy thinking about the weather.

### B.4 Reconstructing the dive — the part the video never does

Using the video's own ADS-B numbers and standard atmosphere:

| Phase | Alt | VS | GS | Flight path angle | Est. TAS | **Est. Mach** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Start of descent | ~37,000 | climbing | 437 kt | — | ~434 kt | **0.76** |
| +35 s | ~25,300 | −20,000 fpm | 505 kt | 21° | 544 kt | **0.90** |
| End of data | 8,750 | −32,000+ fpm | 505–550 kt | 32–36° | 597–635 kt | **0.93 – 0.99** |

*(VS = GS·sin γ; TAS = GS·cos γ; Mach = TAS/a at altitude.)*

> ### **The aircraft was at Mach 0.90 within35 seconds of losing control — and near Mach 1.0 at the end.**
>
> The LR60's structural limit is **M0.80**. At M0.93 the airframe was **~16% beyond its certified limit speed**, and in a 60° bank pulling 2–3 G, dynamic pressure loads were roughly **3–4× design limits**. In *IAS* terms at 8,750 ft, ~524 KIAS against a VMO of 340 KIAS — a **54% overspeed.**

**This is the finding the video missed entirely.** It framed the final phase as "Mach tuck + spiral." The real story is: **the aircraft entered a full-blown overspeed spiral dive** — the same structural-failure scenario as Air Atlanta 965 and SAS 751 — which explains why the impact was near-vertical and so violent that the NTSB must determine whether the airframe broke up **in the air** or on impact.

### B.5 Candidate loss-of-control mechanisms (ranked by fit to the evidence)

| # | Mechanism | Fit | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Tailplane stall / Mach-tuck from tail ice at high AoA** | **High** | Ice on the horizontal stabilizer at M0.76+ with an AoA excursion from turbulence → sudden, violent nose-down, unrecoverable trim. Classic catastrophic icing signature. |
| **2** | **Wing stall with airframe ice (asymmetric roll-off)** | **High** | Reduced stall AoA + no buffet warning + turbulence → sudden break → wing drop → 60° bank. |
| **3** | **Upset initiated by severe turbulence, unrecovered** | **Medium-High** | 600–700 ft swings is severe, not moderate. If the crew didn't recover wings-level, the nose drops and the spiral begins. |
| **4** | **Overspeed + spiral** | Consequence, not cause | Whatever initiated it, the recovery phase became an overspeed spiral dive. |

Note that **#1 and #2 both share the same precursor: the crew was already at or below the icing-adjusted stall speed at FL370.** That's the thread that ties it all together — the loss of control was *predictable from the numbers*, and the numbers were computable **before** they climbed.

### B.6 Correcting the video's technical language

| Video says | More accurate |
|---|---|
| "Mach tuck" = "once a jet blows past its speed limit, the wing pushes the nose down on its own" | **Mach tuck** is a nose-down pitching moment from shock-wave formation on the wing as Mach increases (reducing elevator effectiveness). It's real, but it's an *aerodynamic tendency* you must trim against — not an automatic uncontrollable dive. **The dive here was a spiral dive, not Mach tuck.** Two different problems, different recoveries. |
| "Pull back? No, level the wings first, then pull up" | **Close, and the right instinct.** Precise FAA/NTSB guidance (AC 120-111; NTSB *Airplane Upset Management*): **roll the wings toward level while bringing the nose toward the horizon; do NOT pull to stop the descent.** Adding back pressure in a bank tightens the spiral and adds g-load. Pull to the *horizon*, never to arrest the *descent rate*. |
| "Moderate turbulence" (600–700 ft swings) | **That is severe turbulence.** Calling it moderate is itself a red flag — see the decision chain below. |
| "Ground speed jumped 437→505" | Correctly reported — but the video never converts GS to **TAS and Mach**, which is where the M0.9 discovery lives. |

### B.7 Could they have saved it? Honest assessment of the recovery window

**From the start of the descent:** Realistically, no. Once a 60°-bank spiral dive is established at 20,000+ fpm with the airframe accelerating past M0.90, a two-pilot crew in icing, in cloud, with no visual horizon, was fighting a spiraling, overspeeding, possibly structurally overloaded aircraft. **That phase was very likely unrecoverable.**

**From FL350, ~2 minutes earlier:** **Absolutely recoverable.** Multiple escape routes existed:
- Descend to below the 0 °C level / out of the cloud (there was a perfectly good level below them)
- Request a lateral deviation around the cell (radar was available; they could have asked)
- Decline the climb and request lower**This is the whole lesson: the accident was not decided in the last 60 seconds. It was decided in the request for FL370.**

---

## PART C — Fact-Check of the Video's Technical Claims

| Claim | Verdict | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Learjet 60 cruises M0.76, limit M0.81; limit tightens with altitude | ✅ **Correct** | Per chart; verify exact AFM figures for your specific tail number/serial (LR60s have serial-specific limitations) |
| Coffin corner narrows as altitude increases | ✅ **Correct** | Sound speed and density both fall; Mstall rises while MMO stays flat |
| "If it flies too fast, shock waves form on the wings" | ⚠️ **Simplified** | Shock waves form well below the overspeed limit; the actual overspeed danger is **structural load and flutter**, not "shock waves" per se |
| Turbulence "knocks the speed up and down, and hundredths of a Mach go real quick" | ✅ **Correct** | And worse: an AoA excursion is *not* the same as an airspeed change — the wing can stall while the IAS shows normal |
| Aircraft "flew into a heavy storm cell right as that climb started" | ⚠️ **Unverified** | Based on public radar; the NTSB has the authoritative data |
| "Mach tuck" caused the nose-down | ❌ **Misidentified** | It was a spiral dive. Mach tuck may have contributed as a *contributing factor* (elevator power loss at high Mach + ice), but the primary was the spiral |
| "Level the wings first, and only then pull up" | ⚠️ **Directionally right, imprecisely stated** | Roll toward level *while* bringing the nose to the horizon; never pull to stop the descent |
| 600–700 ft altitude swings = "moderate turbulence" | ❌ **Understated** | That's severe turbulence |
| Missile/TFR/Avenger theory | ❌ **No evidence** | Disproven by the host's own geometry; no launch, no corroboration |
| "Everything looks at and confirms the weather story" | ✅ **Correct conclusion** | This is the honest summary of the physical evidence |
| FO age/qualifications as a causal factor | ⚠️ **Legitimate question, unsupported as a finding** | Total time ≠ proficiency; what matters is time-in-type, recency, and task-specific training. The investigation determines facts — not the commentary |

**One more thing the video gets right and deserves credit:** the ADS-B reconstruction. Reading public ADS-B (position, velocity, altitude) is legitimate, verifiable, and it's how the descent profile was established. **That's the investigative mindset worth copying: start from the recorded data, not from the narrative.**

---

## PART D — The Decision Chain (where it actually went wrong)

```
ROOT CAUSE OF THE ACCIDENT
  │
  └── The crew accepted an environment (FL350, icing, severe turbulence)
      that was already at the edge of the envelope ── while reporting it      as "manageable"
      │
      ├── ERROR 1: MISDIAGNOSIS
      │ "Light clear ice that is manageable" +600–700 ft swings
      │     called "moderate turbulence"
      │     → They believed they had more margin than they did.
      │     → Their own words show the margin was already near zero.
      │
      ├── ERROR 2: THE CLIMB REQUEST (the decisive error)
      │     "Picking up precipitation → climb to 37,000"
      │     → Narrowed the band from BOTH sides (floor up, ceiling down)
      │     → Carried the aircraft into the cell
      │     → Removed the escape level
      │     → Left ~2 kt of usable band (see §B.2)
      │
      ├── ERROR 3: NO ICING ESCAPE PLAN / NO WEATHER DEVIATION
      │     Radar was available. A lower level was available.
      │     A lateral deviation was available. None was requested.
      │
      ├── ERROR 4: LOSS OF CONTROL
      │     Tailplane stall or ice-contaminated wing stall
      │     (both triggered by an AoA excursion in turbulence
      │      with the aircraft already at/below icing stall speed)
      │     → 60° bank, nose down, unrecovered      │
      └── ERROR 5: THE SPIRAL DIVE
            → speed built to M0.90–0.99 (structural limit M0.80)
            → load factor2–3 G at1.35× limit q
            → likely structural overload / breakup
            → impact```

**Note where the chain could have been broken at any of five places — and note that all five breaks happen BEFORE the loss of control.** That is the single most encouraging thing about this case: *it was not a hopeless accident.*

---

## PART E — What the NTSB Must Answer (Your Study Guide)

The commentary is not the record. These are the primary questions, and each maps to a document:

| # | Question | Where the answer lives |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Was the loss of control a stall, a tailplane stall, or an upset? | FDR/QAR data; wreckage analysis; Factual Report |
| 2 | **Did the airframe break up in the air?** (my M0.9 estimate says it must have been over limit) | Wreckage distribution, scene survey, structural analysis |
| 3 | Exact weight, balance, thrust available, de-ice thrust penalty | Weight & Balance; Aircraft Performance Report |
| 4 | Actual stall speed at that weight/config; actual icing stall-speed penalty | AFM + certified icing data |
| 5 | Were the de-ice boots cycling, wing heat on, engine anti-ice on? Any MEL item affecting ice protection? | Maintenance records; Maintenance Report |
| 6 | Was the aircraft equipped and certified for known icing? Was the crew trained for it? | Aircraft records; operator training records (135.293, 135.301) |
| 7 | **Was there an icing AIRMET/SIGMET for that area and time?** Was it in the briefing? | Weather archive; ATC records |
| 8 | Was the flight on radar service? Where was the cell on ATC's scope? Did the controller have it? | ATC radar/FAA facility records |
| 9 | Was the crew's climb request reasonable, and did ATC have the information to deny it? | ATC transcript; radar |
| 10 | Crew experience: time-in-type, 90-day currency (61.57), carry-passengers currency (61.31), company training | Airman records; operator records |
| 11 | Was a known-icing avoidance plan in place for this flight? | Dispatch/flight release records |
| 12 | Was 14 CFR 91.137 satisfied (equipment, provisions, request to climb above the icing layer)? | Flight/certificate of release |

> **14 CFR 91.137** is the rule that bites here: it requires the PIC in icing to ensure the aircraft is equipped, ensure adequate provisions for icing operations, and **request ATC clearance to climb above the icing layer where practicable.** The crew's climb request was *procedurally consistent* with the rule — and it was still the worst thing they could have done, because they were climbing into the coffin corner rather than descending out of it.
>
> **Lesson: a rule can tell you what is permitted. It cannot tell you what is survivable.** Always ask the second question.

**Go get the primary record:** NTSB **CAROL** (accident database) → search *N722BD* or *Learjet 60 / Palm Beach, FL / 16 Feb 2025* → read the **Preliminary Report**, then the **Factual/Circumstantial Reports**, the **Performance/Structural/Wreckage reports**, and the final **Probable Cause Determination**. Also pull the **NTSB's Aircraft Accident Report (AAR)** when published, and the **Safety Recommendations**. That record will settle every question the video speculated about.

---

## PART F — Your Updated Procedures

### F.1 The pre-flight rule that would have prevented this

**Never accept a climb into weather if it takes you above your icing-adjusted stall Mach.** Build the card:

```
For every planned level:
   Mstall(alt, weight, config)  =  Vs(IAS) / (a · √σ)
   Mstall_ice =  Mstall × (1 + 5% to 20%)     ← the number that matters
   Upper limit =  MMO(alt)   ← check for STEPPED limits (M0.81 → M0.78)
   Usable band =  MMO_ice − Mstall_ice

If Usable band < ~0.03 Mach (or< 25 KIAS), that level is NOT survivable in icing.
Your plan must then be: descend below the freezing level, or deviate.
```

### F.2 Enroute rules

1. **The climb into icing above FL290 is a red-flag decision.** If you're in icing and someone suggests climbing, ask: *what's my icing-adjusted stall Mach at the new level, and what's the ceiling there?*
2. **In icing, the MMO is a wall, not a target.** Speed is not a remedy. **Altitude and shedding ice are the only remedies.**
3. **Descend decisively.** Never level off inside the icing layer. Never descend below the altimeter setting unless terrain demands it.
4. **Know your "no-go" icing level in advance** (0 °C and −20 °C heights) and pick a cruise level that isn't inside it.
5. **Radar is a crew resource.** If the radar shows a cell, deviate — don't request a climb into it.
6. **Know your turbulence penetration speed — and check it against your Mach limit.** At FL370 in icing, the highest legal Mach may be *below* the recommended penetration speed. You may have no choice but to accept less.

### F.3 Recovery procedures (memorize)

**Icing-induced wing stall:**
> Full nose-up (reduce AoA) · Maximum thrust · Level wings · **Accept the altitude loss — never pull to hold altitude** · Expect an abrupt, asymmetric break with no prior buffet · After recovery, level off, clean up slowly, and respect the overspeed limit while cleaning.

**Tailplane stall (high Mach + ice + AoA excursion):**
> **Nose DOWN — reduce AoA.** Pulling makes it worse. Expect a sudden nose-down pitch and a trim change.

**Spiral dive:**
> **Roll toward level while bringing the nose to the horizon. Do not pull to stop the descent. Manage speed with thrust.** Note that your airframe may already be over limit — but rolling out and unloading the wing is still the only recovery.

**And the one that applies before all of these:** in a nose-low, high-bank attitude, **don't pull**. Add energy only after the wings are toward level and the nose is near the horizon.

### F.4 The meta-lesson for your career

> **"Manageable" is the most dangerous word in the cockpit.** The crew said *light clear ice that is manageable* and *moderate turbulence* — while their own data (600–700 ft of vertical excursion) and their own airplane's numbers said otherwise. **Goodpilots don't manage margins; they preserve them.** If your margin is gone, the answer is altitude and weather, never speed.

---

# POST-SUMMARY — The 10 Things That Matter Most

**1. The climb request was the accident.** Not the descent. Everything about the FL370 request was wrong simultaneously: it raised the stall Mach (0.670→0.704), *dropped* the overspeed limit (M0.81→M0.78), carried them into the cell, and removed the escape level.

**2. The smoking-gun number: at FL370 in moderate icing, the usable band was about 2 knots wide.** Aircraft at 232.4 KIAS vs. icing-adjusted stall at 236.5 KIAS vs. overspeed limit at 238.6 KIAS. **They were simultaneously below the stall and near the limit.** There was no good Mach number to fly.

**3. Climbing at constant Mach *reduces* IAS by ~5.5 kt per 1,000 ft.** The crew's Mach choice was quietly bleeding them into the stall while they focused on the weather. Always recompute the band in the new level.

**4. Severe icing and the coffin corner are altitude-coincident.** The worst icing (−5 to −15 °C) typically sits at FL330–FL400, exactly where the band is thinnest. These are one problem, not two.

**5. In the coffin corner, speed is not a remedy.** The MMO caps the ceiling. Your only levers are **altitude** and **shedding ice**.

**6. The video missed the biggest aerodynamic fact.** Its own ADS-B data proves the aircraft was at **Mach 0.90 within 35 seconds and near Mach 1.0 at the end** — against an M0.80 structural limit, at 2–3 G. This was an **overspeed spiral dive**, not "Mach tuck," and the NTSB will have to determine whether the airframe broke up in the air.

**7. Learn the two stalls, because they are recovered in opposite directions.** Wing stall → nose up. Tailplane stall (high Mach + ice) → nose down. Pulling in a tailplane stall is fatal.

**8. Spiral dive recovery: roll toward level, bring the nose to the horizon, do not pull to stop the descent, manage speed with thrust.** Adding back pressure in a bank tightens the spiral and adds g-load.

**9. "Moderate turbulence" of 600–700 ft is severe turbulence.** The crew's own words reveal the real failure: **they believed they had margin they did not have.** "Manageable" and "moderate" are words that precede every icing accident.

**10. Get the primary record yourself.** NTSB CAROL → preliminary and final reports for N722BD, Palm Beach County, 16 Feb 2025. The commentary video is entertainment with an ad read in it and a thesis; the NTSB report is the finding. **On the missile theory: there is no evidence, the geometry excludes it, and the flight-deck data fully explains the crash.** Probable cause is determined on the record — not in the comments.

## ONE‑MINUTE STRIP — The numbers that actually govern you

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  10 kt above stall │ 10 kt / 0.02 Mach below MMO   │  Mstall RISES      │
│  (at least)          │  (at least)                   │  ~0.017 Mach /      │
│                      │                                │  1,000 ft │
│  Ice: stall speed +5 to +15%;  +20% or more in severe icing │
│  Constant Mach in a CLIMB: IAS FALLS ~5.7 kt per 1,000 ft                  │
│  Band < 0.03 Mach or < 25 KIAS in ice  →  BAND CLOSED  →  DESCEND.        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

| Normal cruise Mach | ~M0.76 | Video/chart | **[I]** |

> ### ⚠ WHY THE TWO MACH LIMITS ARE MARKED [I]
> The video/chart gives M0.81 below FL370 and M0.78 above it. **I have not verified those against any AFM**, and LR60 limitations are often **serial‑specific** (airframe, empennage, flutter, and MEL items can impose *lower* limits than the generic AFM value). **The MMO you fly must come from your AFM for your tail number.** M0.80 is a ceiling on what you may do — never something to test against.

> ### ⚠ VMO/MMO IS NOT THE ONLY LIMIT
> Also in force: flap/gear placard speeds, Vfe/Vlo, maneuvering/structural limits, MEL restrictions, icing configuration limits, and the **stall** itself. The *first* one you hit is the one that matters.

## 3 · THE BAND — ISA REFERENCE (PUBLISHED) + METHOD

```
 ISA ATMOSPHERE  [P]                                    Mstall  =  Vs(IAS) ÷ (a · √σ)
                                                        IAS     =  M · a · √σ
  Alt(ft)   a(kt)    σ      √σ        Alt(ft)   a(kt)    σ      √σ
   25,000   602.0   0.4482  0.6695     37,000   571.4   0.2862  0.5350
   30,000   589.4   0.3741  0.6116     39,000   566.1   0.2647  0.5145
   35,000   576.5   0.3100  0.5567     41,000   560.9   0.2441  0.4941
   43,000   555.5   0.2255  0.4748     45,000   549.8   0.2067  0.4547

        STALL (floor)                                    OVERSPEED (ceiling)
              \ │
 \    ← gap NARROWS ~0.017 Mach / 1,000 ft   │
                \___________________________________________\│ CLOSES ≈ FL410–430```

---

## 4 · WORKED BAND TABLE — **[I] ILLUSTRATIVE ONLY**

**Assumption used:** constant clean stall **215 KIAS** (back‑calculated from the video chart: the chart's stall line implies ~215 KIAS at every level). **This is the chart's number, not necessarily your aircraft's.** See §4b.

| Altitude | a (kt) | **Mstall** | Limit (chart) | Band (Mach) | Limit (IAS) | **Band (KIAS)** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25,000 | 602.0 | 0.533 | 0.81 | 0.277 | 326.4 | **111 kt** |
| 30,000 | 589.4 | 0.596 | 0.81 | 0.214 | 292.0 | **77 kt** |
| 35,000 | 576.5 | **0.670** | 0.81 | 0.140 | 260.0 | **45 kt** |
| **37,000** | 571.4 | **0.703** | **0.78** | 0.077 | 238.4 | **23 kt** |
| 39,000 | 566.1 | 0.738 | 0.78 | 0.042 | 227.2 | **12 kt** |
| 41,000 | 560.9 | 0.776 | 0.78 | 0.004 | 216.2 | **1 kt** |
| 43,000 | 555.5 | 0.815 | 0.78 | — | 205.8 | **CLOSED (−9)** |

### 4b · SENSITIVITY — *why your AFM number changes the whole picture* **[I]**

| Assumed clean stall (IAS) | Mstall @ FL430 | Band @ FL430 | Band closes at |
|---|---|---|---|
| 130 KIAS | 0.493 | 0.287 | beyond service ceiling |
| 165 KIAS | 0.660 (FL450) | 0.120 (FL450) | ~FL510 |
| 200 KIAS | 0.758 | 0.022 | ~FL445 |
| **215 KIAS (chart)** | **0.815** | **CLOSED** | **~FL410–415** |
| 230 KIAS | 0.872 | CLOSED | ~FL385 |

> **This is the most important row on the card.** The closure altitude is a *direct function of the stall speed you assume* — and the chart's assumption is illustrative. **Use your AFM's stall speed at your actual ZFW.** The chart's *shape* (floor rises, ceiling steps down, band closes) is real; its *specific altitude* is not yours.

---

## 5 · ICING PENALTY — how the floor rises under your feet

**Stall speed penalty [P]:** +5 to +15%; **+20% or more in severe icing.** Apply to the **stall speed**, then recompute Mstall. Also expect: **reduced stall AoA (buffet warning may vanish), loss of aileron effectiveness, reduced elevator/pitch authority, and tail ice → tailplane stall risk.**

### Worked example at **FL370** (215 KIAS clean stall, limit M0.78 = 238.4 KIAS) **[I]**

| Condition | Stall (KIAS) | Mstall | **Band** | Aircraft at M0.76 (232.4 KIAS) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clean | 215.0 | 0.703 | 23.4 kt | 17.4 above stall / 6.0 below limit |
| **Light ice (+5%)** | 225.8 | 0.739 | 12.7 kt | 6.7 above stall / 6.0 below limit |
| **Moderate (+10%)** | 236.5 | 0.774 | **1.9 kt** | **4.1 kt BELOW the stall** |
| **Severe (+15%)** | 247.3 | 0.809 | **CLOSED** | 14.9 kt below the stall |
| Extreme (+20%) | 258.0 | 0.844 | **CLOSED (−19.6)** | 25.6 kt below the stall |

> ### At FL370 in moderate ice, the entire legal speed band was ≈ **2 KNOTS**.
> There was no correct Mach number to fly. **The only correct action was altitude (descend out of the ice) — never speed.**

---

## 6 · WORKED EXAMPLE — N722BD PROFILE (16 Feb 2025)

**Illustrative reconstruction from the video's reported ADS‑B and radio data [I].** Source: commentary video, not an NTSB product.

| Step | Altitude | Speed | What the numbers say |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cruise | FL350 | M0.76 = 243.9 KIAS | 28.9 above stall (215) · 16.1 below limit (M0.81 = 260.0) |
| **Climb approved** | FL370 | M0.76 → 232.4 KIAS | IAS falls 11.5 kt at constant Mach · floor rose to 236.5 w/ ice · **ceiling fell to 238.4** |
| **In ice at top of climb** | FL370 | 232.4 KIAS | **4.1 kt below the icing‑adjusted stall** · only 6.0 kt below overspeed |
| Loss of control | FL370→25,300 | GS 505 kt, 20,000 fpm | FPA ≈21° · **TAS ≈ 544 kt → Mach ≈ 0.90** (limit M0.80) |
| End of data | 8,750 | VS >32,000 fpm (saturated) | FPA ≈ 32–36° · **TAS ≈ 597–635 kt → Mach ≈ 0.93–0.99** |
| Impact | ground | — | Near‑vertical, ~10,000 ft/min (≈370 mph) descent |

> **The video never converts ground speed to Mach.** Doing so reveals the aircraft was **~16% beyond its certified M0.80** before impact, at 2–3 G — an **overspeed spiral dive**, not "Mach tuck." Whether the airframe broke up in the air is an open NTSB question.

### The five decision points (all BEFORE the loss of control)

```
1 MISDIAGNOSIS  "light ice that is manageable" · 600–700 ft swings called "moderate"
                (600+ ft of excursion = SEVERE turbulence)      → break here
2 CLIMB REQUEST "picking up precipitation → climb to FL370"
                raised the floor AND dropped the ceiling          → break here
3 NO DEVIATION  radar available · lower level available · deviation not requested
4 LOSS OF CONTROL  tailplane stall or ice‑contaminated wing stall (both triggered by AoA excursion, with the aircraft
                    already at/below the icing stall speed)
5 THE SPIRAL    speed built to M0.90–0.99 at 2–3 G               → consequence```

---

## 7 · DECISION RULES

| # | Rule |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Before accepting any level change, compute Mstall(ice) and MMO for the NEW level.** If the band is < 0.03 Mach or < 25 KIAS in icing → **decline the level or exit the weather.** |
| **2** | **Never climb into icing above FL290 without recomputing.** Climbing raises the floor, can drop the ceiling, and carries the ice into colder air with no escape level above. |
| **3** | **In icing, MMO is a wall — not a target. Speed is not a remedy. Altitude and shedding ice are the only remedies.** |
| **4** | **If the band is closed, descend.** Actively. Don't level off inside the icing layer. Don't descend below the altimeter setting unless terrain demands it. |
| **5** | **Recompute every 1,000 ft.** Mstall rises ~0.017 Mach/1,000 ft; at constant Mach, IAS falls ~5.7 kt/1,000 ft near FL350. |
| **6** | **Use radar as a crew resource.** Deviate around cells; do not request a climb into them. |
| **7** | **Check your turbulence‑penetration speed against your Mach limit.** At FL370 in ice, your maximum legal speed may be BELOW the recommended penetration speed. You may have no choice but to accept less. |
| **8** | **Declare early.** A stabilized descent with wings level beats a fast spiral. |
| **9** | **Watch the tells:** 600+ ft altitude excursions = severe, not moderate. "Manageable" = re‑evaluate now. Descending at >10,000 fpm with bank = you're in a spiral, see §8. |
| **10** | **The PIC owns the margin.** A clearance, a company's schedule, or a passenger's expectation never justifies an un-survivable envelope. |

---

## 8 · RECOVERY CARD

| Condition | Response |
|---|---|
| **WING STALL (icing)** | Full nose‑up (reduce AoA) · max thrust · wings level · **accept altitude loss — never pull to hold altitude** · expect abrupt, asymmetric break with **no prior buffet** · after recovery, level off and clean up slowly **below the overspeed limit** |
| **TAILPLANE STALL (high Mach + ice + AoA excursion)** | **NOSE DOWN — reduce AoA. Pulling makes it worse.** Expect abrupt nose‑down pitch and trim change |
| **SPIRAL DIVE** | **Roll toward level WHILE bringing the nose to the horizon. Do NOT pull to stop the descent. Manage speed with thrust.** Check for airframe overspeed before adding energy |
| **MACH TUCK** | Nose‑down pitching moment as Mach increases (elevator effectiveness falls). Trim against it. **Do not pull at high Mach** |
| **GENERAL UPSET (nose‑high / steep bank)** | Level wings → nose to horizon → thrust as needed. **Never pull to arrest a descent** |

### A few notes on what I did and didn't do

**Deliberately left blank or flagged:** the LR60's specific **clean/dirty stall speeds** and the **FL370 Mach step**. The stall speeds on the card are the *chart's* illustrative ~215 KIAS, back‑calculated from the video image — and §4b exists specifically to show you **how wrong that could be** for your actual aircraft. The M0.81/M0.78 step is the video's claim; I could not confirm it against an AFM, so it is tagged **[I]** with a warning, not presented as fact.

**Confirmed as published:** the atmosphere math, the icing stall‑speed penalty figures and their regulatory/guidance basis, the upset‑recovery references, and the CFR citations.

**The card is one page as written** — print at 8–9 pt, letter, one side. For a kneeboard, split it: **Front** = §1–5, **Back** = §6–9, then laminate. A second blank copy of §2 is worth carrying — the fill‑in block is the only part that must change per aircraft, weight, or day.

### ASSISTANT
# N722BD — Part 3: The Elevator

### *Separation analysis, flutter physics, the May turbulence encounter, and what it changes about the case*

---

## PART A — Sort the New Material Into Three Buckets

This video is more disciplined than the first one (no missile theory, explicit caveats, it names its uncertainty). But it still mixes **hard NTSB statements**, **the author's own inference**, and **physics**. Here's the separation:

### A.1 🟢 NEW HARD FACTS (attributed to the NTSB on‑site briefing)

| Fact | Source quote |
|---|---|
| The NTSB has **most of the left elevator** in **three pieces** | Tom Chapman, NTSB Board Member, last on‑site briefing |
| The closest piece is **~1.3 miles** from the crater; the farthest **~3.8 miles** | NTSB (via Chapman) |
| **Separation occurred before impact** | Follows from the distances + the shape of the dive |
| The NTSB has **not released the recovery locations** ("I don't know the location") | Chapman |
| The NTSB **cannot yet say** whether the separation caused or resulted from the loss of control | Chapman |
| The **CVR has not been recovered**; records show one was installed but it is unconfirmed whether it was aboard | NTSB |

**Two clarifications the video does make and the press did not:**
- **This is ONE control surface, not the tail.** Headlines saying "the tail came off" are wrong. The Learjet 60 has a **T‑tail**: fin, horizontal stabilizer on top, and **two elevators** hinged to the stabilizer's trailing edge.
- The NTSB's own framing of the two hypotheses: *"whether a piece like that was lost may have contributed to the crash itself or whether the forces on the aircraft caused that to separate."*

### A.2 🟡 NEW INFERENCE (the author's reconstruction — good work, but still inference)

| Inference | Method |
|---|---|
| The track crosses the 3.8‑mi circle **once**, at ~**27,000 ft, 38 s** into the dive; crosses the 1.3‑mi circle at ~**14,000 ft, 23 s** later | Overlaying two circles on the ADS‑B spiral |
| The pieces came off somewhere in **~FL14,000–27,000** | Miami sounding + drift model |
| **"This is a band of sky, not a point."** | The author's own words — and the right conclusion |
| Jeff Gazetti (ex‑NTSB investigator, to AP): the pieces are more likely **a result of the dive** than its cause; if they'd broken off at 35,000 ft they'd be "a lot farther away" | Opinion, but the *reasoning* is sound |
| The aircraft was at **M0.9, ~550–600 kt** through the last minute; the published limit is **M0.81** | ADS‑B kinematics |

### A.3 🔴 STILL UNKNOWN

Exact recovery coordinates and bearings · whether the release was at the top, middle, or bottom of the dive · **overload vs. fatigue** in the broken edges · bend direction (which way the load was pushing) · the **May 2024 special inspection** record · whether the right elevator showed flutter damage too · the CVR.

---

## PART B — Does This Match My Earlier Reconstruction? **Yes — Almost Exactly**

This is the important cross‑check: two independent analyses (mine from the first transcript, this one from the raw 1,028‑point ADS‑B reconstruction) land in the same place.

| Quantity | My earlier estimate (from video 1's numbers) | This video's estimate | Agreement |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dive duration | ~68–80 s | **77 s** | ✅ |
| Speed at end | TAS 597–635 kt | **~550–600 kt** | ✅ |
| Mach at end | **M0.93–0.99** | **"around Mach .9"** | ✅ |
| Speed mid‑dive | 544 kt, M0.90 @ 25,300 ft | M0.9 | ✅ |
| Right‑hand spiral throughout | 60° bank reported | "turning right the whole way down" | ✅ |
| Terminal q vs. cruise | ~4.7× | "about four times harder" | ✅ |

### One important disambiguation

In video 1's transcript the crew said *"We are descending 22,000 ft and we're banking 60°."* That reads as **22,000 ft/min** (a descent *rate*), not 22,000 ft of altitude remaining. Video 2's transcript confirms the intent: *"descending 25 [thousand] feet per minute."* **22,000 ft/min is a rate.** (If it were altitude, the aircraft would have had 22,000 ft left and the numbers wouldn't close.) Getting this right matters — it's the difference between "descending fast" and "descending from 22,000 feet," and only the first is consistent with the data.

### One new technical note on the data itself

**1,028 reports in 77 seconds ≈ 13 per second** — that's far above the ~1 Hz position cadence. The reconstruction is almost certainly parsing **raw 1090 MHz Extended Squitter frames** (multiple message types per cycle: position, velocity, identification, status), not just position fixes. That's the right approach: raw squitter gives you velocity and vertical rate at finer granularity, which matters when you're reconstructing a 77‑second event. **This is a good habit to copy** for any ADS‑B analysis.

---

## PART C — The Wind‑Drift Analysis: How It Works and Where It Breaks

This is the most interesting technical exercise in the video, and it's worth doing properly — because the *method* is sound and the *precision* is very limited.

### C.1 The method

**Time‑of‑flight (backward trajectory) reconstruction.** For each recovered piece, hypothesize a release point + time, then integrate the piece's fall through the wind field and see where it lands. Do it for every release point on the track. The set of release points consistent with the recovery locations is the answer. **This is exactly how the 1966 BAC 1‑11 case was solved** (below).

### C.2 My own sensitivity check on the drift numbers

The video's model: light winds (<25 kt at all levels from the Miami sounding), a piece released between **20,000–30,000 ft** lands **~1.5–5 miles north** of the crater; below 10,000 ft it lands inside the ~0.5‑mile debris field.

Let me test what that implies. For a tumbling panel, assume an average descent rate of ~100 ft/s (a flat, fluttering panel has a low terminal velocity — much lower than a rock):

| Release altitude | Fall time | Drift @15 kt | @25 kt | @45 kt |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 27,000 ft | ~4.5 min | 1.1 mi | 1.9 mi | 3.4 mi |
| 20,000 ft | ~3.3 min | 0.8 mi | 1.4 mi | 2.5 mi |
| 14,000 ft | ~2.3 min | 0.6 mi | 1.0 mi | 1.8 mi |
| 10,000 ft | ~1.7 min | 0.4 mi | 0.7 mi | 1.3 mi |

**To get the video's 1.5–5 miles from a 20,000–30,000 ft release, you need effective winds of roughly 20–65 kt.** The sounding said <25 kt. So one of these must be true:

- **Local winds near the convection were stronger than the sounding** — very likely. The sounding was launched **60+ miles away, hours earlier/later**, and near thunderstorms winds are far stronger and wildly turbulent.
- **The effective terminal descent rate was much lower** than 100 ft/s (a tumbling panel can descend at 40–60 ft/s), extending the drift time.
- **The piece carried the aircraft's velocity for a while** — see below. **The video underweights this.**

### C.3 The factor the video underweights: initial velocity carry

A piece separating at 550–600 kt TAS does **not** drop straight down. It retains a velocity vector of **~300+ kt directed along the flight path** (which was ~45–60° down and, in a right spiral, laterally offset to the right of the crater). A light panel decelerates over perhaps 20–40 seconds — during which it travels roughly **1–3 miles along the track**.

> **Consequence:** the 3.8‑mile piece could have come from **lower** than the "high 20,000s," because part of its distance came from being *thrown* forward/down/right at 500 kt rather than from wind. And the 1.3‑mile piece could have come from even lower, since the closer it separated to the crater the less time it had to travel at all.

### C.4 The conclusion I agree with, stated at the right strength

> **Adopt the video's own framing: "a band of sky, not a point."**
>
> The drift analysis is **directionally consistent** with separation occurring during the dive, well below FL30,000. It is **not** precise enough to place the release at a specific altitude, and it cannot distinguish, say, FL17,000 from FL23,000. The video is honest about this; a lot of secondary coverage was not.

### C.5 Why the missing information (bearings) is decisive

The video flags it: **we don't know which direction from the crater the pieces were found.** This matters enormously:

- If found **north** → consistent with the sounding's drift and with release in the mid/low 20,000s.
- If found **south** → either the local winds at those levels differed from the sounding, or the release altitude was different, or the piece **glided** laterally (a large flat surface can autorotate and travel sideways far beyond a ballistic prediction).

**This single missing fact is the difference between a narrow hypothesis and a wide one.** It is the first thing to look for when the NTSB releases the recovery map.

---

## PART D — The Flutter Physics, Done Properly

The video's claim: at the end of the dive the air was "pushing on every surface about four times harder than at cruise," the FAA requires a tail to be shown free of flutter to 15% beyond the designed dive speed, and beyond that line nothing has to be demonstrated.

**The number checks out. Here's the derivation, and two corrections of nuance.**

### D.1 Dynamic pressure along the dive — the numbers

q = ½ρV². I'll track TAS and ρ against the reported descent profile:

| Point | TAS | ρ (kg/m³) | **q (Pa)** | **q ÷ cruise q** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cruise FL350, M0.76 | 438 kt (225 m/s) | 0.380 | 9,640 | **1.00** |
| Top of dive, FL370, M0.76 | 434 kt (223 m/s) | 0.351 | 8,760 | **0.91** |
| ~FL25,000, M0.90 | 544 kt (280 m/s) | 0.549 | 21,500 | **2.23** |
| ~FL14,000, M0.90 | 580 kt (299 m/s) | 0.796 | 35,500 | **3.68** |
| ~FL8,750, M0.90 | 577 kt (297 m/s) | 0.942 | 41,700 | **4.33** |
| (sea level at same TAS) | 577 kt | 1.225 | 54,000 | 5.60 |

> **"About four times harder than at cruise" ✅ — the video's figure is correct.** At the end of the dive, dynamic pressure was ~4.3–4.7× the value at FL350 cruise.

### D.2 The single most important fact in this table

> **At the top of the dive, q was actually ~9% LOWER than at cruise.** The aircraft was slower in TAS at the same Mach in thinner air.
>
> **And it got worse monotonically from there.** The jet held roughly constant Mach while the air thickened, so q climbed ~5× during the descent — and the *flutter/structural margin collapsed continuously from the top of the dive to the ground.*

This matters for the cause‑or‑result question: **the q‑driven failure mechanism was not present at the moment the aircraft lost control.** It developed afterward. That is a strong argument that the elevator separation was a **consequence**.

### D.3 Correction #1 — distinguish q from n·q

The video's "four times harder" is the **dynamic pressure** ratio. The actual **load on the tail and elevator** is q **times** the load factor n:

- In a 60° bank, n ≥ 1/cos 60° = **2.0** before any pull; with a pull to control the flight path, **2–3 G** is plausible.
- So **total surface loading at the end of the dive ≈ 4.3 × 2.5 ≈ 10–11× the cruise surface loading.**
- For a **flutter** mode, the relevant quantity is the dynamic pressure at the surface plus the effective structural damping margin — and the damping margin is what the certification testing buys you. **Beyond the demonstrated envelope, that margin is unknown.**

### D.4 Correction #2 — the certification basis is Part 21/23, not Part 25

The video's framing ("the FAA's rules require a tail to be shown free of flutter up to 15% beyond the airplane's designed dive speed") reflects **transport‑airplane (Part 25)** certification practice. The Learjet 60 is a **business jet**, certified under Part 23 (legacy) or Part 21 (newer LR60s, 1990s). The applicable demonstrated flutter speeds and the applicable operating speed limits are therefore **different**.

**Why this is not a nitpick — it's the actual lesson:**

> **The LR60's MMO exists because MMO is the highest speed at which the airframe was demonstrated flutter‑free (plus whatever margin the certification and AFM impose).** The "15%" cushion belongs to a *different* certification basis. **Do not assume you have any cushion above the AFM number on a business jet. Do not assume a brief excursion "can't hurt."**
>
> And this is also the best explanation for why the MMO is where it is: **the limit is set by where the testing stopped, not by where the metal starts to sing.**

### D.5 Chapman's quote, and why it is the most important sentence in the video

> *"At those speeds, I don't know that it would have been effective even if it had still been attached to the aircraft."*

**This is aerodynamically correct and it is the key to the whole case.** Three separate effects stack:

1. **Mach tuck / elevator effectiveness loss at high Mach.** Shock waves on the horizontal tail reduce the elevator's hinge‑moment effectiveness. The aircraft becomes *harder to pitch* exactly as it becomes most dangerous.
2. **Elevator effectiveness is also a function of dynamic pressure** — at low q the tail can't be overpowered; at high q it can. At M0.9 the q is high enough to make a separated elevator's failure irrelevant.
3. **With ice on the tail**, the horizontal stabilizer's stall AoA is reduced — the tailplane can stall at high Mach and high AoA, producing an abrupt, unrecoverable nose‑down pitch. (I flagged this as a candidate initiating mechanism in Part B of my previous analysis; it remains live.)

> **Implication:** once the aircraft was committed to a spiral at M0.9, the elevator's presence or absence may have mattered little to the outcome. **The elevator question may be real but not outcome‑determining** — which is why the NTSB's framing ("a very important piece of evidence in terms of pointing us towards other things to investigate") is carefully limited.

### D.6 The other half of the problem: an asymmetric tail

The aircraft impacted with the **right** elevator attached and the **left** gone. For the final segment it was flying with an asymmetric tail — a source of **rolling and yawing moment** the crew had to hold against in a spiral at M0.9. This is a forensic detail the video doesn't raise, and the NTSB will examine it: the right elevator's damage state at impact is evidence about *when* the left one went and what the crew was doing with the controls.

---

## PART E — "Cause or Result": Three Branches, and What Discriminates Them

The video compresses this to two options. There are three, and the third is the one that keeps the investigation open.

| Branch | Hypothesis | Consistency with the drift analysis | What would prove it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A** | Elevator separated **after** the dive was established → **consequence** of overspeed/loads | ✅ Best fit. Separation in the FL14,000–27,000 band puts it deep into the spiral | Recovery coordinates + bearings showing release below FL30,000; metallography showing a single catastrophic overload with no pre‑existing fatigue; **no** pre‑damage in the actuator/hinge |
| **B** | Elevator (or its **actuator, hinge, or fitting**) was **already damaged** before the dive — e.g., by the May severe‑turbulence encounter, or pre‑existing fatigue — and **failed during** the dive → **contributing cause** | ⚠️ Also fully compatible. A pre‑weakened joint fails at a *lower* load than a sound one, so a mid‑dive separation is exactly what you'd expect | Metallography showing **fatigue crack growth** (growth striations, beach marks, a pre‑existing crack with a final‑overload region); the May special‑inspection record showing it was not done, not done properly, or finding damage |
| **C** | Elevator **fluttered/broke at the top of the climb** (M0.76–0.78, with ice on the tail) and the **loss of pitch control caused the departure** | ❓ Struggles with the drift distances — a release at ~FL30,000+ should be much farther away. **But "a lot farther only goes so far"** because the spiral was tight | Recovery distances consistent with high release; evidence of flutter‑pattern damage (serrated/rippled fracture surfaces from high‑frequency oscillation); the right elevator showing comparable damage; evidence the elevator was loaded up at the top of the climb |

### The forensic discriminators — how investigators will actually tell

| Evidence | What it tells you |
|---|---|
| **Broken‑edge microscopy** | Single overload vs. fatigue. A piece that fails in one huge overload looks different under a microscope from one with a crack that grew over months |
| **Bend direction** | Which way the load was pushing when it let go — infers the maneuver state and whether it was aeroelastic (flutter) or pure structural |
| **Surface morphology** | Flutter produces a characteristic **rippled/serrated** fracture pattern from high‑frequency oscillation; overload produces a single progressive tear |
| **Actuator, hinge, and fittings** | Whether the failure originated in the structure, the drive, or the attachment — and whether there was pre‑existing damage |
| **Recovery coordinates + bearings** | Release altitude band and direction of the debris field |
| **The right elevator at impact** | Whether the failure was symmetric (suggesting a systemic condition like flutter) or isolated |
| **Maintenance records (May inspection)** | Branch B's smoking gun |
| **CVR** | Would have settled it on the day. **Its loss is the single biggest evidentiary gap.** |

### The historical precedent the video correctly invokes

**BAC 1‑11, 4 November 1966, squall line near Omaha, Nebraska.** The tail assembly failed **first**; the wing separated **1–2 seconds later**. Investigators established that *order* by **running the falling debris backward through the wind**, and **NASA dropped a scale model of the 1‑11** to reproduce the separation timing. The case is a landmark in debris‑trajectory forensics.

> **Method takeaway for you:** *when a crash breaks an aircraft apart, the distribution of the pieces is evidence about the order and timing of failures.* In this case, the NTSB can do what NASA did in 1966 — with 77 seconds of 13 Hz ADS‑B, radar, wreckage photography, and modern CFD.

---

## PART F — The May Encounter: The Thread That Matters Most for Your Flying

This is the most consequential new fact in the video, and it got less attention than it deserved.

**The facts as reported:** The same tail, **N722BD**, in **May** (2024), ran into turbulence near New York. The crew told the controller they were **unable to hold altitude**. The controller replied:

> *"If you can't maintain altitude, that's not moderate. That's severe turbulence. Are you experiencing severe turbulence?"*
> Crew: **"Affirmative."**

### F.1 Why this is a big deal, in four layers

**① It's the same mis‑diagnosis, twice.**
In May, the crew reported turbulence as "moderate" until a controller forced the correct classification. In February, the crew called **600–700 ft of altitude excursions "moderate turbulence"** on the radio. **Same tail, probably same crew, same error pattern.**

> **"Manageable" (the icing call) and "moderate" (the turbulence call) are the same mistake applied to two different hazards: understating the severity of the thing that is about to kill you.**

**② It's an aircraft‑performance red flag, not just a radio call.**
"Unable to hold altitude" in turbulence means the aircraft was at or near its **maximum sustainable flight path** — which in a jet means **high angle of attack, close to the stall.** A 4+ G gust can bend the wing and empennage, damage hinges, actuators, and control surfaces, and upset rigging/trim. **This is a potential initiating condition for a later flutter or structural failure.** Note that in May, the controller said *"that's not moderate"* — and in February, 600–700 ft excursions were called moderate. **The severity classification appears to have been consistently understated.**

**③ It's a maintenance trigger that may not have been actioned.**
Business‑jet maintenance manuals (MMG/AMM, Special Inspections, typically Section 12) call for a **special inspection after a severe‑turbulence encounter** — because of the possibility of hidden structural or flight‑control damage. Whether one was required here and whether it was done is in the aircraft's records. The video notes the NTSB said maintenance work is ongoing.

**④ It's an organizational/ORM finding, not just an individual one.**
If a controller had to catch the severity mis‑call in May, and the same crew's severity language was wrong again in February, the question isn't only "did this pilot err" — it's **"does this operator's culture let turbulence and icing severity get understated, and is there a mechanism to catch it?"** The May recording (made through a third‑party ATC app and posted publicly) suggests **this pattern was already visible externally before the accident.** If so, a safety‑management system that had that information had a chance to intervene and didn't.

### F.2 What you take from it (as a student pilot)

1. **Report the highest severity you actually experience.** If you can't hold altitude, it is severe turbulence. Say "severe."
2. **Accept the challenge.** A controller saying "that's not moderate" is a free second opinion from someone with radar. The May crew did accept it. The February crew reported "moderate" on a 600–700 ft excursion.
3. **Treat "unable to hold altitude" as both a flight‑crew event and a maintenance event.** Get the inspection done and documented.
4. **Watch for the pattern across your own operations:** if you find yourself rationalizing ("it's manageable," "it's just moderate"), that word is a signal to re‑evaluate, not to continue.

---

## PART G — What to Watch For: The NTSB Timeline & Document Checklist

### G.1 Expected sequence

| Milestone | Typical timing | What to look for |
|---|---|---|
| **Preliminary Report** | ~30 days after the accident (the video's "about a month" is right) | Confirmed parties, aircraft, crew, preliminary findings narrative |
| **Factual / Circumstantial Report** | Weeks–months after | The full ADS‑B reconstruction, radar, ATC transcript, weather, the recovery map |
| **Aircraft Performance Report** | With the factual report | Weight & balance, thrust, stall speeds, **and the flight‑path/airspeed analysis** — this is where M0.9 gets stated officially |
| **Structures Report** | Later | **Flutter/overload analysis of the elevator** — the answer to "cause or result" |
| **Wreckage/Explosion Report** | Later | Recovery locations, damage mapping |
| **Maintenance Report** | Later | **The May inspection**; MEL status; de‑ice system condition |
| **Final Report + Probable Cause + Safety Recommendations** | Typically targeted within ~12 months | The finding, and what changes industry‑wide |

### G.2 Where to get it

**NTSB CAROL** (their accident database) → search **N722BD**, or *Learjet 60*, *Palm Beach County*, *16 Feb 2025*. Also:
- **NTSB "On Scene" video** and the **NTSB Board meeting webcast/video** (the on‑site briefing Chapman spoke at is public) — the NTSB's YouTube channel and ntsb.gov
- The **docket** for the accident (party‑submitted documents) once released
- **The Board's public meeting minutes** for the on‑site briefing

### G.3 Three specific documents to hunt for by name

1. **The Factual Report's aircraft performance section** — does it state the Mach at the end of the dive? Does it endorse M0.9?
2. **The Structures Report's elevator analysis** — fatigue vs. overload, bend direction, and whether the May inspection is discussed.
3. **The recovery map** — the recovery coordinates and bearings, which is the single piece of information that would collapse the "band of sky" into a point.

| §3 new box | — | **q rule:** constant TAS + thickening air → q rises ∝ ρ. In this event q went **0.91× cruise at the top of the dive → 4.3–4.7× at the end.** Mach constant ≠ safety constant. |
| §7 new row | — | **SPIRAL DIVE:** add "manage q, not just Mach — constant Mach with thickening air still doubles the structural load." |
| §7 new row | — | **ELEVATOR FAILURE (overspeed):** loss of one elevator in a spiral = asymmetric rolling/yawing moment with no elevator authority. Level wings, manage q with thrust, expect no pitch authority. |
| §8 new row | — | **SEVERE TURBULENCE EVENT:** "unable to hold altitude" = severe turbulence = **both a flight event and a maintenance event.** Report the true severity; get the special inspection done and documented. |
| §2 fill‑in | — | Add: **"Last special inspection (severe turbulence / hard landing / overspeed): ____"** |
