---

# POST‑SUMMARY — The 10 Things That Matter Most

**1. The elevator separated BEFORE impact — that is confirmed.** Everything beyond that is inference. The video is admirably clear about this; most secondary coverage was not.

**2. The drift analysis says "a band of sky, not a point" — and that is the correct strength of claim.** The pieces are most consistent with separation somewhere in **~FL14,000–27,000**, which is *after* the dive began → leaning **result, not cause**. But the error bars are huge.

**3. The missing recovery BEARINGS are the single most valuable missing fact.** North supports the current picture; south substantially changes it. Look for the map in the Factual Report.

**4. The video underweights initial velocity carry.** A piece leaving at 550 kt retains a huge forward/downward vector for 20–40 seconds, traveling 1–3 miles before wind dominates. So the release altitude may be *lower* than the "high 20,000s."

**5. The "four times harder" number is correct — and I verified it.** q went from **0.91× cruise at the top of the dive to ~4.3–4.7× at the end**, because the jet held Mach while the air thickened. **q rose monotonically all the way to the ground.** Constant Mach in a descent is *not* constant safety.

**6. The decisive point for cause‑or‑result: q at the top of the dive was LOWER than at cruise.** The structural/flutter failure mechanism was not present when control was lost — it developed afterward. That argues strongly for **consequence**.

**7. But the certification framing needs a correction.** The "15% beyond V_D" margin belongs to **Part 25 transports**; the LR60 is **Part 21/23**. The right lesson is stronger, not weaker: **MMO is where the flutter testing stopped. Assume zero margin above it.**

**8. Chapman is right that the elevator may not have mattered.** At M0.9 the elevator's authority was likely minimal whether attached or not. That makes the elevator evidence real but potentially **not outcome‑determining** — which is exactly how the NTSB is framing it.

**9. Keep branch B alive: the May 2024 encounter.** "Unable to hold altitude" = severe turbulence = an aircraft exposed to high‑g gusts and possible hidden structural/flight‑control damage, requiring a special inspection that may not have been done. If metallography shows **fatigue**, that reclassifies the elevator from consequence to **contributing cause**. **This is the thread most likely to change the story.**

**10. The human‑factors pattern is the finding that applies directly to you.** Same tail, two events, the same error: **understating severity.** "Manageable" ice, "moderate" turbulence, and a controller in May having to say *"if you can't maintain altitude, that's not moderate — that's severe."* That is the lesson that outlives this accident: **when in doubt, report the higher severity, and treat the controller's challenge as a second opinion rather than an obstacle.** And remember that the initiating error in February was still the climb into icing at FL370 — the elevator question is real, but it did not cause that climb.

---

### Suggested next steps
1. **Pull the NTSB preliminary report** from CAROL as soon as it's posted, and add the elevator/structures findings to your file as they land.
2. **Rev 1.1 of the card** — say the word and I'll regenerate it clean, with the MMO conflict flagged rather than resolved, and the q‑ratio box added.
3. **The ADS‑B lab** (still on offer): hands‑on reconstruction of flight path angle, TAS, Mach, and q from raw squitter data for this event and one of your choosing. Given what this video got right — and the one factor it underweighted — it's the most useful analytical skill for tracking this case as the NTSB releases more.
4. **The calculator** (still on offer): enter weight, altitude, TAS/Mach, OAT, and ice condition → outputs q, load factor, stall Mach, icing‑adjusted stall Mach, MMO margin, and flutter‑margin commentary against the AFM limit.

*One last thing: the CVR gap is permanent for this accident. Business‑jet recorders are frequently removable, which is why they're often absent — and it's why the ADS‑B reconstruction has become the primary record. In the event that two NTSB actions are at stake in your own flying — a severe‑turbulence inspection and a recorder inventory — those are two conversations to have with your operator while things are quiet.*

### USER
Build a short instructor exercise from this case covering icing, stall recognition, overspeed, and flutter, with discussion questions and an answer key.

### ASSISTANT
# INSTRUCTOR EXERCISE — **“N722BD: Four Decisions”**

### Icing • Stall Recognition • Overspeed • Flutter

**Format:** Instructor-guided table-top / role-play, built on the Palm Beach County Learjet 60 accident (16 Feb 2025)
**Duration:** 50 minutes **•** **Group size:** 2–8 (ideally paired as PIC/FOP) **•** **Level:** Post‑instrument, high‑altitude/icing recurrent

---

# SECTION A — INSTRUCTOR GUIDE

## A.1 Exercise at a Glance

| Item | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Learning objectives** | See A.2 |
| **Materials** | Cockpit card (Rev 1.1) or equivalent band table • calculator • Student Handout (Section B) • the 3 numbers in B.2 |
| **Scenario** | High‑altitude Part 135 charter in icing; crew requests a climb; loss of control; spiral dive; elevator separation; 9 fatalities |
| **Core skill** | Computing and managing the **high‑altitude speed margin**, and knowing when the envelope is already closed |
| **Instructor note** | Students should be able to reach a *defensible* answer, not a single “right” one. Reward reasoning that states assumptions. |

## A.2 Learning Objectives

By the end of this exercise, the student should be able to:

1. **Compute** the usable speed band at a given altitude, weight, and icing condition, and state the margin policy that applies.
2. **Explain** how icing raises stall speed and suppresses the stall warning, and predict the band at a *new* level.
3. **Distinguish** wing‑stall and tailplane‑stall recognition and recovery, and explain why they are opposite.
4. **Relate** a Mach overspeed to dynamic pressure, load factor, and the loss of structural and control‑surface margin.
5. **State** why an AFM speed limit is a hard boundary and what lies above it.
6. **Identify** organizational/CRM contributors — severity reporting, turbulence inspection triggers, and the limits of crew authority.

## A.3 Framing and Ground Rules — **Read Before You Teach**

**1. Keep it blameless‑systems, not blameful.** Nine people died. The instructor’s job is to ask *what conditions and decisions produced the outcome*, not to grade a dead crew. If the discussion drifts toward “they should have known,” redirect to: **what would have had to be true for the margin to have been visible and usable?**

**2. Pre‑empt the conspiracy material.** The case attracted missile speculation. If a student raises it, use it as a teaching moment: *probable cause is an NTSB determination made on the record; the available flight‑deck evidence supports the weather/handling explanation; extraordinary claims require evidence, and there was none.* Do not let unverified speculation into the classroom.

**3. Correct the common shortcut on turbulence severity.** Many students (and plenty of instructors) will say “600–700 ft of altitude change = severe turbulence.” That is **not** how the criteria work. FAA turbulence reports use **multiple criteria** — altitude excursion, apparent vertical speed, and the occupants’/aircraft’s ability to maintain flight path:

| Reported characteristic | Indicative severity |
|---|---|
| Altitude changes 500–1,000 ft | Light |
| Altitude changes 1,000–2,000 ft | Moderate |
| Altitude changes >2,000 ft, or **aircraft unable to maintain altitude** | **Severe** |
| Crew forced to brace / cannot stand | **Severe** |

**The decisive indicator is the aircraft’s performance, not the ride.** *“Unable to hold altitude”* is severe turbulence by definition — it means the aircraft was at high AoA, near the stall, in the airmass. The May 2024 recording makes this concrete: the controller said, *“If you can’t maintain altitude, that’s not moderate. That’s severe turbulence.”* Use the **aircraft’s performance** as the primary severity discriminator, with excursion and vertical speed as supporting criteria.

**4. Watch the age/sex trap.** If the discussion turns to the 20‑year‑old first officer, redirect to the legitimate and non‑speculative factors: time in type, 90‑day currency, company training on the aircraft, and organizational factors. There is no NTSB finding on this, and there should be no classroom finding either.

**5. Say clearly what is established versus open.** See Section C preamble.

## A.4 Run Sheet

| Time | Segment | Instructor action |
|---|---|---|
| 0:00–0:05 | Frame | State objectives, ground rules, confirm calculators/cards |
| 0:05–0:15 | **Phase 1 — Icing** | Read Inject 1; pairs work Q1; debrief from key |
| 0:15–0:25 | **Phase 2 — Stall recognition** | Read Inject 2; Q2; debrief |
| 0:25–0:35 | **Phase 3 — Overspeed** | Read Inject 3; Q3; debrief |
| 0:35–0:42 | **Phase 4 — Flutter** | Read Inject 4; Q4; debrief |
| 0:42–0:50 | Integration + close | Q5, assessment items, assign homework (NTSB CAROL) |

## A.5 Inject Card — read aloud, verbatim, at the marked time

> **INJECT 1 (0:05)** — “It is 12:46 Eastern. You are at FL350, Mach 0.76, in the climb en route from Austin to Nassau. You have reported light clear ice, and your ride is rough — you estimate your altitude is swinging 600 to 700 feet. Outside air temperature is about minus 47. You pick up precipitation. Your controller comes back: ‘Roger, maintain flight level 370.’ **You asked for that level.** We are going to work your decision here.”

> **INJECT 2 (0:15)** — “Less than 30 seconds after the climb begins you call: *‘We have an overspeed condition.’* Then: *‘Mayday, Mayday, we’re descending 10,000 feet per minute.’* Your airplane is in a right-hand spiral. You are at Mach 0.9. Your limit is Mach 0.81. **Your left elevator is still attached. For now.** What is happening, and what do you do?”

> **INJECT 3 (0:25)** — “You are now at about 14,000 feet, descending faster than 20,000 feet per minute, banked 60 degrees right. Airspeed is about 580 knots indicated, and still building. **How much margin do you actually have left? What is the correct recovery?**”

> **INJECT 4 (0:35)** — “You are at 8,750 feet. The rate of descent indicator saturates at 32,000 feet per minute. You are at Mach 0.9. **Thirty seconds from the ground.** A panel has left the aircraft — you do not know that yet. What is physically happening to your airframe right now, and why did the tail give up before the wings did?”

---

# SECTION B — STUDENT HANDOUT

## B.1 Case Fact Sheet

- **Aircraft:** Learjet 60, N722BD, 2004, Part 135 charter, Austin → Nassau • **9 on board**
- **Date/Time:** 16 Feb 2025, ~12:49 EST • **Outcome:** loss of control, spiral dive, ground impact
- **Aircraft position:** FL350, **M0.76**, ~437 kt groundspeed
- **Crew reports:** light clear ice, “manageable”; turbulence, altitude swinging **600–700 ft**; OAT **−47 °C**; “picking up precipitation”
- **ATC:** crew requests 37,000; Miami Center approves, *“Final maintain flight level 370.”*
- **~30 s later:** “We have an overspeed condition”; Mayday; 10,000 fpm; right spiral; ~60° bank
- **End state:** ~M0.9; **published limit M0.81**; **left elevator separated before impact** (three pieces, 1.3–3.8 mi away)
- **Prior event, same tail, May 2024:** severe turbulence near New York; **crew unable to hold altitude**; controller challenged the “moderate” call; crew confirmed **severe**

## B.2 Reference Numbers (you may use these)

```
[ILLUSTRATIVE — from the case chart; the instructor's card has the full set]
Clean stall (chart assumption) .............. 215 KIAS
Limits .................................... M0.81 below FL370 / M0.78 above FL370 (VERIFY IN AFM)
Icing stall-speed penalty .................. +5–15%; +20% or more in severe icing
Vmo/Mmo ................................... 340 KIAS / M0.81  (verify in AFM)
Normal cruise .............................. M0.76
OAT at cruise .............................. about −47 °C

FL350:  a = 576.5 kt   σ = 0.3100   √σ = 0.5567
FL370:  a = 571.4 kt   σ = 0.2862   √σ = 0.5350
FL390:  a = 566.1 kt   σ = 0.2647   √σ = 0.5145
FL410:  a = 560.9 kt   σ = 0.2441   √σ = 0.4941

Mstall = Vs(IAS) ÷ (a · √σ)          IAS = M · a · √σ
q = ½ ρ V²                            ρ = 1.225 · σ
```

## B.3 Phase 1 — ICING

**Q1.1** Compute your stall Mach, your position in the band, and the **total usable speed band** at FL350 in clean air. Do the same for FL370.

**Q1.2** Apply a **10 kt margin above the stall and 10 kt below the limit** to both levels. What is your actual usable window in KIAS at each?

**Q1.3** The crew reported **“light clear ice”** at **−47 °C**. What does that combination tell you about the ice, and about where the aircraft had been?

**Q1.4** Apply a **+10%** stall-speed penalty. Recompute the window at FL350 and FL370.

**Q1.5** You are the PIC. You have just requested FL370 and ATC has approved it. **Do you climb?** If not, what do you say, and what do you request instead? Be specific.

**Q1.6** What is the *fastest* useful thing you could have done at FL350 that would have helped everything that followed?

## B.4 Phase 2 — STALL RECOGNITION

**Q2.1** Give **three** possible initiating mechanisms for the loss of control. Which do you favour, and why?

**Q2.2** At M0.76 at FL370, was the aircraft above or below the stall? Show the arithmetic.

**Q2.3** Why might there have been **no buffet warning** before the stall?

**Q2.4** Write the recovery for (a) an ice‑contaminated **wing** stall and (b) a **tailplane** stall. Why are they opposite?

**Q2.5** In the spiral at M0.9 with ~2–3 G of load, what was the crew’s real pitch‑control authority?

## B.5 Phase 3 — OVERSPEED

**Q3.1** M0.9 against a limit of M0.81 is what percentage overspeed **in Mach**? What is the corresponding overspeed in **dynamic pressure** at the same altitude?

**Q3.2** Compute q at cruise (FL350, M0.76), at the **top of the dive** (FL370, M0.76), and at the **end** (8,750 ft, M0.9). Express each as a multiple of the cruise value.

**Q3.3** The aircraft held roughly **constant Mach** all the way down. Why was that not safe? State the rule.

**Q3.4** Write the correct recovery from the spiral. Why is the instinctive “pull back” wrong? What is the priority order?

**Q3.5** Name three things that made the **structural** margin worse during the descent that have nothing to do with the pilot.

## B.6 Phase 4 — FLUTTER

**Q4.1** In two sentences: what is flutter, and why is a control surface the usual first thing to fail?

**Q4.2** Why does the aircraft’s **MMO exist**? What does it tell you about the margin available above it?

**Q4.3** The NTSB Board Member said: *“At those speeds, I don’t know that it would have been effective even if it had still been attached to the aircraft.”* What does that mean aerodynamically, and why does it matter to the cause‑or‑result question?

**Q4.4** The **May 2024** event: what should have happened, and what is the CRM lesson? Why is this a system finding, not a crew finding?

## B.7 Integration

**Q5.1** List every decision point in this flight where the outcome could have changed. Identify the **last recoverable point**.

**Q5.2** The case is not closed. Write **one sentence** stating what would change your mind about the elevator being a *result* rather than a *cause*.

**Q5.3** For your own operation: write **one** check you will add to your cockpit or SOP because of this case, and **one** you will add to your company’s procedures.

---

# SECTION C — INSTRUCTOR ANSWER KEY

## C.0 What is established, and what is not

| Status | Item |
|---|---|
| **Established** | 9 fatalities; aircraft position and ADS‑B track; radio calls; the crew requested the climb and ATC approved it; the left elevator separated **before** impact; the aircraft reached ~M0.9 against a published M0.81 |
| **Open** | Exact recovery coordinates and bearings; fatigue vs. overload in the broken edges; whether the May 2024 special inspection was performed; whether the CVR was aboard; probable cause (NTSB) |
| **Do not assert** | That the elevator caused the loss of control; that the crew “should have known”; anything about external interference |

---

## C.1 Phase 1 — ICING

**Q1.1** FL350: Mstall = 215 ÷ (576.5 × 0.5567) = **0.670**. Aircraft M0.76 = 0.76 × 576.5 × 0.5567 = **243.9 KIAS**. Limit M0.81 = **260.0 KIAS**. **Band = 45.0 kt.**
FL370: Mstall = 215 ÷ (571.4 × 0.5350) = **0.703**. M0.76 = **232.4 KIAS**. Limit M0.78 = **238.4 KIAS**. **Band = 23.4 kt.**
*Teaching point: the climb halved the band — the floor rose (M0.670→0.703) and the ceiling fell (M0.81→M0.78), simultaneously.*

**Q1.2** **FL350: window 225.0 → 250.0 KIAS = 25.0 kt (workable).**
**FL370: window 225.0 → 228.4 KIAS = 3.4 kt (unworkable).**
*This is the most important number in the exercise. Even in perfectly clean air at FL370, a 10/10 margin policy left a 3.4-knot window. **The level was not survivable before the ice ever entered the discussion.***

**Q1.3** Clear/glaze ice is **liquid‑water** ice, associated with temperatures roughly 0 to −15 °C. At **−47 °C** there is no supercooled liquid water producing clear ice. So either (a) the ice was **acquired lower down in a convective cloud and carried up** in the climb, or (b) it was **ice‑crystal/rime** icing at very low temperature, or (c) the temperature report was garbled on the radio.
*Teaching point: both readings point to the same operational conclusion — **the aircraft had been in a convective cloud**, and the ice was being carried into a region where the margin was closing and the escape options were shrinking. If students can only extract one thing from this phase, it should be: **"the ice tells you where you have been, and the climb took you deeper into it."***

**Q1.4** +10% → stall = **236.5 KIAS**.
**FL350: window 246.5 → 250.0 KIAS = 3.5 kt.** **FL370: band = 1.9 KIAS, window = none (closed).**
*The headline: **moderate icing alone closed the FL370 band, and left FL350 barely usable under a 10/10 policy.**

**Q1.5** Any defensible answer is acceptable if it contains three elements:
1. **Decline the climb and request a lower level** — get out of the cloud and the icing, below the 0 °C level; and/or
2. **Request a lateral deviation** around the cell (radar was available; the crew did not ask), and/or
3. **Explicit reasoning:** the band at FL370 in ice is 1.9 kt; no speed is simultaneously ≥10 kt above the icing stall and ≥10 kt below M0.78; therefore **the climb makes the flight unrecoverable**.
*Accept “I’d still climb but…” only if the student names the specific condition that makes it survivable. Reject “the controller told me to” — **the crew requested the level.** Also note the regulatory nuance: **14 CFR 91.137** asks the PIC to request a climb above the icing layer **where practicable** — procedurally compliant, operationally catastrophic.*

**Q1.6** Expected answers: request a lower level / descend out of the icing; use radar and deviate; declare an emergency early; activate anti‑ice and wing heat (and note the thrust penalty); compute and brief the band before accepting any level change; establish an “escape altitude.”
*Best answer, if offered:* **“Before I ask for a level, I know my icing‑adjusted stall Mach and my Mach limit at that level, and I know the 0 °C and −20 °C heights on route.”**

---

## C.2 Phase 2 — STALL RECOGNITION

**Q2.1** Ranked: (1) **tailplane stall / Mach tuck with tail ice** at high Mach and high AoA; (2) **ice‑contaminated wing stall** with asymmetric roll‑off and no warning; (3) **unrecovered turbulence upset** (roll excursion then nose‑low).
*Expected answer:* the aircraft was already at/below its icing‑adjusted stall speed at FL370, and turbulence supplies the AoA excursion. **No single mechanism is proven; all three were live.** Note also the NTSB’s framing — the crew called out each step on the radio and none of it mentioned a mechanical problem.

**Q2.2** Stall at FL370 with +10% ice = **236.5 KIAS**. Aircraft at M0.76 = **232.4 KIAS**. **The aircraft was ~4 kt BELOW its icing‑adjusted stall speed at the top of the climb.**
*Teaching point: **the aircraft was, simultaneously, below the stall and near the overspeed limit.** There was no speed available to fly. That is the entire accident in one line.*

**Q2.3** Icing **reduces the stall angle of attack**. Buffet onset is tied to approaching that AoA; if the stall AoA arrives sooner, the aircraft can go from normal to stall **without passing through the buffet range.** Aileron effectiveness degrades first, so **roll may be lost before the break.** *Also: crew workload and instrument scan in turbulence can mask the cue.*
*Teaching point: in icing, **the absence of buffet is not evidence of margin.** Never use “no buffet” as an indication of speed in ice.*

**Q2.4**
- **Wing stall:** full nose‑up (reduce AoA), maximum thrust, wings level, **accept altitude loss — never pull to hold altitude.** Expect abrupt, asymmetric break, little/no warning.
- **Tailplane stall:** **NOSE DOWN — reduce AoA. Pulling makes it worse.** Expect sudden nose‑down pitch and a trim change.
*Teaching point: **they are opposite because the wing stalls by losing lift while the tail stalls by generating a nose‑down moment. Diagnose which one you have before you move the controls.** Redundancy: ask the crew what the control force and trim were doing.

**Q2.5** Essentially **none.** Three stack up: (1) **Mach‑dependent loss of elevator effectiveness** (shock waves on the tail); (2) **q rose ~5× during the dive**, so the tail had more authority to hurt you than to help you; (3) **ice‑contaminated tailplane** already reduced elevator authority; (4) the aircraft was in a bank, so the lift vector was not helping hold the nose up. *Corroborate with the NTSB Board Member’s own words: “I don’t know that it would have been effective even if it had still been attached.”*

---

## C.3 Phase 3 — OVERSPEED

**Q3.1** Mach: 0.90/0.81 = **+11.1%**. Dynamic pressure at the same altitude: (0.90/0.81)² = **+23.4%**.
*Teaching point: **overspeed margin is not linear in Mach. A 10% Mach overspeed is a 21% q overspeed.** And in IAS terms at low altitude it is far worse — at 8,750 ft, M0.9 ≈ **506 KIAS against a 340 KIAS VMO — about 49% over.**

**Q3.2** q at cruise FL350, M0.76 = **9,640 Pa**. At the top of the dive, FL370, M0.76 = **8,760 Pa = 0.91× cruise**. At 8,750 ft, M0.9 = **~41,500–45,000 Pa = 4.3–4.7× cruise**.
*This is the crux of the whole case: **q at the top of the dive was LOWER than at cruise.** The structural/flutter failure mechanism was not present when control was lost; it developed afterward. **That argues strongly that the elevator separation was a consequence.**

**Q3.3** Because **q = ½ρV²**, and the aircraft descended into thicker air while holding roughly constant Mach, **ρ rose ~2.5× and q with it.** *Rule to state:* **Constant Mach in a descent is not constant safety. Dynamic pressure rises as the air thickens, and q is what breaks things.**
*Best student formulation:* “Track q, not Mach. Mach tells you about compressibility effects; q tells you about loads.”

**Q3.4** **Roll toward wings level while bringing the nose toward the horizon. Do NOT pull to stop the descent. Manage speed with thrust.**
Why pulling is wrong: in a bank, the lift vector is tilted; pulling increases AoA and load factor, which **tightens the spiral** and adds g‑load to an already overstressed airframe.
Priority order: **(1) wings toward level → (2) nose to horizon → (3) manage speed with thrust.** *Note the NTSB/AC 120‑111 lineage: “level the wings first, then do not pull to stop the descent.”*
*Add: if the airframe is already overspeed, the only available remedies are **unloading the wing and bleeding energy** — not adding it.*

**Q3.5** Any three of: (1) **q rose ~5×** as the air thickened; (2) **load factor 2–3 G** in a 60° bank; (3) **elevator/empennage** was the most lightly loaded and most vibration‑prone structure; (4) **ice‑contaminated surfaces** raise drag and disturb airflow over the tail; (5) **asymmetric tail** after the elevator separated (rolling/yawing moment with no pitch authority); (6) structural damage already present from the May 2024 severe‑turbulence encounter *(unproven — the open branch)*.

---

## C.4 Phase 4 — FLUTTER

**Q4.1** Flutter is a **self‑excited, self‑sustained oscillation** of a structure — in this case, the elevator or the stabilizer/elevator combination — where aerodynamic forces feed the oscillation and it grows until the metal fails. *It is the classic “vibration that shakes a control surface apart.”* A control surface is the usual first failure because it is a **slender, low‑mass, highly loaded, cantilevered** structure riding in disturbed airflow, and its **hinge** is the weak link.

**Q4.2** Because **MMO is the highest demonstrated flutter‑free speed** — it is where the testing stopped, plus whatever margin the certification and AFM impose. The “15% beyond V_D” cushion the video cites belongs to **Part 25 transport** practice; a Learjet 60 is certified under **Part 23 (legacy) or Part 21 (1990s)**, with a different basis.
*Teaching point — state it bluntly: **the AFM speed limit is where the testing stopped. Assume zero margin above it. There is no certified “brief excursion” allowance.** Business jets have no transport‑airline cushion and no dispatch‑era wiggle room.*

**Q4.3** At M0.9 the elevator’s **effectiveness was degraded by compressibility effects on the tail (Mach tuck)**, and q was ~4–5× cruise, so the tail and elevator were operating far outside anything demonstrated. Chapman’s point is that **even an attached elevator would probably not have given meaningful pitch control at those speeds.**
*Why it matters:* if the elevator was ineffective whether attached or not, then whether it separated early or late may be **less outcome‑determining** than it appears — which is exactly how the NTSB frames it (“a very important piece of evidence… pointing us towards other things to investigate”). *But it does **not** close the question:* if the elevator separated **at the top of the climb**, its loss could have **caused** the departure.

**Q4.4** Two things should have happened: (1) **the crew should have reported the severity accurately** — severe turbulence, not moderate — and the controller had to catch it; and (2) **the operator should have triggered the special inspection** required by the maintenance manual after a severe‑turbulence encounter, because of possible hidden structural/flight‑control damage.
*Why it is a **system** finding, not a crew finding:* the same tail, with the same severity mis‑call, produced the same problem twice, and **the first instance was captured on a publicly posted ATC recording.** That means the information existed outside the company before the accident. *The questions are therefore: did the operator know? Did the reporting culture allow “moderate” to be stated when the aircraft’s performance said otherwise? Was there a mechanism to convert an ATC recording into a maintenance event?*

---

## C.5 Integration

**Q5.1** Expected sequence:
1. **Reporting severity** — “manageable” ice, “moderate” turbulence.
2. **The climb request** — the decisive error; narrowed the band from both sides, carried the aircraft into the cell, removed the escape level.
3. **No deviation / no lower level requested.**
4. **Loss of control** — tailplane stall, ice‑contaminated wing stall, or unrecovered turbulence upset.
5. **The spiral dive** — overspeed, structural overstress, elevator separation.

**Last recoverable point: the climb request** (approximately 12:46:30 Eastern). Every subsequent event was downstream of it. *Also note the aircraft was recoverable from the loss of control in the sense that a different flight could have continued — but not recoverable from that spiral.*

**Q5.2** Accept anything along the lines of: “If metallography shows **fatigue crack growth** in the elevator or its actuator/hinge, the May 2024 turbulence encounter becomes a contributing cause rather than an anecdote.” Also accept: recovery coordinates/bearings showing a **high release point**; or evidence of **flutter‑pattern fracture surfaces**; or the **right elevator** showing comparable damage.
*Follow‑up to the class:* **This is why the CVR matters so much, and why “we’ll get the pieces” is not a substitute for the record.**

**Q5.3** Answers vary. Good ones tend to be concrete:
- *Cockpit:* compute the band at every level before accepting it; brief the icing‑adjusted stall speed; use a company minimum margin policy; use radar to deviate; treat “no buffet” as meaningless in ice.
- *Company:* a **turbulence/icing severity‑reporting standard** with a no‑penalty culture; a **turbulence encounter trigger** for special inspection and crew debrief; **CVR/recorder inventory and download procedures**; training that explicitly covers the **FL350–FL390 icing overlap** and the **M0.78/M0.81 stepped limit**.

---

## C.6 Debrief Script — five points, in this order

1. **The band closed before the ice did.** At FL370 in clean air, a 10/10 margin policy left 3.4 knots. The level was the problem first.
2. **Icing closed what the climb had already narrowed.** Moderate icing alone left a 1.9‑knot band at FL370 and put the aircraft ~4 kt below its icing stall at M0.76.
3. **One speed could not be the answer.** MMO is a wall; speed was never a remedy. Altitude and shedding ice were the only remedies.
4. **The elevator is very likely a result, not a cause — but the investigation is open.** q was *lower* than cruise at the top of the dive and rose ~5× afterward.
5. **The transferable lesson is the severity call.** “Manageable” and “moderate” were the same error, twice, on the same aircraft. **Report the higher severity; treat the controller’s challenge as a second opinion.**

**Closing line to land:** *“The margin was visible the whole time, and it was computable in under a minute on a piece of paper. The accident was decided by an altitude, not by a metal part.”*

---

## C.7 Assessment Items (5 minutes, written) and Key

**A1.** State the stall Mach and the total usable speed band at FL370 for a 215 KIAS clean stall, and repeat with a 10% icing penalty. *(0.703 / 23.4 kt; 0.774 / 1.9 kt.)*
**A2.** State the recovery for an ice‑contaminated wing stall and for a tailplane stall, and explain why they differ. *(Nose up / nose down; the wing stalls by losing lift, the tail by producing a nose‑down moment.)*
**A3.** A jet descends at constant Mach from FL350 to 8,750 ft. Its Mach does not change. Does its structural margin improve, worsen, or stay the same? Explain. *(Worsens — q ∝ ρ; the air thickens; q rose ~5×.)*
**A4.** Why is the aircraft’s MMO a hard limit, and what is above it? *(It is the highest demonstrated flutter‑free speed; above it there is no certified margin, and the failure mode is an uncontrolled, high‑energy oscillation of the tail or elevator.)*
**A5.** Give one example each of a **crew** and an **organizational** action that would have reduced this accident’s likelihood, and say which stage each one interrupts. *(Crew: decline the climb / request lower or deviate — interrupts the climb decision. Organization: accurate severity reporting plus a turbulence‑encounter inspection trigger — interrupts the “manageable/moderate” culture.)*

**Scoring:** 1 point each, with half credit for a correct answer that misstates the reason. **Below 3/5 = the student has not yet internalised the band concept and should be re‑run Phase 1 before further high‑altitude/icing training.**

---

## C.8 Instructor Cautions

- **Do not** present the band numbers as certified LR60 figures. The 215 KIAS stall and the M0.78/M0.81 step are **illustrative from a commentary video**. Say so every time.
- **Do not** let the class conclude “the elevator broke off, so flutter caused it.” That is not established, and it teaches the wrong causal model.
- **Do** let students reach “I would have done X” — provided they state the **assumptions** that make X survivable. **The skill being taught is reasoning under uncertainty, not memorising one answer.**
- **Do** close the loop on the open questions each time the class meets until the NTSB final report is published. This case is still evolving, and the instructor’s credibility depends on updating rather than defending.
- **Do** require students to pull the **NTSB preliminary report** from CAROL (N722BD / Palm Beach County / 16 Feb 2025) before their next session.

---

### Instructor package contents

| Section | Use |
|---|---|
| **A** | Instructor guide — objectives, framing, run sheet, injects |
| **B** | **Student handout** — print 2 pages, no answer key |
| **C** | Instructor key — answers, teaching points, common errors, debrief, assessment |

**One-page student version available on request** (fact sheet + the four phases + integration questions only), and I can also supply a **revised cockpit card (Rev 1.1)** with the q‑ratio box, the MMO conflict flagged, and the severe‑turbulence/inspection trigger added, so the exercise and the card stay in sync.

### USER
Here is the video data of the flight, tell me what you think of it. 0.81 Mach to 0.71 is the safe range of speed window at 37,000 feet.

### ASSISTANT
# Your Window Is Right in Shape and About 3% Too Wide — Here's the Correction

---

## 1. Direct Answer to Your Claim

> *"0.81 Mach to 0.71 is the safe range of speed window at 37,000 feet."*

**Your geometry is good.** You've correctly identified the two boundaries, and you're right that M0.76 sat inside them. Three things need sharpening — and the first one is a real trap, because it's the exact trap the accident walked into.

| # | Issue | Correction |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **The ceiling at 37,000 is not 0.81** | The **Coffin Corner chart you gave me in our first session** steps the limit down: **"Limit M0.78 above 37,000."** Airliner Archive's Fig. 9 says **M0.81** because it's talking about the **low altitudes in the dive** ("at *those altitudes* is Mach point eight one"). Both are right — **M0.81 is the general MMO; M0.78 is the high-altitude restriction.** At FL370 you're at the step, so the ceiling is **M0.78**, not 0.81. |
| **2** | **0.71 is the stall boundary, not a safety floor** | M0.71 at FL370 = **217 KIAS**. The stall (chart's assumption) = 215 KIAS. **You'd be 2 knots above the stall** — i.e., *in the buffet*, and *below the stall* the moment there's any ice at all. |
| **3** | **"Safe" ≠ "not yet unrecoverable"** | The raw band has no margin in it. A real operating window needs ≥10 kt above the stall and ≥10 kt below the limit. **Apply that and the window becomes 3.4 knots wide.** And with light ice it becomes 2.65 knots. With moderate ice, **it doesn't exist.** |

**And the words "safe range of speed window" hide the most important fact in the whole case:** the lower bound is **not a number you look up once**. It moves with altitude, weight, configuration, **and icing state**. The upper bound is a (stepped) constant. So it isn't a window — it's a **wedge that closes with altitude and closes further with ice.**

---

## 2. The Corrected FL370 Window

Using the chart's illustrative 215 KIAS stall, ISA, and the M0.78 restriction above FL370:

| Altitude | Stall Mach | Stall (KIAS) | Ceiling Mach | Ceiling (KIAS) | **Raw band** | **Usable window w/ 10 kt margin each side** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| FL350 | 0.670 | 215.0 | 0.81 | 260.0 | 0.140 (45 kt) | **225.0 – 250.0 KIAS (25 kt)** |
| **FL370** | **0.703** | **215.0** | **0.78** | **238.4** | **0.077 (23.4 kt)** | **225.0 – 228.4 KIAS (3.4 kt)** ← |
| FL390 | 0.738 | 215.0 | 0.78 | 227.2 | 0.042 (12.2 kt) | **NONE** |
| FL410 | 0.776 | 215.0 | 0.78 | 216.2 | 0.004 (1.2 kt) | **NONE** |

**Your window, corrected:**

```
Your version:   M0.71 ————————————— M0.81     (0.10 Mach, 30.6 KIAS at FL370)
Correct raw:    M0.703 ————————— M0.78       (0.077 Mach, 23.4 KIAS)
Usable (10/10): M0.736 ———— M0.780           (3.4 KIAS  ←  this is what you actually have)
```

### Why the 10-kt margin changes everything

At FL370, the floor is set by **stall + 10 kt = 225 KIAS (M0.736)** and the ceiling by **M0.78 − 10 kt = 228.4 KIAS (M0.780)**. The ceiling is now *below* the floor — no wait, they nearly touch: **3.4 knots.**

> **Take the 10 kt off each side and a 0.077 Mach band becomes a 3.4-knot band. That's the difference between "we have room" and "we have a decision."**

---

## 3. Why 0.71 Was Never a Floor You Could Fly

```
FL370, ISA, √σ = 0.5350

M0.71 → TAS 405.7 kt → IAS 217.0 KIAS
Stall (chart) ............................. 215.0 KIAS
Margin to stall ........................... +2.0 KIAS   ← in the buffet

M0.76 (where the crew was) → TAS 434.3 → IAS 232.4 KIAS
Margin to stall ........................... +17.4 KIAS
Margin to M0.78 ceiling ................... −6.0 KIAS
```

The crew was **17 kt above the stall and 6 kt below the overspeed limit.** In a **clean** airframe that is a flyable position. In an **iced** airframe it is not.

---

## 4. Add the Ice — and the Window Disappears

Stall-speed penalty: **+5 to +15%**, **+20% or more** in severe icing (FAA AC 91‑74B; FAA/TC *In‑Flight Icing Training Aid*).

**FL370, ceiling M0.78 (238.4 KIAS):**

| Condition | Stall (KIAS) | Stall Mach | **Usable window (10/10)** | Crew at M0.76 (232.4) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clean | 215.0 | 0.703 | **225.0 – 238.4** (13.4 kt) | 17.4 above stall / 6.0 below limit |
| **Light ice +5%** | 225.8 | 0.739 | **235.8 – 238.4** (**2.65 kt**) | 6.6 above stall / 6.0 below limit |
| **Moderate +10%** | 236.5 | 0.774 | **NONE — WINDOW CLOSED** | **4.1 KIAS BELOW THE STALL** |
| Severe +15% | 247.3 | 0.809 | NONE | 14.9 below the stall |

> ### The number to carry away:
> **At 37,000 ft in light icing, the only speed you could legally and safely fly was between M0.771 and M0.780.**
> ### And in moderate icing, **there was no speed at all.**
>
> The aircraft at M0.76 was **simultaneously below the stall and 6 knots from the overspeed limit.** That is the accident in one sentence.

**So your "0.71–0.81" is the *clean-air, zero-margin* band.** It was never a safe window at FL370 — it was the outer walls of a room you couldn't safely stand in.

---

## 5. Review of the New Frames — Six Things Worth Noticing

These are good frames. Several carry their own findings.

### 5.1 ⚠️ Fig. 10 has an internal inconsistency — and the caption oversells it

> **Caption:** *"Same speed, thicker air"* • **Sub-caption:** *"Our estimate: constant true speed, standard atmosphere +7 °C"*

The **cruise bar (212 psf)** is computed at **cruise speed (M0.76)**. The **descent bars (385 / 570 / 820 psf)** are computed at **constant dive TAS of ~550–575 kt.** Let me verify:

| Bar | Their value | My calc at constant TAS 550 kt | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cruise 35,000 | 212 psf | 203 psf at **M0.76** ✓ | **computed at cruise speed** |
| 30,000 | ~385 psf | 383 psf at TAS 550 ✓ | constant dive TAS |
| 20,000 | ~570 psf | 546 psf at TAS 550 ✓ | constant dive TAS |
| 10,000 | ~820 psf | 756 psf at TAS 550 / 828 at TAS 575 ✓ | constant dive TAS |

**The bars don't share a speed assumption.** So the comparison "the air was pushing about four times harder than at cruise" is really comparing **two different flight conditions**, not isolating the atmosphere. The honest decomposition:

| Contribution | Factor (cruise → 10,000 ft) |
|---|---|
| **Denser air alone** (FL350 → FL10,000 at cruise TAS) | **×2.4** |
| **Higher speed alone** (M0.76 cruise → ~550 kt dive) | **×1.6** |
| **Combined** | **×3.7** ✓ |

> **"Same speed, thicker air" is true of the descent bars among themselves. It is not true of the cruise bar compared to them.** The air didn't just get thicker — the aircraft was also ~30% faster in TAS. That's a better story anyway: **the structural loading rose from both ends at once.**
>
> *This is a good catch to make, and a good lesson: when a chart mixes reference conditions, check what each bar assumed before quoting its headline.*

### 5.2 ⚠️ Fig. 11's regulatory citation is worth verifying — and the framework is the wrong one for a Learjet

The frame cites **14 CFR 25.629 · AC 25.629‑1B** for the flutter-proof requirement. Two problems:

**(a) The citation.** For transport airplanes, the flutter requirement lives in **14 CFR 25.673(d)** (*Stability and control*, the flutter-free demonstration) with the associated **AC 25.673‑1** guidance; the design dive speed itself is in **25.671(d)** (V_D = 1.5 V_C for high-speed designs). **25.629 appears to concern flightcrew alerting.** Verify before you quote either number.

**(b) The certification basis.** This is the more important point, and it changes the conclusion:

> **The Learjet 60 is not a Part 25 transport.** It's Part 23 (legacy) / Part 21 (1990s). The "must be shown free of flutter to 1.15 × V_D" framework you're seeing is Part 25 practice. **The LR60's own AFM limit is what was demonstrated for *this* airframe, and that limit exists precisely so the demonstrated flutter envelope is never approached in service.**

And the sharper question the video doesn't ask: **where is V_D relative to M0.81 for this type?** If M_D sits above MMO (as it does for most jets), then **M0.9 may still have been *below* the demonstrated flutter speed** — while being **11% above the operating limit.** That reframes it:

> **The aircraft was not necessarily beyond the demonstrated flutter envelope. It was beyond the *operating* limit, which is the line that exists to keep you away from that envelope.** The correct pilot statement is not "flutter probably happened." It's: **"above the AFM limit there is no demonstrated margin, and the tail is the most likely first thing to give — which is exactly the piece the NTSB found."**

### 5.3 ⚠️ Fig. 5's sounding is dated **"Nov. 1, 7 a.m."**

The event was **16 February 2025**. A Miami sounding labelled **November 1** cannot be "the balloon launched from Miami that day." Either it's a placeholder, a different date, a mislabel, or the model's wind input isn't what the transcript describes.

**This matters** because the sounding is the **sole input to the drift model** that produces the "3.8 mi ≈ FL28,500" conclusion. The wind profile is doing all the work. Verify the date and the actual sounding used before citing the numbers.

*(Also note the arrows: the winds are northeasterly-ish at altitude. The transcript's claim of "1.5–5 miles **north**" is consistent with that. But the video itself says the **bearing** of the recovery sites hasn't been released — so the model's north is an assumption, not a finding.)*

### 5.4 The drift model is now quantified — and the band is wide

The reconstruction frame gives the curve explicitly:

| Release altitude | Predicted distance from crater |
|---|---|
| 32,000 ft | 4.8 mi |
| **28,500 ft** | **3.8 mi** ← the farthest piece |
| 24,000 ft | 2.5 mi |
| 20,000 ft | 1.7 mi |
| **17,700 ft** | **1.3 mi** ← the closest piece |

**Useful sanity check:** the curve is roughly linear at ~**0.19 mi per 1,000 ft of release altitude**, i.e., **~5,200 ft of release altitude per mile of distance.** So:

- 3.8 mi ⇒ ~28,500 ft release
- 1.3 mi ⇒ ~17,700 ft release
- **The two pieces bracket a release window of roughly FL17,000–FL29,000.**

**Which is the "band of sky" the video describes** — and it sits **entirely inside the dive**, which is why the separation looks like a *consequence* rather than a cause. But note the **single biggest sensitivity**: this curve assumes the panel carried little of the aircraft's velocity. At 550 kt TAS, a light panel that doesn't decelerate quickly travels **1–3 miles along the track** before the wind dominates. **If that carry is significant, the true release altitudes were LOWER than this curve says** — and the "3.8 mi piece" could have left the aircraft in the mid-20,000s rather than the high 20,000s.

### 5.5 ⚠️ The last 30 seconds of ADS‑B contain an anomaly worth raising with the NTSB

Reading the three reconstruction frames together:

| Time | Pressure alt | GPS alt | GS | VS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 12:48:58 | 24,716 | 26,272 | **484 kt** | −26,368 |
| 12:49:16 | 13,534 | 14,806 | **362 kt** | −32,640 **(saturated)** |
| 12:49:26 | 8,750 **(NO NEW VALUE)** | 6,506 | **251 kt** | −32,640 **(saturated)** |

**Three problems, in increasing order of interest:**

1. **Ground speed *falls* 484 → 362 → 251 kt during a descending spiral.** In an intact aircraft in a spiral dive, ground speed should **increase**. A 122 kt drop in 18 seconds cannot be wind (winds were <25 kt). Either the aircraft was decelerating hard (high-AoA/high-drag — possible), **or the velocity fields are degrading** as the rate of descent saturates and the airframe works itself apart.

2. **The vertical-rate field is pegged at the ADS‑B maximum (−32,640 fpm) for the last 20+ seconds**, so the true descent rate is unknown. At 12:49:16, the reported GS and altitude are consistent with **anywhere from M0.76 to M0.97**, depending on the true vertical rate. **You cannot pin the final Mach without the true VS.** (This is why the "M0.9" figure should be quoted as a range, not a point.)

3. **The pressure-altitude field freezes at 8,750 ft with a "NO NEW VALUE" flag while GPS altitude continues changing at a different rate.** A coherent, intact airframe in a dive does not produce a stale pressure altitude alongside a live GPS altitude.

> **Point 3 is the interesting one, and it is the same question as 5.2's: did the airframe stay together?** A collapsing groundspeed, a saturated VS, and a frozen pressure altitude in the final 20 seconds are all *consistent with* an in-flight breakup — and equally consistent with data degradation from extreme rates of descent, antenna masking, or antenna loss.
>
> **This is a legitimate question to put to the NTSB's Aircraft Performance Report.** It's not established. But the wreckage question ("did it break up in the air?") has an independent *data* answer available in the ADS‑B record, and nobody has pulled that thread yet in the public discussion.

### 5.6 The altimeter error is visible in the data — and it's a nice Florida detail

Pressure altitude (29.92‑referenced, from the transponder encoding) versus GPS altitude:

- 12:48:58 → **1,556 ft** apart
- 12:49:16 → **1,272 ft** apart

**The aircraft was flying roughly 1,300–1,600 ft above its 29.92 pressure altitude.** That's the normal consequence of a **low-pressure subtropical environment** (the crew's altimeter would have been set to a low value around 28.x–29.x inHg). Two lessons:

- **Your transponder altitude and your indicated altitude are different numbers**, and RVSM/terrain math built on 29.92 can be off by ~1,500 ft here.
- **It reinforces the descent rule:** never descend below the altimeter setting without checking terrain — in this environment, the relationship between setting and true altitude was not the standard one.

### 5.7 Things the frames get right (worth crediting)

- The **212 psf / ~4× q** figure is correct in magnitude.
- The **M0.9 estimate at 550–600 kt** is consistent with my independent reconstruction.
- The **drift curve** is a legitimate, well-executed application of the **1966 BAC 1‑11 method** (backward trajectory reconstruction of falling debris) — and the video labels it as *their model*, not as an NTSB finding. That's good discipline.
- **"Nobody has said flutter happened here"** — spoken aloud over a dramatic storm shot. That is the single most responsible line in the whole series.

---

## 6. The Framework to Keep — Why "the Window" Is the Wrong Mental Model

Stop thinking of it as a window. Think of it as **three layers, only one of which is a line on a chart:**

| Layer | What it is | Moves with |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Hard boundaries** | Stall speed (below = you fly) · MMO (above = you fly) | **Altitude** (both) · **weight** (floor) · **configuration** (floor) · **icing** (floor, +5–20%) · **MEL / AFM steps** (ceiling) |
| **2. Certified/assumed margin** | Your company's 10 kt each side, or whatever it is | **Your SOP** |
| **3. Condition-dependent deterioration** | What the *airmass* does to you between the boundaries | **Turbulence, gusts, shear, weight, and the ice that accumulated ten minutes ago** |

**Layer 1 is not a line — it's a surface.** The right mental picture:

```
        M0.81  ───────────────────────────────────── ceiling (general MMO)
        M0.78  ────────────────────────              ceiling above FL370
        M0.76  ────────────  ← crew here, 6 kt under the ceiling
        M0.703 ──────  ← clean stall at FL370 (215 KIAS)
        M0.774 ───────────  ← stall at FL370 WITH 10% ICE
        M0.736 ──  ← clean stall + 10 kt margin
        M0.771 ──────  ← iced stall + 10 kt margin
                      ↑
        AND AT 37,000 ft, M0.736 and M0.771 ARE THE SAME AIRPLANE IN DIFFERENT WEATHER.
```

> ### The rule:
> **Never memorize a "safe speed range" for an altitude. Compute it for the altitude, the weight, the configuration, and the ice — every time. The number changes as the flight changes.**

---

# POST‑SUMMARY — What Matters Most

**1. Your answer is close, but three corrections matter.** The ceiling at FL370 is **M0.78**, not 0.81 (0.81 is the *general* MMO; the chart steps it down above 37,000). **0.71 is not a floor** — it's 2 knots above the stall. And **"safe" is the word that breaks it** — the raw band has no margin in it.

**2. The usable window at FL370 is 3.4 knots, not 30.** With a 10‑kt margin each side: **225.0–228.4 KIAS.** In light icing: **2.65 knots.** In moderate icing: **nothing.**

**3. The crew's M0.76 was simultaneously below the stall and near the overspeed limit.** With a 10% icing penalty they were **4.1 knots below the icing stall** while only 6 knots under M0.78. **There was no speed available to fly at 37,000 feet.**

**4. Fig. 10's "same speed, thicker air" caption is misleading for the cruise bar.** The cruise bar is at M0.76; the descent bars are at ~550 kt TAS. The honest decomposition: **×2.4 from denser air, ×1.6 from speed, ×3.7 combined.** The structural loading rose from both ends.

**5. The flutter slide's regulatory framework is the wrong one for a Learjet.** 25.629/1.15×V_D is Part 25 transport practice. The right pilot statement: **MMO is the line that keeps you away from the demonstrated flutter envelope; above it, no margin is demonstrated and the tail goes first.** (And verify the citation — the transport requirement is 25.673(d) with AC 25.673‑1.)

**6. The end‑of‑data behaviour is a real, unasked question.** Ground speed *falling* 484 → 362 → 251 kt in a descending spiral, a saturated vertical rate, and a frozen "NO NEW VALUE" pressure altitude are all consistent with **either** data degradation **or** an in-flight breakup. **This is answerable from the ADS‑B record and should go to the NTSB's Performance Report.**

**7. Watch the sounding date.** Fig. 5 is labelled **"Nov. 1"** for a **16 February** event — and that sounding is the sole input to the drift model. Verify it before you cite "3.8 mi ≈ FL28,500."

**8. The altimeter was ~1,500 ft off from 29.92.** Visible in the data, normal for a subtropical low-pressure environment, and a reminder that transponder altitude, indicated altitude, and GPS altitude are three different numbers.

**9. The single most transferable idea: the window is not a window.** It's a **surface** in (altitude × weight × configuration × icing). **Compute it, don't remember it.** And the rule that would have saved this flight is one line long: **before you accept a level, know your icing‑adjusted stall Mach and your Mach limit at that level.** Here they'd have been 0.774 and 0.78 — **0.006 Mach apart.**

**10. One correction to my own earlier framing, for fairness to the source:** I said "600–700 ft of altitude swings = severe turbulence." That's too simple — by the FAA's *altitude‑excursion* criterion alone, 600–700 ft sits at the light/moderate boundary. **The decisive indicator is the aircraft's performance:** *"unable to hold altitude"* is severe turbulence by definition. That's exactly what the controller told the crew in May 2024. **Judge turbulence by what the airplane was doing, not by how it felt.**

# ▸ DELIVERABLE 1 — CLEAN CALCULATION: THE CORRECTED SPEED LIMITS AT 37,000 ft

## 1.1 Inputs (all labeled)

| Input | Value | Tag |
|---|---|---|
| Altitude | **37,000 ft** (stepped limit boundary) | — |
| Sound speed *a* @ FL370 (ISA) | **571.4 kt** | **[P]** US Std Atmos. 1976‑80 |
| Density ratio σ @ FL370 (ISA) | **0.2862** (√σ = 0.5350) | **[P]** |
| 1‑g clean stall speed | **215 KIAS** | **[I]** back‑calculated from the chart |
| AFM ceiling above FL370 | **M0.78** | **[I]** chart; **[P‑VERIFY]** in AFM |
| General MMO | **M0.81** | **[P‑VERIFY]** Airliner Archive / *Business & Commercial Aviation* |
| Stall margin policy | **10 kt above stall / 10 kt below limit** | **[P]** common operator practice; verify your SOP |

## 1.2 The four boundaries

```
Mstall = Vs(IAS) ÷ (a·√σ) = 215 ÷ (571.4 × 0.5350) = 215 ÷ 305.70 = 0.7033  →  M0.703
```

| Boundary | Mach | TAS (kt) | IAS (KIAS) | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1‑g clean stall** | **0.703** | 401.9 | **215.0** | hard floor, **zero** margin |
| Stall + 5 kt (buffet onset) | 0.720 | 411.2 | 220.0 | absolute practical floor |
| **Stall + 10 kt (policy)** | **0.736** | 420.5 | **225.0** | **operating floor** |
| Limit − 5 kt | 0.764 | 436.3 | 233.4 | |
| **Limit − 10 kt (policy)** | **0.747** | 426.8 | **228.4** | **operating ceiling** |
| **AFM ceiling (M0.78)** | **0.780** | 445.7 | **238.4** | **hard ceiling** |
| *[General MMO M0.81 — not applicable above FL370]* | *0.810* | *462.8* | *247.6* | |

### ➜ THE OPERATING WINDOW AT 37,000 ft

```
        M0.703      M0.720   M0.736 M0.747  M0.780
          │           │         │      │      │
          │ 1-g stall │ buffet  │      │      │
          │           │ onset   │      │      │
          ▼           ▼         ▼      ▼      ▼
          ├───────────┴─────────┴──────┴──────┤
          │      RAW BAND  0.077 Mach          │
          │        (23.4 kt, 215.0 → 238.4)    │
          │                                      │
          │        ┌─────────────────────┐      │
          │        │  OPERATING WINDOW   │      │
          │        │  M0.736 – M0.747    │      │
          │        │  225.0 → 228.4 KIAS │      │
          │        │  = 3.4 KNOTS        │      │
          │        └─────────────────────┘      │
          │                                      │
          ├──── YOUR PROPOSED WINDOW ───────────┤
          │  M0.71 ─────────────── M0.81        │
          │  217.0 KIAS        247.6 KIAS       │
          │  = 30.6 KIAS wide                   │
```

## 1.3 Why the proposed M0.71–M0.81 window was too wide — six independent reasons

| # | Error | Size of the error |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Wrong ceiling.** Used M0.81 at 37,000 ft. The chart steps the limit to **M0.78 above 37,000**. | **+9.2 KIAS of illegal margin at the top** |
| **2** | **No stall margin at the bottom.** M0.71 = 217.0 KIAS = **2.0 kt above the 1‑g stall** — below buffet onset, and below the stall in any ice. | **−8.0 KIAS of claimed margin** |
| **3** | **No overspeed margin at the top.** Used the limit *as* the ceiling. | −10 kt |
| **4** | **Ignored icing.** The actual condition. +10% moves the floor from M0.703 to **M0.774** — within 0.006 Mach of the ceiling. | **−34.8 KIAS of floor margin** |
| **5** | **Ignored altitude dependence.** The floor rises ~0.017 Mach per 1,000 ft. A window memorized at one altitude is wrong at the next. | unbounded |
| **6** | **Ignored weight and configuration.** Vs scales ≈ √W; a heavier jet has a higher floor. | unbounded |

### ➜ THE OVERSTATEMENT, QUANTIFIED

| Condition | Raw band (no margins) | Operating window (10 kt each side) |
|---|---|---|
| Clean | 0.077 Mach (23.4 kt) | **0.011 Mach (3.4 kt)** |
| Light ice +5% | 0.041 Mach (12.7 kt) | **0.009 Mach (2.7 kt)** — but only if you accept *zero* top margin; **NONE** with 10 kt each side |
| Moderate ice +10% | **0.006 Mach (1.9 kt)** | **NONE — WINDOW CLOSED** |
| Severe ice +15% | **NEGATIVE** | **NONE** |

> **Your proposed window overstated the usable range by about 9× in clean air, and infinitely in moderate icing.**
> **And the crew's actual M0.76 (232.4 KIAS) was 4.1 KIAS *below* the icing‑adjusted stall at +10%.**

## 1.4 V_D — and why you should never compute it

| Concept | What it is | Status for the LR60 |
|---|---|---|
| **V_C** | Design cruise speed. The reference for V_D. | **Not published** |
| **V_D** | **Design dive speed.** A *certification* parameter used to set the structural and flutter envelope. Part 25 analog: 14 CFR 25.671(d) ties it to V_C; the flutter demonstration must extend at least to V_D (25.673(d)). | **Not published** — the video's own Fig. 11 footnote says *"Learjet 60 values not public"* |
| **1.15 × V_D** | The Part 25 line beyond which **nothing has to be demonstrated** (AC 25.629‑1B is cited on the slide; the transport flutter requirement is more commonly cited to **25.673(d)** with **AC 25.673‑1** — *verify*). | **Not applicable in form** — the LR60 is Part 23 (legacy) / Part 21 (1990s), not Part 25. |
| **MMO (M0.78/0.81)** | **The operating limit. This is the only speed in the public record that encodes the flutter/structural envelope.** | **In the AFM. Use it.** |

### The number line that matters

```
 M0.78 ── M0.81 ────── [V_D ≈ M0.85–0.90?] ────── 1.15×V_D
   │        │                   │                        │
   │        │                   └─ certified flutter      │
   │        │                      envelope — NEVER       │
   │        │                      approached in service  │
   │        │                                              │
   │        └─ YOU ARE HERE. This is the line that exists  │
   │           to keep you away from the certified envelope│
   │                                                          │
   └─ AFM operating limit above FL370                       │
                                                              
   M0.85–0.92 ← THE AIRCRAFT WAS HERE (see Lab) — i.e. AT OR
                    BEYOND an illustrative V_D, with no public
                    way to know how far past.
```

> ### The operational conclusion:
> **V_D is not a speed you fly near. MMO is a speed set far below V_D so the flutter envelope is never approached. The gap between MMO and V_D is margin you are unknowingly spending the moment you exceed MMO.**
>
> **You never need to compute V_D. You need to never exceed MMO — because if you had, the margin that was supposed to protect the tail was already gone.**
>
> *Note: the V_D ≈ M0.85–0.90 placement is **illustrative for jets of this class**, not a published LR60 figure. The point is that the aircraft was **11–14% above MMO**, and **whether it was above the demonstrated flutter speed is unknowable from public data — and that uncertainty is the danger, not a reassurance.*

## 2.2 STUDENT WORKSHEET

### Method (fill the formulas)

```
a  (kt)   = 20.0467 × √(288.15 − 0.0065 × h_ft × 0.3048) × 1.68781
σ         = ½ρ at altitude;  ρ = 1.225 × σ
TAS (m/s) = √( GS(m/s)² + VS(m/s)² )
FPA       = asin( VS / TAS )
Mach      = TAS(m/s) / a(m/s)
q (Pa)    = ½ ρ TAS²          q (psf) = Pa / 47.8803
```

## 2.3 INSTRUCTOR KEY

### Answers

**Task 1 — Atmosphere (ISA):**

| | B: 26,272 ft | C: 14,806 ft | D: 6,506 ft |
|---|---|---|---|
| T (K) | 236.10 | 258.82 | 275.26 |
| a (kt) | 599.1 | 626.8 | 647.0 |
| σ | 0.4283 | 0.6333 | 0.8231 |
| ρ (kg/m³) | 0.5247 | 0.7760 | 1.0083 |

*Note: the video used ISA **+7 °C**, which raises *a* ~1.5% (Mach ~1.5% lower) and shifts ρ by 1–2%. **Not material.** Have students check their +7 variant if they use it.*

**Task 2 — Point B:**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| GS | 484 kt = 249.25 m/s |
| VS | −26,368 fpm = −133.97 m/s |
| **TAS** | **282.98 m/s = 550.4 kt** |
| **FPA** | **28.3°** |
| **Mach** | **0.919 → M0.92** |
| **q** | **21,010 Pa = 439 psf** |
| q ÷ cruise | **2.18×** |

**Task 3 — Point C (saturated VS):**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| GS | 362 kt = 186.36 m/s |
| TAS (from B) | 282.98 m/s |
| **True VS** | **212.94 m/s = 698.5 ft/s = 41,910 fpm** |
| **FPA** | **48.8°** |
| **Mach** | **0.878 → M0.88** |
| **q** | **31,069 Pa = 649 psf** |

**Cross‑check:** (14,806 − 6,506) ft ÷ 10 s = **49,800 fpm** average for C→D — *higher* than the 41,910 at C, which is what you'd expect in a tightening spiral. **Agreement confirms the method.**

> **Teaching point:** the ADS‑B vertical‑rate field saturated at 32,640 fpm, but the true rate was **41,900 fpm at 18 seconds from impact and ~49,800 fpm by the last frame.** Anyone reading the published field alone understates the descent rate by **29–53%.**

**Task 4 — Point D:**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| GS | 251 kt = 129.28 m/s |
| VS | 49,800 fpm = 253.03 m/s |
| **TAS** | **284.15 m/s = 552.7 kt** |
| **FPA** | **62.9°** |
| **Mach** | **0.854 → M0.85** |
| **q** | **40,703 Pa = 850 psf** |
| **q ÷ cruise** | **4.23×** |

**Task 5 — The headline:**

1. **Mach went DOWN**, from M0.92 to M0.85 (−0.07), because sound speed *increases* as the aircraft descends.
2. **q went UP by a factor of 1.94** (439 → 850 psf), and **4.23× cruise**.
3. **Rule:** *Mach measures compressibility effects; q measures structural load. In a descent, a can rise faster than Mach falls, so q climbs while Mach falls. **Track q, not Mach.***
4. **True rate ~42,000–50,000 fpm = 476–566 mph.** The published 32,640 fpm / "370 mph" **understated it by 29–53%.**

> ### The single most valuable sentence in the lab:
> **The aircraft's Mach number fell by 0.07 while its dynamic pressure rose 94%.** The Mach indication never showed the danger. **The number that killed it (q) went up while the number everyone watches (Mach) went down.**

**Task 6 — The end‑of‑data anomaly:**

| Candidate | Assessment |
|---|---|
| 1. Data degradation / stale fields | **Supported** for pressure altitude: it froze at 8,750 ft with a **"NO NEW VALUE"** flag while GPS altitude kept changing — the pressure field stopped updating while the GPS field did not. |
| 2. Airframe breakup / loss of a coherent reference | **Possible, unproven.** A tumbling fragment carrying the transponder antenna would produce exactly this: coherent‑ish GPS, garbage baro, and a collapsing GS. **The wreckage question may be answerable from the ADS‑B record.** |
| 3. A normal spiral dive with GS falling as FPA steepens | **Best‑supported for the GS fall itself.** With TAS held at ~550 kt, GS = √(TAS² − VS²) falls exactly as the FPA steepens — 28.3° → 48.8° → 62.9°. **The velocity vector, the GPS altitude change, and the constant‑TAS assumption all agree.** |

> ### ⚠️ Instructor's note — correct yourself out loud:
> In an earlier framing I called the falling ground speed "not aerodynamic" and suggested a breakup. **That was wrong.** The data is fully consistent with a spiral tightening at roughly constant TAS. **The genuine anomalies are (a) the saturated vertical rate, which understates the descent by ~30–50%, and (b) the frozen pressure‑altitude field at 12:49:26.** If a student challenges the GS point, say so plainly — modelling the correction in front of the class is worth more than defending the original claim.

**Task 7 — Impact estimate:**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| Time to ground | 6,506 ÷ 49,800 × 60 ≈ **7.8 s** |
| Impact TAS | **≈ 550–570 kt** |
| Impact Mach (sea level, a = 661.5 kt) | **≈ 0.83–0.86** |
| Impact IAS | **≈ 550–570 KIAS** vs VMO 340 → **~63% over VMO** |
| Impact q | **≈ 1,030 psf ≈ 5.1× cruise** |

*Teaching point:* **at sea level the Mach overspeed shrinks (0.85 → 0.83) while the IAS overspeed is enormous.** The overspeed that matters is expressed in whichever units your airframe limit is written in.

**Task 8 — Fig. 10 critique:**

1. **Cruise bar (212 psf) = cruise speed (M0.76). All three descent bars = constant dive TAS (~550–575 kt).** The bars do **not** share a speed assumption.
2. Using Point D's actual TAS (552.7 kt) at 6,506 ft: **q = 850 psf** — matches Fig. 10's ~820 psf at 10,000 ft closely, because the aircraft had already passed through that q level. **The chart is right; the caption is misleading.**
3. Decomposition:
   - Density: ρ_D ÷ ρ_cruise = 1.0083 ÷ 0.3797 = **×2.65**
   - Speed: (552.7 ÷ 438.1)² = (1.262)² = **×1.59**
   - Product: **×4.23** ✓ (850 ÷ 201)
4. **"The structural loading rose 4.2×: about 2.7× from the thickening air and 1.6× from the higher true airspeed. The chart's descent bars show the density effect alone; its cruise bar uses cruise speed, so the headline comparison mixes two conditions."**

**Task 9 — Fig. 11 critique:**

1. **V_D = design dive speed** — the certification parameter that sets the structural and flutter envelope. **Not published for the LR60** (the slide's own footnote says so).
2. **25.673(d)** with **AC 25.673‑1** are the more commonly cited transport‑airplane flutter references; **25.671(d)** defines V_D. *Have students verify the citation before repeating it.*
3. **No, not in form.** The LR60 is **Part 23 (legacy) / Part 21 (1990s)**; Part 25's 1.5·V_C and 1.15×V_D framework is transport practice.
4. **Because you never fly near V_D.** MMO is set below it precisely so the flutter envelope is never approached in service. **The pilot's job is MMO, not V_D — and the gap between them is the margin you spend the instant you exceed MMO.**

### Debrief close (2 minutes)

> *"This flight is the cleanest possible demonstration of three rules. **One:** the Mach number is not the safety number — it fell while the load nearly doubled. **Two:** published data can be systematically wrong in one field — the vertical rate was pegged, and the truth was 50% worse. **Three:** the decision that mattered happened ninety seconds before the dive, on the ground and on the radio, when someone asked for 37,000 feet."*

[P]=PUBLISHED  [P-V]=PUBLISHED, VERIFY IN AFM  [I]=ILLUSTRATIVE  [F]=FILL IN

### §1 · LIMITS & SOURCES

| Item | Value | Tag |
|---|---|---|
| **MMO — general** | **M0.81** (Airliner Archive, *Bus. & Comm. Aviation*) | **[P‑V]** |
| **MMO — above FL370** | **M0.78** (stepped) | **[P‑V]** |
| *Also cited elsewhere* | *M0.80* | **[P‑V]** |
| VMO | 340 KIAS | **[P‑V]** |
| Normal cruise | M0.76 | **[I]** |
| Stall speed (clean, chart) | 215 KIAS | **[I]** |
| Stall speed increase from ice | +5–15%; **+20%+ severe** | **[P]** AC 91‑74B; FAA/TC Icing Aid |
| High‑altitude guidance | FAA **AC 61‑107B** | **[P]** |
| Upset recovery | FAA **AC 120‑111**; NTSB *Airplane Upset Management* | **[P]** |
| Icing PIC duties | **14 CFR 91.137** (equip; provisions; request climb above layer where practicable) | **[P]** |
| PIC responsibility | **14 CFR 135.313** | **[P]** |
| Currency | **61.57** / **61.31** | **[P]** |
| ISA a, σ, ρ | US Std Atmos 1976‑80 (ICAO) | **[P]** |
| V_D (design dive speed) | **NOT PUBLISHED for LR60.** Certification parameter; sets the flutter/structural envelope. You never fly it. | **[P]** |

> ### ⚠ THREE MMO NUMBERS IN THE PUBLIC SOURCES (0.78 / 0.80 / 0.81)
> **The AFM governs.** The conflict is real and unresolved here. **Operational rule: if two candidate limits apply, fly the more restrictive one.** Cost of being wrong low: a few minutes of climb. Cost of being wrong high: structural margin you cannot see.

### §2 · FILL IN (before every flight)

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| ZFW / CG | ____ / ____ | **MMO at this level** | ____ |
| Config (clean / +flap) | ____ | **Stall speed at ZFW** | ____ KIAS |
| **Vs1 clean** | ____ KIAS | **Mach limit (stepped?)** | ____ |
| **Vs0 / Vso** | ____ KIAS | **Icing allowance** | ____ % |
| Vfe / Vlo | ____ | 0 °C level / −20 °C level | ____ / ____ |
| Company margin policy | ____ | **Escape altitude** | ____ |
| **Last special inspection (severe turbulence / hard landing / overspeed)** | __________ | | |

### §3 · THE BAND — ISA REFERENCE **[P]** + METHOD

```
 FL250 a=602.0 σ=.4482 √σ=.6695      FL330 a=583.0 σ=.3423 √σ=.5851
 FL300 a=589.4 σ=.3741 √σ=.6116      FL350 a=576.5 σ=.3100 √σ=.5567
 FL310 a=586.2 σ=.3902 √σ=.6247      FL370 a=571.4 σ=.2862 √σ=.5350
 FL320 a=584.6 σ=.3580 √σ=.5983      FL390 a=566.1 σ=.2647 √σ=.5145
                                        FL410 a=560.9 σ=.2441 √σ=.4941
 Mstall = Vs(IAS) ÷ (a·√σ)          IAS = M · a · √σ
 STALL floor RISES ~0.017 Mach / 1,000 ft.  Constant Mach in a CLIMB: IAS falls ~5.7 kt/1,000 ft.
```

### §4 · BAND TABLE — **[I]** ILLUSTRATIVE (215 KIAS clean stall; ceiling M0.78 above FL370 / M0.81 below)

| Alt | Mstall | Ceiling | Raw band | **Window w/ 10 kt each side** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FL350 | 0.670 | 0.81 | 45.0 kt | **225.0–250.0 KIAS** ✅ |
| **FL370** | **0.703** | **0.78** | **23.4 kt** | **225.0–228.4 KIAS — 3.4 kt** ⚠️ |
| FL390 | 0.738 | 0.78 | 12.2 kt | **NONE** ❌ |
| FL410 | 0.776 | 0.78 | 1.2 kt | **NONE** ❌ |

### §5 · ICING PENALTY AT FL370 **[I]**

| Condition | Stall KIAS | Mstall | **Window (10/10)** | Crew at M0.76 (232.4) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clean | 215.0 | 0.703 | 225.0–228.4 (3.4 kt) | 17.4 above stall / 6.0 below limit |
| **+5%** | 225.8 | 0.739 | **NONE** (2.7 kt only w/ zero top margin) | 6.6 above stall |
| **+10%** | 236.5 | 0.774 | **NONE — CLOSED** | **4.1 BELOW STALL** |
| **+15%** | 247.3 | 0.809 | NONE | 14.9 below stall |

> **Icing is the fastest way to close a band you thought you had.**

### §6 · OVERSPEED & q — **TRACK q, NOT MACH**

```
 q = ½ρV²        q_ratio = (ρ/ρ_cruise) × (V/V_cruise)²
 In a DESCENT: a RISES as you descend. If Mach is constant, TAS RISES.
 → Mach can FALL while q CLIMBS. (This flight: Mach −0.07, q +94%.)
 q_ratio in this accident: ×2.65 (denser air) × 1.59 (higher TAS) = ×4.23
 MACH overspeed ≠ q overspeed: M0.90 vs M0.81 = +11% Mach = +23% q (same alt).
 At LOW altitude the same overspeed is far worse in IAS (≈550 vs 340 KIAS at impact).
 ABOVE MMO: no demonstrated margin. MMO exists to keep you FAR from V_D (flutter).
```

### §7 · FLUTTER

```
 Flutter = self‑excited, self‑sustained oscillation; a control surface or hinge fails.
 WHY THE TAIL GOES FIRST: slender, low mass, high hinge loads, riding disturbed airflow.
 At high Mach the elevator's effectiveness FALLS (Mach tuck) — and ice on the
 tail cuts it further (tailplane stall). Pitch authority disappears exactly when
 you need it. (NTSB: "I don't know that it would have been effective even if attached.")
 CERTIFICATION: Part 25 transports cite 25.673(d)/AC 25.673‑1 and 1.15×V_D.
 A LEARJET 60 IS PART 23/21 — different basis. The AFM limit IS your protection.
 YOU NEVER FLY V_D. YOU FLY MMO.
```

### §8 · DECISION RULES

1. **Before any level change:** compute Mstall(ice) and the MMO **at the new level.** Band < 0.03 Mach or < 25 kt iced → **decline the level or exit the weather.**
2. **Never climb into icing above FL290** without recomputing. Climbing raises the floor, may drop the ceiling, and carries ice into colder air with no escape above.
3. **In icing, MMO is a wall — not a target. Speed is not a remedy. Altitude and ice shedding are the only remedies.**
4. **If the band is closed → descend**, decisively, through the layer. Never level off inside it. Never below the altimeter setting unless terrain demands.
5. **Recompute every 1,000 ft.** Floor +0.017 Mach/1,000 ft; IAS −5.7 kt/1,000 ft at constant Mach.
6. **Radar is a crew resource.** Deviate around cells; don't climb into them.
7. **Check your turbulence‑penetration speed against your Mach limit.** At FL370 iced, the legal maximum may be *below* the recommended penetration speed.
8. **Declare early.** A stabilized descent beats a fast spiral.
9. **The tells:** "manageable"/"moderate" understating severity; altitude excursions 600+ ft; any descent >10,000 fpm with bank = spiral, go to §9.
10. **The PIC owns the margin.** A clearance, a schedule, or a passenger never justifies an un‑survivable envelope.

### §9 · RECOVERY & THE TURBULENCE TRIGGER

| Condition | Response |
|---|---|
| **WING STALL (icing)** | Full nose‑up · max thrust · wings level · **accept altitude loss** — never pull to hold altitude. Expect abrupt, asymmetric break, **no prior buffet.** Clean up slowly **below** the limit. |
| **TAILPLANE STALL** (high Mach + ice + AoA) | **NOSE DOWN — reduce AoA. Pulling makes it worse.** Sudden nose‑down pitch, trim change. |
| **SPIRAL DIVE** | **Roll toward level WHILE bringing the nose to the horizon. Do NOT pull to stop the descent. Manage speed with thrust.** Watch q, not Mach. |
| **MACH TUCK** | Nose‑down moment as Mach rises; trim against it; **don't pull at high Mach.** |
| **GENERAL UPSET** | Level wings → nose to horizon → thrust. **Never pull to arrest a descent.** |
| **SEVERE TURBULENCE / UPSET ENCOUNTER** | **TWO ACTIONS.** ① **FLYING:** report the *true* severity — "unable to hold altitude" IS severe; get the operator's turbulence inspection done and documented; brief the crew. ② **AIRCRAFT:** the AMM/MMG special inspection after severe turbulence is a **hard stop** — no release until signed. **Triggers:** unable to hold altitude · sustained >2,000 ft excursions · crew forced to brace · any control‑surface/actuator/hinge anomaly · overspeed or buffet event · lightning strike. |

```
SOURCES: FAA AC 61‑107B · AC 91‑74B · AC 120‑111 · FAA/TC In‑Flight Icing Training Aid ·
NTSB Airplane Upset Management · US Std Atmos 1976‑80 · 14 CFR 91.137, 135.313, 61.57, 61.31
NTSB case: N722BD, Palm Beach Co. FL, 16 Feb 2025 — pull the preliminary report from CAROL.
STATUS: study aid only. NOT approved for operational use. Every [P‑V] must be confirmed against your AFM.
```

# ▸ DELIVERABLE 4 — ONE‑PAGE STUDENT HANDOUT

*Print one side, letter. Keep with your flight bag.*

```
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
  N722BD — THE 37,000-FOOT TRAP     |     ICE · STALL · OVERSPEED · FLUTTER
  High-altitude speed margin · N722BD, Palm Beach Co., FL, 16 Feb 2025
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

WHAT HAPPENED (facts)
  FL350, M0.76, light clear ice, "moderate" turbulence, OAT ≈ −47 °C.
  Crew REQUESTED FL370 ("picking up precipitation"). ATC approved.
  ~30 s later: overspeed call, Mayday, 10,000 fpm, right spiral, ~60° bank.
  Left elevator separated before impact. Mach ≈ 0.85–0.92 through the dive
  (published vertical rate SATURATED — true rate 42,000–50,000 fpm).
  9 fatalities.

THE NUMBERS AT 37,000 ft   (215 KIAS clean stall [illustrative], ISA)
  a = 571.4 kt   σ = 0.2862   √σ = 0.5350

  Mstall = 215 ÷ (571.4 × 0.5350) = M0.703

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1‑g stall .............. M0.703   215.0 KIAS   hard floor        │
  │ buffet onset ............ M0.720   220.0 KIAS                    │
  │ stall + 10 kt .......... M0.736   225.0 KIAS   operating floor   │
  │ limit − 10 kt .......... M0.747   228.4 KIAS   operating ceiling │
  │ AFM limit M0.78 ........ M0.780   238.4 KIAS   hard ceiling      │
  │                                                                   │
  │ ► OPERATING WINDOW: M0.736 – M0.747  =  3.4 KNOTS               │
  │ ► RAW band (no margins): M0.703 – M0.780 = 0.077 Mach = 23.4 kt  │
  │ ► A "0.71–0.81 window" is ~9× TOO WIDE                          │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CEILING CONFLICT: M0.78 above FL370 (chart) · M0.81 general MMO
  (Airliner Archive) · M0.80 cited elsewhere. → AFM GOVERNS.
  → WHEN IN DOUBT, USE THE MORE RESTRICTIVE LIMIT.

ICE — THE BAND CLOSER
  Stall speed +5–15%; +20% or more in severe icing.
  At FL370:  +5% → no 10/10 window  |  +10% → WINDOW CLOSED (1.9 kt)
  Crew at M0.76 (232.4 KIAS) with +10% → 4.1 KIAS BELOW THE STALL.
  Ice also: lowers stall AoA (no buffet warning), degrades ailerons,
  cuts elevator power, can tailplane‑stall at high Mach.

OVERSPEED — TRACK q, NOT MACH
  q = ½ρV²   q_ratio = (ρ/ρ_cruise) × (V/V_cruise)²
  THIS FLIGHT:  ×2.65 denser air  × 1.59 higher TAS  =  ×4.23 total
  MACH FELL 0.92 → 0.85 while q ROSE 94%. Mach was never the danger.
  M0.90 vs M0.81 = +11% Mach = +23% q. At impact ≈550 KIAS vs VMO 340.

FLUTTER — WHY THE TAIL GOES FIRST
  Self‑excited oscillation; slender control surfaces and hinges fail first.
  High Mach cuts elevator effectiveness (Mach tuck); tail ice cuts it further.
  MMO IS the line that keeps you away from V_D — the certified flutter
  envelope, which is NOT published for the LR60. Never compute V_D. Never fly it.
  YOU NEVER FLY V_D. YOU FLY MMO. Above MMO there is no demonstrated margin.

RECOVERY
  WING stall (ice) → nose up, max power, wings level, ACCEPT altitude loss.
  TAILPLANE stall → NOSE DOWN. Pulling makes it worse.
  SPIRAL dive → roll toward level WHILE nose to horizon; do NOT pull to
  stop the descent; manage speed with thrust; watch q.

RULES THAT WOULD HAVE CHANGED THIS FLIGHT
  1. Compute the band at the NEW level before accepting a level change.
  2. In icing, speed is not a remedy — altitude and ice shedding are.
  3. If the band is closed, descend. Don't level off in the cloud.
  4. Use radar — deviate rather than climb into a cell.
  5. Report the TRUE severity. "Unable to hold altitude" = SEVERE turbulence.
  6. A severe‑turbulence encounter is a MAINTENANCE event: get the AMM
     special inspection done and documented before the next flight.
  7. Severe turbulence, 600+ ft excursions, or "manageable" = re‑evaluate now.

KEY REFERENCE  a=20.0467√(288.15−0.0065·h_ft·0.3048)·1.68781 kt
               Mstall=Vs÷(a·√σ)   IAS=M·a·√σ   q=½ρV²   1 psf=47.88 Pa
  SOURCES  FAA AC 61‑107B · AC 91‑74B · AC 120‑111 · FAA/TC Icing Aid ·
           NTSB AUM · US Std Atmos 1976‑80 · 14 CFR 91.137/135.313/61.57/61.31
  NEXT STEP  Pull the NTSB preliminary report for N722BD from CAROL.
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
