<script>
"use strict";
var R=287.05287,GA=1.4,RHO0=1.225,KT=1.687809857,MPH=1.150779448,PSF=47.8803;
var T0=288.15,P0=101325,LC=0.0065,G=9.80665,NCHK=20,ERR=[];
function $(i){return document.getElementById(i);}
function T(i,t){var e=$(i);if(e)e.textContent=t;}
function H(i,h){var e=$(i);if(e)e.innerHTML=h;}
function V(i){var e=$(i);if(!e)return null;var v=parseFloat(e.value);return (v===null||isNaN(v))?null:v;}
function F(v,d){if(v===null||v===undefined||!isFinite(v))return '--';return Number(v).toFixed(d===undefined?2:d);}
function N(v){if(v===null||v===undefined||!isFinite(v))return '--';return Math.round(v).toLocaleString('en-US');}
function bail(m){ERR.push(m);var b=$('bootErr');if(!b){b=document.createElement('div');b.id='bootErr';if(document.body)document.body.appendChild(b);}
  b.style.cssText='background:#2d1113;border:1px solid #ff5252;color:#ffc9c9;padding:9px;border-radius:6px;margin:0 0 10px;font-size:12.5px';
  b.textContent='SCRIPT ERROR: '+m;var s=$('stamp');if(s){s.className='bad';s.textContent='SCRIPT ERROR: '+m;}}
/* table fill that survives a stripped <tbody> */
function fill(id,html){var t=$(id);if(!t)return;var b=t.getElementsByTagName('tbody')[0];if(!b){b=document.createElement('tbody');t.appendChild(b);}b.innerHTML=html;}
function ensure(){
  ['bt','qt','isa','dIn','dOut'].forEach(function(id){if(!$(id)){var t=document.createElement('table');t.id=id;t.appendChild(document.createElement('tbody'));if(document.body)document.body.appendChild(t);}});
  ['kvs','atm','work','adv','health','testOut','dAdv','verdict','diag','scale'].forEach(function(id){if(!$(id)){var e=document.createElement('div');e.id=id;if(document.body)document.body.appendChild(e);}});
  if(!$('track')){var d=document.createElement('div');d.id='track';d.className='track';
    d.innerHTML='<div class="zone zraw" id="zRaw"></div><div class="zone zsafe" id="zSafe"></div>'+
 '<div class="mk st" id="mStallMk"></div><div class="lab st" id="lStall"></div>'+
      '<div class="mk cl" id="mCeil"></div><div class="lab cl" id="lCeil"></div>'+
      '<div class="mk cu" id="mCur"></div><div class="lab cur" id="lCur"></div>';
    if(document.body)document.body.appendChild(d);}
}

function isaAt(hf){
  var hm=Math.max(0,Math.min(65000,hf||0))*0.3048,T,p;
  if(hm<=11000){T=T0-LC*hm;p=P0*Math.pow(1-LC*hm/T0,5.25588);}
  else{T=216.65;p=22632.1*Math.exp(-G*(hm-11000)/(R*T));}
  return {T:T,p:p,Tc:T-273.15,hm:hm};
}
function atmos(hf,oat){
  var I=isaAt(hf),hv=(oat!==null&&!isNaN(oat)),T=hv?oat+273.15:I.T;
  var rho=I.p/(R*T),sig=rho/RHO0,aMs=Math.sqrt(GA*R*T),aKt=aMs*KT,dev=hv?I.Tc-oat:0;
  return {I:I,T:T,Tc:I.Tc,p:I.p,rho:rho,sig:sig,ss:Math.sqrt(sig),aMs:aMs,aKt:aKt,aMph:aMs*MPH,
    dev:dev,aSig:aKt*Math.sqrt(sig),geom:hv?hf*(1-dev/T0):null};
}
var REF=(function(){var I=isaAt(35000);var rho=I.p/(R*I.T);var aMs=Math.sqrt(GA*R*I.T);var V=0.76*aMs;return {rho:rho,V:V,q:0.5*rho*V*V};})();

var RU={weight:{min:500,max:250000,lab:'Weight',u:'lb',d:27500},wRef:{min:500,max:250000,lab:'Reference weight',u:'lb',d:27500},
 vsRef:{min:30,max:450,lab:'Stall speed at reference weight',u:'KIAS',d:185},cfgInc:{min:0,max:150,lab:'Config increment',u:'KIAS',d:0},
 vmo:{min:80,max:700,lab:'VMO',u:'KIAS',d:340},mmo:{min:0.20,max:1.00,lab:'MMO',u:'Mach',d:0.81},
 stepAlt:{min:0,max:65000,lab:'Stepped altitude',u:'ft',d:null},stepMach:{min:0.20,max:1.00,lab:'Stepped MMO',u:'Mach',d:null},
 alt:{min:-500,max:65000,lab:'Pressure altitude',u:'ft',d:35000},oat:{min:-110,max:60,lab:'OAT',u:'deg C',d:null},
 ice:{min:0,max:60,lab:'Ice penalty',u:'%',d:0},mach:{min:0.05,max:1.30,lab:'Mach',u:'',d:0.76},
 mStall:{min:0,max:60,lab:'Stall margin',u:'kt',d:10},mVmo:{min:0,max:60,lab:'Ceiling margin',u:'kt',d:10}};

function resolve(){
  var iss=[],val={},used=[];
  for(var k in RU){
    var r=RU[k],v=V(k);
    if(v===null||!isFinite(v)){
      if(r.d===null){val[k]=null;continue;}
      val[k]=r.d;used.push(r.lab);iss.push(r.lab+' blank - using default '+r.d+' '+r.u);
    }else if(v<r.min){val[k]=r.min;iss.push(r.lab+' below minimum, clamped');}
    else if(v>r.max){val[k]=r.max;iss.push(r.lab+' above maximum, clamped');}
    else val[k]=v;
  }
  if((val.stepAlt===null)!==(val.stepMach===null)){iss.push('Enter BOTH stepped altitude and stepped MMO, or neither');val.stepAlt=null;val.stepMach=null;}
  if(val.weight>val.wRef*1.02)iss.push('Above reference weight - sqrt(W) scaling is first-order');
  if(val.alt>50000)iss.push('Above FL500 - beyond any business-jet service ceiling');
  if(val.ice>25)iss.push('Ice penalty above the severe-icing range - verify');
  if(val.oat!==null){var d=isaAt(val.alt).Tc-val.oat;if(Math.abs(d)>25)iss.push('Large ISA deviation '+F(d,1)+' deg C');}
  if(val.mach>1.0)iss.push('Above Mach 1.0');
  return {val:val,iss:iss,used:used};
}

var PRE={lr60:{vsRef:185,wRef:27500,vmo:340,mmo:0.81,stepAlt:37000,stepMach:0.78,
  t:'Learjet 60 TEACHING CASE - ILLUSTRATIVE (commentary-chart values, NOT certified data). Replace with your own AFM.'},
 custom:{t:'Custom - enter Vs at reference weight, reference weight, VMO and MMO from your AFM, plus the stepped limit if you have one.'}};

function fillPreset(force){
  var p=PRE[$('preset').value];
  ['vsRef','wRef','vmo','mmo','stepAlt','stepMach'].forEach(function(k){
    var e=$(k);if(!e)return;if(p[k]!==undefined){if(force||!e.value)e.value=p[k];}});
  T('pHint',p.t);
  H('vsHint',$('preset').value==='lr60'?'Chart-implied stall line back-calculated at about 185 KIAS. ILLUSTRATIVE - not an AFM figure.':'From your AFM. Scaled as sqrt(weight / reference weight).');
}

function compute(v){
  var A=atmos(v.alt,v.oat);
  var vsW=v.vsRef*Math.sqrt(v.weight/v.wRef)+v.cfgInc, vsIce=vsW*(1+v.ice/100);
  var as=A.aSig, ok=(as>100&&as<700);
  var Mst=vsIce/as, MstC=vsW/as, ias=v.mach*as, tas=v.mach*A.aKt;
  var sm=($('stepMode')&&$('stepMode').value==='gt')?'gt':'ge';
  var step=(v.stepAlt!==null&&v.stepMach!==null&&(sm==='gt'?v.alt>v.stepAlt:v.alt>=v.stepAlt));
  var mmoEff=step?v.stepMach:v.mmo, mVmo=v.vmo/as;
  var cd=[[mmoEff,'MMO'+(step?' (stepped)':'')],[mVmo,'VMO '+v.vmo+' KIAS']].sort(function(a,b){return a[0]-b[0];});
  var ceil=cd[0][0], src=cd[0][1], iasC=ceil*as, tasC=ceil*A.aKt;
  var floor=(vsIce+v.mStall)/as, cpol=ceil-(v.mVmo)/as, closed=(floor>=cpol);
  var Vv=v.mach*A.aMs, q=0.5*A.rho*Vv*Vv, dens=A.rho/REF.rho, spd=Math.pow(Vv/REF.V,2);
  var qC=0.5*A.rho*Math.pow(ceil*A.aMs,2);
  var sKt=ias-vsIce, cKt=iasC-ias;
  var over=v.mach>ceil+1e-9, under=v.mach<Mst-1e-9, ufl=v.mach<floor-1e-9, opol=v.mach>cpol+1e-9;
  var verd='GO',cls='go',why=[],act=[],adv=[];
  if(!ok){verd='UNIT ERROR SUSPECTED';cls='no';why.push('a*sqrt(sigma) = '+F(as,1)+' kt is outside the plausible range 100-700 kt. A unit error is likely.');act.push('Do not use these figures.');}
  else if(closed){verd='NO-GO - BAND CLOSED';cls='no';why.push('No speed is simultaneously '+F(v.mStall,0)+' kt above the icing stall (M'+F(floor,3)+') and '+F(v.mVmo,0)+' kt below the ceiling (M'+F(cpol,3)+').');act.push('DESCEND. Change altitude, not speed.');}
  else if(over){verd='NO-GO - OVERSPEED';cls='no';why.push('Mach '+F(v.mach,3)+' exceeds the '+src+' ceiling of M'+F(ceil,3)+'.');act.push('Reduce speed and level the wings. Above the AFM limit there is no demonstrated structural or flutter margin.');}
  else if(under){verd='NO-GO - BELOW STALL';cls='no';why.push('Mach '+F(v.mach,3)+' is below the icing-adjusted stall Mach M'+F(Mst,3)+' ('+F(vsIce,1)+' KIAS).');act.push('Add energy now: pitch up, increase thrust. Expect no buffet warning in ice and a possible asymmetric break.');}
  else if(ufl){verd='NO-GO - STALL MARGIN INSUFFICIENT';cls='no';why.push('Only '+F(sKt,1)+' kt above the icing stall; policy requires '+F(v.mStall,0)+' kt.');act.push('Increase to at least M'+F(floor,3)+' if the ceiling allows, or descend out of the icing.');}
  else if(opol){verd='CAUTION - OVERSPEED MARGIN INSUFFICIENT';cls='caution';why.push('Below the hard ceiling but only '+F(cKt,1)+' kt below it; policy requires '+F(v.mVmo,0)+' kt.');act.push('Reduce Mach toward M'+F(cpol,3)+', or descend to open the band.');}
  else{why.push('Mach '+F(v.mach,3)+' is inside the policy window with '+F(sKt,1)+' kt above the icing stall and '+F(cKt,1)+' kt below the ceiling.');}
  if(step)adv.push({c:'info',t:'<b>Stepped limit applies:</b> '+src+'. The more restrictive of the two limits binds.'});
  if(over||opol)adv.push({c:'bad',t:'At or near the AFM speed limit. Beyond MMO there is no demonstrated margin - control-surface flutter and structural overstress become credible.'});
  if(v.ice>0)adv.push({c:'',t:'<b>Icing penalty applied:</b> stall '+F(vsW,1)+' to '+F(vsIce,1)+' KIAS (+'+F(v.ice,0)+' percent). Ice also lowers the stall angle of attack (buffet may never appear before the stall), degrades ailerons, and cuts elevator power - tailplane stall risk rises with Mach.'});
  if(closed&&ok)adv.push({c:'bad',t:'<b>The usable band is CLOSED</b> at this altitude, weight, configuration and ice state. No speed is safe. Altitude is the only variable that helps.'});
  if(v.mach>0.80)adv.push({c:'',t:'Above M0.80: Mach tuck reduces elevator effectiveness. Do not pull at high Mach.'});
  if(A.dev!==0)adv.push({c:'info',t:'OAT is '+F(Math.abs(A.dev),1)+' deg C '+((A.dev>0)?'colder':'warmer')+' than ISA here. Estimated geometric altitude about '+F(A.geom,0)+' ft.'});
  return {A:A,vsW:vsW,vsIce:vsIce,Mst:Mst,MstC:MstC,ias:ias,tas:tas,ceil:ceil,src:src,iasC:iasC,tasC:tasC,
    floor:floor,cpol:cpol,closed:closed,rawK:iasC-vsIce,winK:(cpol-floor)*as,q:q,qC:qC,dens:dens,spd:spd,
    qR:q/REF.q,sKt:sKt,cKt:cKt,step:step,mmoEff:mmoEff,mVmo:mVmo,as:as,ok:ok,
    verd:verd,cls:cls,why:why,act:act,adv:adv};
}

function render(){
  try{
  var rs=resolve(),v=rs.val,r=compute(v),mach=v.mach;
  var ve=$('verdict');ve.className='v '+r.cls;ve.innerHTML=r.verd+'<small>'+r.why.join(' ')+'</small>';
  var rows=[];
  function row(l,M,ta,ia){rows.push('<tr'+(l.indexOf('YOUR MACH')>=0?' style="font-weight:700"':'')+'><td>'+l+'</td><td>'+F(M,3)+'</td><td>'+F(ta,1)+'</td><td>'+F(ia,1)+'</td><td>'+(ia===null?'--':F(ia-r.vsIce,1))+'</td><td>'+F(M-mach,3)+'</td></tr>');}
  row('Stall - clean',r.MstC,r.MstC*r.A.aKt,r.vsW);
  row('Stall - +'+v.ice+'% ice',r.Mst,r.Mst*r.A.aKt,r.vsIce);
  row('Policy floor (stall +'+v.mStall+' kt)',r.floor,r.floor*r.A.aKt,r.vsIce+v.mStall);
  row('YOUR MACH',mach,r.tas,r.ias);
  row('Policy ceiling (limit -'+v.mVmo+' kt)',r.cpol,r.cpol*r.A.aKt,r.iasC-v.mVmo);
  row('Ceiling - '+r.src,r.ceil,r.tasC,r.iasC);
  fill('bt',rows.join(''));
  T('bNote','Raw band (hard boundaries, no margin) = '+F(r.rawK,1)+' kt ('+F(r.ceil-r.Mst,3)+' Mach). Policy window = '+F(r.winK,1)+' kt (M'+F(r.floor,3)+' to M'+F(r.cpol,3)+'). '+(r.closed?'POLICY WINDOW CLOSED.':'Outside it means less than '+v.mStall+' kt of stall margin or less than '+v.mVmo+' kt of ceiling margin.'));
  /* ---- band graphic ---- */
  var lo=Math.min(0.35,r.floor-0.06),hi=Math.max(0.90,r.ceil+0.06);
  lo=Math.floor(lo*20)/20;hi=Math.ceil(hi*20)/20;
  function pc(m){return Math.max(0,Math.min(100,(m-lo)/(hi-lo)*100));}
  var zr=$('zRaw'),zs=$('zSafe'),ms=$('mStallMk'),mc=$('mCeil'),mu=$('mCur');
  if(zr){zr.style.left=pc(r.Mst)+'%';zr.style.width=(pc(r.ceil)-pc(r.Mst))+'%';}
  if(zs){if(r.closed){zs.style.left='0%';zs.style.width='0%';}else{zs.style.left=pc(r.floor)+'%';zs.style.width=Math.max(0,pc(r.cpol)-pc(r.floor))+'%';}}
  if(ms)ms.style.left=pc(r.Mst)+'%';
  if(mc)mc.style.left=pc(r.ceil)+'%';
  if(mu)mu.style.left=pc(mach)+'%';
  var ls=$('lStall'),lc2=$('lCeil'),lu=$('lCur');
  if(ls){ls.style.left=Math.min(pc(r.Mst)+4,66)+'%';ls.textContent='stall M'+F(r.Mst,3);}
  if(lc2){lc2.style.left=Math.max(pc(r.ceil)-36,2)+'%';lc2.textContent='ceiling M'+F(r.ceil,3);}
  if(lu){lu.style.left=Math.min(pc(mach)+2,84)+'%';lu.textContent='YOU M'+F(mach,3);}
  var sc='';for(var mm=lo;mm<=hi+1e-9;mm+=0.1)sc+='<span style="left:'+pc(mm)+'%">M'+mm.toFixed(2)+'</span>';
  H('scale',sc);
  /* ---- tiles ---- */
  function K(l,val,s,c){return '<div class="k '+c+'"><div class="l">'+l+'</div><div class="v">'+val+'</div><div class="s">'+s+'</div></div>';}
  var cs=r.sKt<0?'bad':(r.sKt<v.mStall?'warn':'good'), cc=r.cKt<0?'bad':(r.cKt<v.mVmo?'warn':'good');
  H('kvs',
 K('Stall margin',F(mach-r.Mst,3),F(r.sKt,1)+' kt above the icing stall',cs)+
   K('Overspeed margin',F(r.ceil-mach,3),F(r.cKt,1)+' kt below the ceiling',cc)+
   K('q at your Mach',F(r.q/PSF,0)+' psf',F(r.q,0)+' Pa',r.qR>1.2?'warn':'info')+
   K('q vs cruise',F(r.qR,2)+'x',F(r.dens,2)+' density x '+F(r.spd,2)+' speed',r.qR>1.5?'warn':'')+
   K('q vs limit q',F(r.q/r.qC,3)+'x','(M/MMO)^2',r.q/r.qC>1?'bad':'good')+
   K('Your IAS',F(r.ias,1)+' K','TAS '+F(r.tas,1)+' kt','')+
   K('Icing stall',F(r.vsIce,1)+' K','M'+F(r.Mst,3)+' clean '+F(r.vsW,1)+' K','info')+
   K('Ceiling','M'+F(r.ceil,3),r.src+' to '+F(r.iasC,1)+' K','info'));
  var qr=[];
  function qrow(l,M){var Vv=M*r.A.aMs,q2=0.5*r.A.rho*Vv*Vv;qr.push('<tr><td>'+l+'</td><td>'+F(M,3)+'</td><td>'+F(M*r.A.aKt,1)+'</td><td>'+F(q2,0)+'</td><td>'+F(q2/PSF,0)+'</td><td>'+F(q2/REF.q,2)+'x</td></tr>');}
  qrow('Your Mach',mach);qrow('Policy ceiling',r.cpol);qrow('Hard ceiling ('+r.src+')',r.ceil);qrow('Stall +'+v.ice+'% ice',r.Mst);
  fill('qt',qr.join(''));
  T('qNote','Reference cruise = 35,000 ft, M0.76, ISA (q = '+F(REF.q/PSF,0)+' psf). In a descent the air thickens AND true airspeed rises: Mach can fall while q climbs. Track q, not Mach.');
  var A=r.A;
  H('atm',
   K('Pressure altitude',N(v.alt)+' ft','ISA here '+F(A.Tc,1)+' deg C','')+
   K('OAT',v.oat===null?'ISA':F(v.oat,0)+' deg C','ISA deviation '+F(A.dev,1)+' deg C','info')+
   K('Est. geometric alt',A.geom?F(A.geom,0)+' ft':'--','from OAT + pressure alt','info')+
   K('Sound speed a',F(A.aKt,1)+' kt',F(A.aMs,1)+' m/s = '+F(A.aMph,1)+' mph','')+
   K('Density sigma',F(A.sig,4),F(A.rho,4)+' kg/m3','')+
   K('a * sqrt(sigma)',F(A.as,1)+' kt','IAS per unit Mach',''));
  T('work',
'ATMOSPHERE\n'+
'  h_m   = '+N(v.alt)+' ft x 0.3048                    = '+F(A.I.hm,1)+' m\n'+
'  T_ISA = 288.15 - 0.0065*h_m                     = '+F(A.Tc,2)+' deg C  ('+F(A.I.T,2)+' K)\n'+
'  p     = 101325*(1 - 0.0065*h_m/288.15)^5.25588   = '+F(A.p,0)+' Pa\n'+
'  T     = '+(v.oat===null?'T_ISA (OAT blank)':(F(v.oat,1)+' deg C + 273.15'))+'               = '+F(A.T,2)+' K\n'+
'  rho   = p/(R*T) = '+F(A.p,0)+'/(287.053*'+F(A.T,2)+')        = '+F(A.rho,5)+' kg/m3\n'+
'  sigma = rho/1.225 = '+F(A.sig,5)+'\n'+
'  a     = sqrt(1.4*287.053*'+F(A.T,2)+')                = '+F(A.aMs,2)+' m/s = '+F(A.aKt,1)+' kt = '+F(A.aMph,1)+' mph\n'+
'  a*sqrt(sigma) = a_kt*sqrt(sigma)                = '+F(A.aKt,1)+'*'+F(A.ss,4)+'      = '+F(A.as,2)+' kt   (IAS per unit Mach)\n'+
'\nSTALL\n'+
'  Vs(W)   = '+F(v.vsRef,0)+'*sqrt('+N(v.weight)+'/'+N(v.wRef)+') + '+F(v.cfgInc,0)+'         = '+F(r.vsW,1)+' KIAS\n'+
'  Vs_ice  = '+F(r.vsW,1)+'*(1 + '+F(v.ice,0)+'%/100)             = '+F(r.vsIce,1)+' KIAS\n'+
'  Mstall  = Vs_ice/(a*sqrt(sigma)) = '+F(r.vsIce,1)+'/'+F(A.as,2)+'          = M'+F(r.Mst,3)+'\n'+
'\nCEILING  (more restrictive limit binds)\n'+
'  MMO_eff = '+(r.step?'stepped (alt '+(($('stepMode')&&$('stepMode').value==='gt')?'>':'>=')+' '+N(v.stepAlt)+' ft)':'general')+'                 = M'+F(r.mmoEff,3)+'\n'+
'  M_VMO   = '+F(v.vmo,0)+'/'+F(A.as,2)+'                           = M'+F(r.mVmo,3)+'\n'+
'  Ceiling = min(MMO_eff, M_VMO)  ['+r.src+']         = M'+F(r.ceil,3)+'\n'+
'\nPOLICY WINDOW\n'+
'  Floor = ('+F(r.vsIce,1)+' + '+F(v.mStall,0)+')/'+F(A.as,2)+'                 = M'+F(r.floor,3)+'\n'+
'  Ceiling_pol = M'+F(r.ceil,3)+' - '+F(v.mVmo,0)+'/'+F(A.as,2)+'                 = M'+F(r.cpol,3)+'\n'+
'  Width       = ('+F(r.cpol,3)+' - '+F(r.floor,3)+')*'+F(A.as,2)+'           = '+F(r.winK,1)+' kt'+(r.closed?'  CLOSED':'')+'\n'+
'\nMARGINS\n'+
'  Stall margin   = M'+F(mach,3)+' - M'+F(r.Mst,3)+'                = M'+F(mach-r.Mst,3)+'  ('+F(r.sKt,1)+' kt)\n'+
'  Ceiling margin = M'+F(r.ceil,3)+' - M'+F(mach,3)+'                = M'+F(r.ceil-mach,3)+'  ('+F(r.cKt,1)+' kt)\n'+
'\nq\n'+
'  V       = M*a = '+F(mach,3)+'*'+F(A.aMs,2)+'                  = '+F(mach*A.aMs,2)+' m/s\n'+
'  q       = 0.5*rho*V^2 = 0.5*'+F(A.rho,4)+'*'+F(mach*A.aMs,1)+'^2         = '+F(r.q,0)+' Pa = '+F(r.q/PSF,0)+' psf\n'+
'  q_ratio = (rho/rho_ref)*(V/V_ref)^2 = ('+F(r.dens,3)+')*('+F(r.spd,3)+')      = '+F(r.qR,3)+'x cruise\n'+
'\nVERDICT: '+r.verd);
  var h='';
  if(rs.iss.length)h+='<div class="nb">Input notes: '+rs.iss.join('; ')+'</div>';
  r.adv.forEach(function(a){h+='<div class="nb '+a.c+'">'+a.t+'</div>';});
  if(r.act.length)h+='<div class="nb info"><b>RECOMMENDED ACTION:</b> '+r.act.join(' ')+'</div>';
  H('adv',h);
  health(r,v,rs);
  }catch(e){bail('render: '+(e&&e.message?e.message:e));}
}

function health(r,v,rs){
  function K(l,val,s,c){return '<div class="k '+c+'"><div class="l">'+l+'</div><div class="v">'+val+'</div><div class="s">'+s+'</div></div>';}
  H('health',
   K('Boot','OK','script parsed and running','good')+
   K('Errors',ERR.length?ERR.join(' | '):'none','error trap','good')+
   K('a*sqrt(sigma) units',F(r.as,1)+' kt','must be 100-700 kt - '+(r.ok?'OK':'SUSPECT'),r.ok?'good':'bad')+
   K('Input notes',String(rs.iss.length),rs.used.length?('defaults used: '+rs.used.join(', ')):'all fields valid',rs.iss.length?'warn':'good')+
   K('Verdict',r.verd,r.src,'info'));
}

var DIV={rows:[{t:'12:48:58',alt:26272,gs:484,vs:-26368},{t:'12:49:16',alt:14806,gs:362,vs:-32640},{t:'12:49:26',alt:6506,gs:251,vs:-32640}]};
function diveIn(){
  var h='';
  DIV.rows.forEach(function(o,i){
    function vv(x){return (x===null||x===undefined||isNaN(x))?'':x;}
    h+='<tr><td>'+(i===0?'B':(i===1?'C':(i===2?'D':'E')))+'</td><td><input data-d="t" data-i="'+i+'" value="'+vv(o.t)+'" style="width:80px;padding:4px;font-size:13px"></td>'+
      '<td><input data-d="alt" data-i="'+i+'" value="'+vv(o.alt)+'" style="width:80px;padding:4px;font-size:13px"></td>'+
      '<td><input data-d="gs" data-i="'+i+'" value="'+vv(o.gs)+'" style="width:70px;padding:4px;font-size:13px"></td>'+
      '<td><input data-d="vs" data-i="'+i+'" value="'+vv(o.vs)+'" style="width:80px;padding:4px;font-size:13px"></td>'+
      '<td><button class="btn" data-del="'+i+'" style="padding:2px 7px">x</button></td></tr>';});
  fill('dIn',h);
}
function diveCalc(){
  try{
  var pts=[];
  DIV.rows.forEach(function(o){
    var al=parseFloat(o.alt),gs=parseFloat(o.gs),vs=parseFloat(o.vs);
    if(isNaN(al)||isNaN(gs))return;
    var A=atmos(al,null),g=gs*0.514444,vv=(isNaN(vs)?0:vs)/60*0.3048;
    var tas=Math.sqrt(g*g+vv*vv),fpa=Math.asin(Math.max(-1,Math.min(1,vv/tas)))*180/Math.PI;
    pts.push({alt:al,tas:tas,kt:tas/KT,fpa:fpa,mach:tas/A.aMs,q:0.5*A.rho*tas*tas,gs:gs,sat:(!isNaN(vs)&&Math.abs(vs)>=32600)});});
  var h='';
  pts.forEach(function(p,k){
    var tv=null;
    if(k>0&&pts.length){var d2=pts[0].tas*pts[0].tas-(p.gs*0.514444)*(p.gs*0.514444);if(d2>0)tv=Math.sqrt(d2)/0.3048*60;}
    h+='<tr><td>'+(k===0?'B':(k===1?'C':(k===2?'D':'E')))+'</td><td>'+F(p.kt,1)+'</td><td>'+F(p.tas,2)+'</td><td>'+F(p.fpa,1)+'</td><td>'+F(p.mach,3)+'</td><td>'+F(p.q,0)+'</td><td>'+F(p.q/PSF,0)+'</td><td>'+F(p.q/REF.q,2)+'x</td><td>'+(tv===null?'--':F(tv,0)+(p.sat?' *':''))+'</td></tr>';});
  fill('dOut',h);
  var a='';
  if(pts.length>=2){
    var p0=pts[0],p1=pts[pts.length-1];
    a+='<div class="nb info"><b>Mach went '+(p1.mach<p0.mach?'DOWN':'UP')+' from M'+F(p0.mach,3)+' to M'+F(p1.mach,3)+' while q went '+(p1.q>p0.q?'UP':'DOWN')+' from '+F(p0.q/PSF,0)+' to '+F(p1.q/PSF,0)+' psf ('+F(p1.q/p0.q,2)+'x).</b> In a descent the sound speed rises, so Mach can fall while dynamic pressure climbs. <b>Track q, not Mach.</b></div>';
    if(pts.some(function(p){return p.sat;})){var lo=1e9,hi=0;
      pts.forEach(function(p,k){if(k>0){var d2=pts[0].tas*pts[0].tas-(p.gs*0.514444)*(p.gs*0.514444);if(d2>0){var t=Math.sqrt(d2)/0.3048*60;lo=Math.min(lo,t);hi=Math.max(hi,t);}}});
      if(isFinite(lo)&&hi>0)a+='<div class="nb bad"><b>Vertical-rate field SATURATED</b> (32,640 fpm is the ADS-B maximum). Solving from a constant-true-airspeed assumption gives about '+N(lo)+' to '+N(hi)+' fpm - the published field understates the descent rate by roughly '+F((1-32640/hi)*100,0)+' to '+F((1-32640/lo)*100,0)+' percent.</div>';}
    if(pts.length>=3)a+='<div class="nb info">Cross-check: GPS altitude changed '+N(p0.alt-p1.alt)+' ft between the first and last point. Compare with the solved vertical rate above.</div>';
  }
  H('dAdv',a);
  }catch(e){bail('diveCalc: '+(e&&e.message?e.message:e));}
}

function selfTest(){
  var out=[],pass=true,failed=0;
  function chk(n,g,e,tol){var o=Math.abs(g-e)<=(tol||0.05);if(!o){pass=false;failed++;}out.push((o?'PASS  ':'FAIL  ')+n+'   got '+g+'   expected '+e);}
  var snap={},keys=['preset','vsRef','wRef','weight','cfgInc','vmo','mmo','stepAlt','stepMach','alt','oat','ice','mach','mStall','mVmo','stepMode'];
  keys.forEach(function(k){snap[k]=$(k)?$(k).value:'';});
  $('preset').value='lr60';fillPreset(true);$('stepMode').value='ge';
  $('weight').value=27500;$('alt').value=37000;$('oat').value=-47;$('ice').value=10;$('mach').value=0.76;$('cfgInc').value=0;
  var rs=resolve(),r=compute(rs.val);
  chk('T1 Mstall FL370 +10% ice',r.Mst,0.767,0.002);
  chk('T1 IAS at M0.76',r.ias,201.7,0.3);
  chk('T1 stall margin KIAS',r.sKt,-1.8,0.3);
  chk('T1 ceiling Mach stepped',r.ceil,0.78,0.002);
  chk('T1 overspeed margin KIAS',r.cKt,5.3,0.3);
  chk('T1 q psf',r.q/PSF,183,1.5);
  chk('T1 verdict BELOW STALL',r.verd.indexOf('BELOW STALL')>=0?1:0,1,0);
  $('alt').value=35000;$('ice').value=0;rs=resolve();r=compute(rs.val);
  chk('T2 Mstall FL350 clean',r.Mst,0.664,0.002);
  chk('T2 IAS at M0.76',r.ias,211.8,0.3);
  chk('T2 stall margin KIAS',r.sKt,26.8,0.4);
  chk('T2 ceiling Mach general',r.ceil,0.81,0.002);
  chk('T2 overspeed margin KIAS',r.cKt,13.9,0.4);
  chk('T2 q psf',r.q/PSF,201,1.5);
  chk('T2 q ratio vs cruise',r.qR,1.00,0.02);
  chk('T2 verdict GO',r.verd==='GO'?1:0,1,0);
  chk('T2 raw band KIAS',r.rawK,40.7,0.5);
  $('alt').value=37000;$('ice').value=10;rs=resolve();r=compute(rs.val);
  chk('T3 band CLOSED at +10% ice',r.closed?1:0,1,0);
  chk('T3 a kt FL370 ISA',atmos(37000,null).aKt,497.9,0.6);
  chk('T3 a kt FL350 ISA',atmos(35000,null).aKt,500.5,0.6);
  chk('T4 REF q psf',REF.q/PSF,201.3,0.6);
  chk('T4 a*sqrt(sigma) kt FL350',atmos(35000,null).aSig,278.6,0.5);
  chk('T4 a*sqrt(sigma) kt FL370',atmos(37000,null).aSig,265.4,0.5);
  chk('T4 a constant above tropopause',Math.abs(atmos(39000,null).aKt-atmos(43000,null).aKt),0,0.05);
  keys.forEach(function(k){if($(k))$(k).value=snap[k];});
  fillPreset(false);
  out.push('');out.push(pass?'ALL '+(NCHK-failed)+'/'+NCHK+' CHECKS PASSED':'*** '+failed+' CHECK(S) FAILED ***');
  T('testOut',out.join('\n'));
  $('testOut').className=pass?'ok':'bad';
  render();
  return pass;
}

function diag(){

  var t='MISSING IDS ('+m.length+'): '+(m.length?m.join(', '):'(none)')+'\nERRORS ('+ERR.length+'): '+(ERR.length?ERR.join(' | '):'(none)')+
    '\na*sqrt(sigma) FL350 = '+F(atmos(35000,null).aSig,2)+' kt (expect 278.6)'+
    '\na*sqrt(sigma) FL370 = '+F(atmos(37000,null).aSig,2)+' kt (expect 265.4)'+
    '\na kt FL370 ISA = '+F(atmos(37000,null).aKt,2)+' kt (expect 497.9)';
  T('diag',t);$('diag').style.display='block';
  try{console.log(t);}catch(e){}
}

 'Stall(ice) M'+F(r.Mst,3)+' ('+F(r.vsIce,1)+' KIAS)   Ceiling M'+F(r.ceil,3)+' ('+F(r.iasC,1)+' KIAS, '+r.src+')\n'+

# POST‑SUMMARY

**1. The missing band graphic was my call and it was the wrong one.** I trimmed v1.7 and swept the graphic out with the chrome. That graphic — amber raw band, green policy window, three markers — is the **teaching centrepiece** of the tool. It's back, and it now carries a **"Mach vs YOU"** column beneath it showing signed distances (+0.037 / −0.018 / +0.020), so the graphic and the table say the same thing.

**2. I also made the table fills immune to a stripped `<tbody>`.** `fill()` now creates the `<tbody>` if it's missing, and `ensure()` recreates any missing element at boot. If your pipeline strips empty `<tbody>` tags — which some HTML minifiers do — that alone would have killed the table *and everything after it*. Now it can't.

**3. The provenance panel now states the case correctly:** *"The aircraft departed controlled flight during a climb at about 35,450 ft."* Not "at FL370."

**4. Once the graphic is confirmed, the tool is done.** I'd rather not iterate again on the file — and I'd rather move on to the things that actually matter.

**5. Next: the study materials, all rebuilt around the corrected physics.** Ready to generate on your word:
- **Cockpit card v1.1** — tropopause‑correct ISA table (a = 497.9 kt constant above FL360), corrected Mstall (FL390 0.731, FL410 0.767, FL430 0.805, band closes ≈ FL415), q‑ratio decomposition, the MMO conflict, flutter/certification basis, and the severe‑turbulence inspection trigger.
- **One‑page student handout** — the 35,450 ft case, the "6‑knot margin" headline, and the tropopause lesson.
- **Revised instructor exercise + answer key** — with the margin‑adjusted window, the ADS‑B dive lab, and the Fig. 10 / Fig. 11 critiques.

**6. One question, and then I'll stop asking:** does the band graphic appear? If yes, say **"graphic is there"** and I'll produce the three refreshed artefacts. If no, press **DIAGNOSE** in the REFERENCE tab and paste me the output — it lists every missing ID and the first JS error, which will close this out in one pass.

### USER
Some new data from today, Detailed Summary and Analysis
**Scope note:** This is an analysis of the transcript supplied, not an independent fact-check. The 2026 Green Acres crash is presented as unresolved, so its causes, exact radio wording, weather findings, ownership details, and any allegations concerning the pilots should be treated as **claims pending official evidence**, not established facts.

## Central thesis

The transcript argues that aviation crashes are repeatedly compressed into emotionally satisfying stories about the individual holding the controls, while the deeper causes—aircraft design, certification, maintenance systems, training, prior accidents, regulatory delays, and institutional incentives—disappear from public understanding.
Its strongest historical example is the 2008 Learjet 60 crash in Columbia, South Carolina. The official probable cause did identify the pilots’ actions and inadequate tire maintenance, but the full NTSB report also documented a flight-critical electronic reverser flaw that converted an error into a fatal runaway. The video’s larger argument is that:

> **The question “What did the pilot do?” is necessary but insufficient. The more consequential question is: “Why did the organization design, certify, maintain, train, and regulate the aircraft in a way that made a recoverable mistake unsurvivable?”**

That distinction is the heart of the transcript.

* * *

# 1. Executive Summary

1.  **The script opens with an unresolved 2026 crash.**  
    A nine-person Learjet 60 flight from Austin to the Bahamas disintegrates during a descent over South Florida. All nine occupants die. The twenty-year-old first officer is quickly blamed online for her age, voice, gender, and perceived lack of experience.
    
2.  **The central legal and ethical criticism is premature blame.**  
    The script invokes NASA administrator Jared Isaacman to argue that the public cannot know whether maintenance problems, structural failure, weather, overspeed, control failure, or some other mechanism caused the accident.
    
3.  **The script then revisits the 2008 Columbia Learjet 60 crash.**  
    Four people aboard that aircraft died, including both pilots. The aircraft overran the runway after a rejected takeoff and struck an embankment across a highway.
    
4.  **The official Columbia narrative named two principal causes.**  
    The NTSB’s 2010 probable cause cited:
    *   Inadequate tire maintenance leading to severe underinflation and tire failure.
        
    *   The captain’s rejected takeoff after V1, inconsistent with training and operating procedures.
        
5.  **The full report, however, contained a consequential design and certification finding.**  
    Debris from the tire failures damaged hydraulic lines and a landing-gear “squat switch.” The electronic reverser system misinterpreted the damaged switch as evidence that the aircraft was airborne. The reversers stowed, and the engine controls switched to forward thrust—even though the pilot still had the reverse levers pulled aft.
    
6.  **This created the mechanism behind the Columbia accident.**  
    The airplane initially slowed, then accelerated toward the runway end while its brakes deteriorated. The cockpit offered no explanation that forward thrust had been reintroduced. The only corrective action was counterintuitive: move the reverse levers forward.
    
7.  **The same failure mode had already caused an accident in 2001.**  
    After a Learjet 60 struck deer during landing in Alabama, debris jammed the squat switch. The reversers stowed, forward thrust returned, and the airplane ran off the runway. Both pilots survived with serious injuries. Thus, the danger was documented in a federal accident report before Columbia occurred.
    
8.  **The manufacturer and FAA did not make the systemic correction demanded by the evidence.**  
    The reverser procedure was revised and moved in the flight manual, but no immediate mandatory hardware redesign, cockpit warning, physical lever synchronization, or takeoff-specific simulator training was required.
    
9.  **The regulatory response was extremely slow.**  
    Although the NTSB issued urgent reverser recommendations in 2009, the FAA did not mandate a voice-warning system until 2020, with a phased compliance period extending as late as 2024. The FAA itself acknowledged that relying on pilot response to address a catastrophic hazard was “not proper.”
    
10.  **The 2026 crash cannot simply be explained by the Columbia mechanism.**  
     Green Acres was not a runway-overrun accident. It involved ice, turbulence, a possible convective cell, overspeed, a steep bank, a high-speed dive, and separation of the left elevator. The final causal sequence remains unknown.
     

* * *

# 2. Chapter-by-Chapter Analysis

## Chapter 1 — The 2026 Green Acres crash: the hook and the blame narrative

### What the transcript presents

At 12:48 p.m. on November 1, 2026, a Learjet 60 with nine people aboard is flying from Austin to the Bahamas over South Florida at 35,000 feet. The flight has been airborne for approximately two hours.
The copilot reports:

*   Light clear ice.
    
*   Moderate turbulence.
    
*   Altitude deviations of up to roughly 700 feet.
    
*   A request to climb from 35,000 to 37,000 feet.
    

The climb is approved. Within approximately thirty seconds, the crew transmits a mayday, reports an overspeed condition, and says they are descending at about 20,000 feet per minute in a 60-degree bank. A later transmission states that they are going to crash.
The airplane hits a military trail in Green Acres at roughly 350 feet per second. All nine occupants die:

*   A technology founder.
    
*   His wife.
    
*   Their four-year-old and two-year-old children.
    
*   The children’s caregiver.
    
*   Two family friends.
    
*   Captain Jeff Gregory, age fifty-five.
    
*   First Officer Nathalia Filgeras, age twenty.
    

The script says that the recording circulated widely and that public commentary quickly focused on the first officer’s voice, age, and the word “DEI.” It contrasts this reaction with Isaacman’s reminder that the public did not know the aircraft’s maintenance history, whether a structural failure occurred, or what actually initiated the loss of control.

### Analytical function

This opening serves three purposes:

1.  **It creates an urgent, contemporary moral problem:** blaming a dead, young worker without evidence.
    
2.  **It establishes the recurring pattern:** the pilot becomes the instinctive explanation for a crash.
    
3.  **It introduces the documentary’s method:** look beyond the individual to the larger system.
    

The scene is intentionally emotionally forceful. Its central question is not whether the copilot did anything wrong, but whether the public has any evidentiary basis for saying so.

* * *

## Chapter 2 — The elevator: the unresolved physical cause

Two days after the crash, the NTSB reportedly announced that most of the airplane’s left elevator had been recovered in three pieces between approximately 1.3 and 3.8 miles behind the impact site.
That establishes that the left elevator separated substantially before ground impact. Two causal sequences remain possible:

### Sequence A: The elevator separated first

*   The elevator or its attachment failed or tore away.
    
*   The crew lost or degraded pitch-control authority.
    
*   The airplane departed controlled flight.
    
*   The dive and overspeed followed.
    

### Sequence B: The dive separated the elevator

*   Some other initiating event began the descent.
    
*   The increasing speed and aerodynamic loads then tore away the elevator.
    
*   The separation further worsened control and structural stress.
    

The investigator quoted in the script reportedly doubted that keeping the elevator attached would have helped at the aircraft’s speed during the dive.

### Analytical importance

The elevator finding prevents a false analogy between Green Acres and Columbia. The earlier accident involved a wheel-well failure and electronic reverser logic. Green Acres involved an in-flight structural or control-surface separation. The institutional lesson may recur; the physical mechanism probably does not.

* * *

## Chapter 3 — The 2008 Columbia accident

### The people and aircraft

On September 19, 2008, musicians Travis Barker and DJ AM leave a concert at the University of South Carolina. Barker, who reportedly disliked flying, travels with assistant Chris Baker and security guard Charles Still.
The aircraft is:

*   Learjet 60.
    
*   Registration N999LJ.
    
*   Operated by Global Exec Aviation.
    
*   Less than two years old.
    

The crew consists of:

*   Captain Sarah Lemon, thirty-one, with approximately 3,100 total flight hours and about 35 hours in the Learjet 60, eight of them as captain.
    
*   First Officer James Bland, fifty-two, with approximately 8,200 total hours and roughly 300 hours in type.
    

Lemon had passed her type check five weeks earlier, and the examiner reportedly found her performance well within standards. Both pilots had joined the company that year.

### The takeoff

At approximately 11:53 p.m., the crew receives takeoff clearance. The aircraft accelerates normally through the first 2,000 feet.
Approximately 1.5 seconds after V1:

1.  A series of sharp bangs is heard.
    
2.  Bland asks what the sound was.
    
3.  Lemon says she does not know.
    
4.  She states, “We’re not going through.”
    
5.  She reduces thrust, applies brakes, and reaches for the reverse levers.
    

At approximately 144 knots, the aircraft briefly slows. It then accelerates again.
Bland tells the crew to roll the equipment and warns that they are going off the end. The Learjet crosses the runway end, the safety area, airport fencing, lights, and concrete structures, then crosses a five-lane highway before striking an embankment.
Barker and DJ AM escape through a burning cabin. Lemon, Bland, Baker, and Still remain inside and die. Six people were aboard; four died.

### Why this sequence matters

The transcript uses the acceleration after the apparent deceleration as the clue to the full story. A mechanical failure had occurred, but the airplane’s subsequent behavior was not simply the result of blown tires. Something else was adding or sustaining forward thrust.

* * *

## Chapter 4 — The official narrative and how it traveled

The NTSB’s April 6, 2010 probable-cause finding identified:

1.  The operator’s inadequate tire maintenance.
    
2.  Severe underinflation.
    
3.  Multiple tire failures during the takeoff roll.
    
4.  The captain’s rejected takeoff after V1 as inconsistent with training and standard operating procedures.
    

The script argues that the first half was administratively less compelling than the second. Tire maintenance sounds mundane; a young captain “panicking” after V1 is narratively memorable.
The report also discussed:

*   Lemon’s limited time in type and as pilot in command.
    
*   Poor crew resource management as a contributing factor.
    
*   The standard rule that a rejected takeoff after V1 is hazardous and contrary to training.
    

The transcript contrasts this with the counterfactual discussed in accident literature: if the airplane remained controllable and the pilot continued takeoff, the landing might have been rough or dangerous, but all six people might have survived.

### Critical qualification

The transcript does not deny that the rejected takeoff after V1 was contrary to established procedures. Its more precise claim is:

> The pilot’s decision was wrong by the book, but the airplane then behaved in a way the cockpit did not reveal, turning a serious decision into a fatal one.

That is a causal-context argument, not an exculpation based on ignorance of aviation standards.

* * *

## Chapter 5 — Maintenance failure and a barrier placed in front of the pilots

The Learjet 60’s main landing-gear tires were supposed to operate at approximately 219 psi. Investigators reconstructed the pressures on N999LJ at approximately 140 psi—about one-third below specification.
The script notes that even a 5% underinflation condition can sharply shorten aircraft-tire fatigue life because each taxi cycle flexes the sidewall beyond its design condition. At the reconstructed pressure, the tires may have been deteriorating for weeks.
Other reported deficiencies include:

*   The tires had not apparently been checked for approximately three weeks.
    
*   The maintenance director did not know the required inspection interval.
    
*   Nobody had complied with the manufacturer’s stated first-flight-of-the-day or periodic check requirement.
    
*   Operators were directed to check tire pressures at least every 96 hours under a later FAA directive.
    

Most importantly for the pilot-blame argument, **the pilots were not permitted to perform the inspection themselves**. Under the charter-operation rules then in effect, tire-pressure checking was classified as maintenance work.
The visible condition of an aircraft tire also does not reliably communicate its internal pressure. An underinflated aircraft tire may not look flat.
The NTSB consequently recommended allowing pilots in air-taxi operations to conduct pressure checks. As the transcript puts it, the dead crew was denied a practical means of detecting one of the initiating conditions.

* * *

## Chapter 6 — The technical mechanism of the Columbia disaster

This is the documentary’s most important technical section.

### Step 1: Multiple tire failures

The right-side tires fail first, followed by the left-side tires. Shredded rubber and steel bead wire are expelled into the wheel well at more than 100 mph.

### Step 2: Hydraulic and sensor damage

The debris:

*   Cuts hydraulic lines, causing the brakes to lose pressure.
    
*   Damages a squat switch, a sensor that tells the aircraft’s systems whether the landing gear is on the ground or in the air.
    

### Step 3: The electronic reverser system misinterprets the sensor

The Learjet 60 was the first Learjet with fully electronic thrust-reverser control. There is no mechanical cable linking the cockpit levers to the engines. The pilot commands a position, and the engine-control computers decide how to interpret it.
The system contains a “fail-safe” rule: if the aircraft is believed to be airborne, the reversers must stow to prevent an in-flight deployment.

### Step 4: The damaged squat switch creates a false airborne condition

While the aircraft is still on the runway, the damaged switch tells the system that it is flying. The reversers stow.
The cockpit levers do not follow them. The pilot still has the reverse levers pulled fully aft because she is trying to stop the airplane.

### Step 5: Reverse-lever position produces forward thrust

The engine computers switch from the reverse-thrust schedule to the forward-thrust schedule. Because the levers remain aft, that command is translated into something close to takeoff thrust, directed forward.
Thus:

*   The pilot believes she is commanding maximum reverse thrust.
    
*   The engines are actually delivering powerful forward thrust.
    
*   Her brakes are simultaneously failing.
    
*   The airplane initially decelerates and then accelerates.
    

### Step 6: The cockpit gives no warning

There was:

*   No warning light.
    
*   No oral alert.
    
*   No movement of the cockpit levers.
    
*   No clear indication that the engines had reverted to forward thrust.
    

According to the transcript, the FAA later advised pilots that the only way to reduce the forward thrust was to push the reverse levers forward into their stowed position—the exact opposite of the instinctive action during a rejected takeoff.

### The NTSB finding

The report identified deficiencies in Learjet’s design of, and the FAA’s certification of, the thrust-reverser system. Those deficiencies permitted wheel-well failures to produce uncommanded forward thrust that increased the severity of the accident.

### The crucial nuance

This finding does not establish that the design flaw alone caused the crash. It establishes that the design materially worsened the outcome.
The accident therefore contains at least two distinct questions:

1.  **Why did the takeoff become hazardous?**  
    Severely underinflated tires failed.
    
2.  **Why could the crew not recover from the hazardous condition?**  
    The electronic reverser system converted the wheel-well damage into uncommanded forward thrust, with no cockpit indication.
    

The official headline largely captured the first and a pilot decision, while relegating the second to contributing factors.

* * *

## Chapter 7 — The 2001 Alabama precedent

On January 14, 2001, a Learjet 60 touched down at Troy Municipal Airport in Alabama at approximately 124 knots. About 4.2 seconds and 1,500 feet later, it struck two deer.
The crew selected reverse thrust, but the reversers stopped functioning. The airplane continued off the end of the 5,000-foot runway, collapsed its landing gear, and caught fire. Both pilots survived with serious injuries.
The investigators found deer fur jammed in the left main-gear squat switch. The same sequence followed:

1.  The sensor falsely indicated that the aircraft was airborne.
    
2.  The reversers stowed.
    
3.  The engine controls returned to forward thrust.
    
4.  The levers remained aft because the pilot was trying to stop.
    
5.  The airplane accelerated during rollout and departed the runway.
    

The NTSB explicitly identified inadvertent reverser stowage and subsequent forward thrust as the probable cause.

### Why this is decisive

The Columbia mechanism was not an unforeseeable hypothetical. It had already:

*   Injured two pilots.
    
*   Been formally documented.
    
*   Been known to the manufacturer and FAA.
    

Yet the corrective response was limited to a manual-procedure change. No immediate mandatory design change, cockpit warning, physical lever feedback, or takeoff-specific simulator training was required.
This is the central organizational-failure claim: **the industry possessed a known failure mode and did not convert that knowledge into a robust engineering or training solution.**

* * *

## Chapter 8 — Certification, derivative design, and charter economics

### Certification lineage

The Learjet 60 was certified in 1993 as a changed product rather than as a completely new aircraft. It was derived from older designs, including a longer Learjet 55 fuselage, new engines, and a tail derived from the Learjet 35, all connected to a certification lineage reaching back to the Learjet 24 of the 1960s.
That was legally normal and commercially advantageous. A derivative model need not demonstrate that the entire aircraft meets every modern standard; it need only establish that the changes are safe.
The NTSB’s later criticism was that this approach left a gap when a new electronic reverser system was added to an old certification basis. No certification question adequately addressed what would happen if the wheel well were destroyed while the aircraft remained on the ground.

### Market economics

Original Learjet 60 production ended in 2007 after slightly more than 300 aircraft had been built. N999LJ was among the last of the line.
By 2018, a used Learjet 60 could be bought for less than $1 million. The transcript connects this market position to:

*   Heavy charter utilization.
    
*   Thin maintenance margins.
    
*   Ownership through Delaware limited-liability companies.
    
*   Operations at the lowest regulatory cost.
    
*   A large pool of aircraft entering high-utilization business flying.
    

### Analytical point

The aircraft was not simply an old design suffering from an isolated pilot mistake. Its design, production history, resale value, and operating economics placed it in an environment where cost pressure and regulatory minimums could dominate safety margins.

* * *

## Chapter 9 — Litigation and regulation

### Legal positioning

Before the final NTSB report, Learjet answered Travis Barker’s lawsuit by arguing that any aircraft conditions were solely the result of improper maintenance and service—not the manufacturer’s design.
The manufacturer then settled confidentially with the plaintiffs before a jury could weigh that position against the federal findings.

### Important legal qualification

A legal defense is not proof of deliberate deception, and a confidential settlement is not necessarily an admission. The transcript’s stronger and more defensible claim is about **incentive and asymmetry**:

*   The manufacturer had lawyers, institutional resources, and the ability to shift causation toward maintenance personnel.
    
*   The pilots were dead and unable to answer.
    
*   Settlements removed much of the public evidence that would have tested the competing narratives in court.
    

### NTSB recommendations and FAA response

In July 2009, the NTSB issued urgent recommendations including:

1.  Redesign the thrust-lever system so that the cockpit levers move with the reversers, giving tactile feedback.
    
2.  Retrofit the existing fleet.
    
3.  Install oral or visual warnings of inadvertent reverser stowage.
    
4.  Train every Learjet 60 pilot on the scenario during takeoff, not merely landing.
    
5.  Examine the Raytheon Hawker 1000 for a similar failure mode.
    
6.  Require tire-pressure monitoring for transport-category aircraft.
    
7.  Establish a minimum time in type for charter captains rather than relying only on a type rating.
    

The FAA responded relatively quickly on tires, issuing a 2010 airworthiness directive requiring pressure checks at least every 96 hours.
The reverser warning took much longer:

*   NTSB request: 2009.
    
*   FAA directive requiring a voice-warning system: effective June 25, 2020.
    
*   Estimated cost: just under $30,000 per aircraft.
    
*   Affected registered fleet: approximately 289 aircraft in the United States.
    
*   Compliance period: within 1,200 flight hours or 48 months, whichever came first.
    
*   Potential absence of the warning on a low-utilization aircraft until June 2024.
    

When the NTSB later asked the FAA to shorten the window, the FAA declined.
The most revealing portion of the transcript is the FAA’s own statement in the Federal Register that relying on pilot response to address a catastrophic hazard was “not proper.” That response exposes the contradiction: the agency had spent two decades depending on pilot response while simultaneously acknowledging that this was not a proper safety strategy.
The NTSB’s highest-priority recommendation—physically matching the levers to the engines—was directed at future production. But production had already ended. As the script puts it, the redesign was requested for an airplane that would never be built again, while the retrofit depended on a design change that had no corporate reason to exist.

* * *

## Chapter 10 — Institutional failure and the continuing incident record

The transcript lists several later Learjet 60 events, including:

*   A 2013 crash on approach in Valencia, Venezuela, killing both pilots.
    
*   A brake fire in 2018.
    
*   A runway excursion at Chicago Executive Airport in 2020.
    
*   A 2023 Boston-area incident involving a JetBlue flight.
    
*   An April 2026 runway excursion in which the pilot reported brake problems.
    

It cites the aircraft type’s Wikipedia entry as describing an unusually high incident rate for its class, with many accidents occurring on runways, and attributing part of that pattern to the combination of:

*   Small wheels.
    
*   Small tires.
    
*   Small brakes for the aircraft’s weight.
    
*   An electronic reverser capable of producing forward thrust when the cockpit command and engine state diverge.
    

### Analytical importance

These cases do not by themselves prove that every event had the same cause. Runway excursions can have many initiating and contributing causes.
They do, however, support the transcript’s argument that the type developed a persistent pattern that was not eliminated by moving a checklist paragraph and waiting more than a decade for a warning box.

* * *

## Chapter 11 — Returning to Green Acres

The script deliberately returns to the unresolved 2026 accident and again cautions against transferring the Columbia mechanism into it.

### Reported aircraft and operator

The transcript describes the aircraft as a 2004 Learjet 60, approximately 22 years old, with a reported serial number of 271. It identifies:

*   Registered owner: LR6271 LLC, Wilmington, Delaware.
    
*   Operator: Aurora Aviation of Fort Lauderdale, trading as Upper Jets.
    
*   Certificate: Part 135.
    
*   Fleet: four aircraft, three of them Learjet 60s.
    

The FAA accident database categorizes the event under general aviation, while the flight was a charter. These labels can appear contradictory, but accident-database categories and operating certificates are different classifications; a Part 135 air-taxi operation may still appear within general-aviation accident records.

### Weather and flight profile

The transcript identifies weather as a major investigative focus:

*   Reported light clear ice.
    
*   Outside temperature quoted as approximately −47°C.
    
*   Moderate turbulence.
    
*   Altitude excursions of roughly 700 feet.
    
*   A possible entry into a rapidly developing convective cell.
    
*   Prior controller warnings about storms.
    

Some of those details—especially the temperature unit—would need verification against the official record. The transcript itself acknowledges that its causal claims remain preliminary.
The aircraft was reportedly cruising around Mach 0.76, with a maximum operating speed around Mach 0.81. That leaves a relatively narrow margin between too slow and too fast, and turbulence can move airspeed within that margin.
The crew requested a climb, and shortly afterward reported overspeed. Tracking data reportedly showed ground speed increasing by about 70 knots as the dive began.

### Why the elevator is the decisive clue

Because large portions of the elevator were recovered well before the impact site, investigators must determine whether it departed:

*   First, causing the pitch-control loss and dive.
    
*   Second, as a consequence of the dive.
    
*   Or as part of a broader sequence involving weather, icing, control-system failure, structural damage, or another initiating event.
    

The required evidence would include:

*   Fracture surfaces and attachment-point examination.
    
*   Structural-load calculations.
    
*   Maintenance and inspection history.
    
*   Flight-data recordings.
    
*   Control-surface position and pilot inputs.
    
*   Weather, wind, and icing data.
    
*   Radar and lightning information.
    
*   Wreckage trajectory reconstruction.
    
*   Any surviving cockpit recorder data.
    

### The public response

The script says that strangers diagnosed the first officer’s voice and personality, questioned her presence in the seat, invented a hiring narrative, and recast her extensive flight experience as evidence of tokenism.
It contrasts this with:

*   Her youth and prior experience.
    
*   The fact that she held the qualifications required for the position.
    
*   The normal charter practice of pairing a developing pilot with a senior captain.
    
*   Captain Gregory’s transport-pilot and flight-instructor credentials and charter experience.
    
*   The family’s statement of solidarity with both pilots’ families.
    

### Correct epistemic position

No one yet knows whether any human action contributed to the elevator separation or the initiation of the dive. It is possible that no action could have prevented the outcome once a failure sequence began. It is equally inappropriate to assume that human action was irrelevant until the data establishes that.

* * *

## Chapter 12 — The documentary’s synthesis

The script closes by drawing the two crashes together:

*   Both were Learjet 60 accidents.
    
*   Both initially became stories about the woman in the cockpit.
    
*   Both pilots died and could not rebut the narrative.
    
*   In Columbia, the deeper story involved aircraft design, ignored precedent, deficient training, certification practices, regulatory delay, and litigation strategy.
    
*   In Green Acres, the causal story remains unknown—but the speed of public blame already resembles the earlier pattern.
    

The script also explains the structural problem with accident reporting itself:

> Official findings must be compressed into a short probable-cause sentence. That sentence can contain several elements, but readers and media tend to retain only the most vivid one.

The truth does not need to be removed from the report; it only needs to be placed outside the first sentence.

* * *

# 3. The Most Accurate Technical Reconstruction of Columbia

The transcript’s Columbia story can be expressed as a layered chain:
text

Multiple tire failures during takeoff roll
↓
Wheel-well debris damages hydraulic lines and squat switch
↓

↓
Electronic reversers stow
↓
Engine computers enter forward-thrust schedule
↓
Pilot's aft reverse levers now command powerful forward thrust
↓
No cockpit indication; counterintuitive recovery action required
↓
Aircraft accelerates past the runway end
↓
Runway excursion and fatalities
The most important distinction is between **initiation** and **amplification**:
| Function | Factor |
| --- | --- |
| Initial hazard | Severely underinflated tires and multiple tire failures |
| Immediate pilot decision | Rejected takeoff after V1 |
| Mechanical amplification | Electronic reverser logic and damaged squat switch |
| Operational barrier failure | Maintenance oversight and prohibition on pilot tire checks |
| Training barrier failure | No practical takeoff-specific training for the scenario |
| Certification barrier failure | Derivative certification left a design integration gap |
| Learning-system failure | Alabama precedent not converted into binding design changes |
| Regulatory failure | Eleven-year delay before warning-system mandate |
| Narrative failure | Public attention concentrated on the individual decision |

This layered view does not make the pilot irrelevant. It assigns responsibility in proportion to causal influence.

* * *

# 4. The Deeper Argument About Accountability

A strong institutional critique can accidentally become another form of blame reduction. The transcript is strongest when it avoids that trap.
A fair assessment of Columbia requires acknowledging all of the following:

1.  **The tires were catastrophically underinflated.**
    
2.  **The captain’s post-V1 rejection was inconsistent with standard training and procedures.**
    
3.  **The aircraft suffered from a serious, previously known design vulnerability.**
    
4.  **The maintenance system and regulatory structure had allowed the vulnerability to persist.**
    
5.  **The cockpit did not reveal the mechanism that made recovery impossible or nearly impossible.**
    
6.  **The manufacturer knew a materially similar failure mode had caused an earlier accident.**
    
7.  **The FAA did not promptly require an engineering solution or effective crew training.**
    

A sound conclusion is therefore not:

> “No one is responsible.”

It is:

> **Responsibility must be distributed according to actual causal power and knowledge, not concentrated on the person who died holding the controls.**

* * *

# 5. What the Transcript Does Particularly Well

## A. It distinguishes the probable-cause sentence from the full investigation

The transcript’s central insight is strong: the most cited portion of an NTSB report is not the whole report. The systemic findings may be in the same document but outside the sentence repeated by the press.

## B. It explains causal mechanics rather than relying on moral outrage

The thrust-reverser analysis gives readers a concrete mechanism:

*   Squat-switch damage.
    
*   False airborne status.
    
*   Reverser stowage.
    
*   Forward-thrust schedule.
    
*   Unmoved cockpit levers.
    
*   Loss of braking.
    
*   No warning.
    

That is more useful than simply declaring the accident “the airline’s fault.”

## C. It identifies a genuine learning-system failure

The Alabama accident is especially important. It demonstrates that the 2008 event was not the first appearance of this failure mode. The relevant institutional question therefore shifts from “Could anyone have predicted it?” to “Why did prediction not produce correction?”

## D. It resists forcing the 2026 crash into the Columbia template

The script expressly states that Green Acres was not a runway crash and probably involved a different mechanism. That is an important safeguard against motivated reasoning.

## E. It identifies how social identity can displace causal analysis

The criticism of age, gender, ethnicity, and “DEI” as substitutes for evidence is legitimate. None of those characteristics establishes or excludes a causal contribution.

* * *

# 6. What Remains Uncertain or Requires Additional Verification

Several claims in the script should be read as narratorial assertions rather than established conclusions.

## The 2026 crash

The following are not yet supported by a final causal finding in the material provided:

*   Exact quotations from the cockpit recording.
    
*   Whether the recorder survived.
    
*   The precise weather conditions.
    
*   The role, if any, of ice accumulation.
    
*   Whether the convective cell was entered.
    
*   Whether the elevator departed first.
    
*   Whether overspeed was a cause, consequence, or symptom.
    
*   Whether a control-system, structural, or maintenance failure initiated the sequence.
    
*   Whether the dive created the aerodynamic loads that separated the elevator.
    
*   Whether the pilots could have influenced any stage of the sequence.
    

## Green Acres as evidence of another systemic failure

The repeat crash may indicate continued institutional weakness, but that is not the same as proving the 2026 cause. The script itself makes this distinction.

## The legal record

The manufacturer’s litigation position and confidential settlements demonstrate a defensive incentive. They do not, standing alone, prove that corporate decision-makers consciously concealed the design issue.

## Specific weather and incident details

The transcript’s quoted outside temperature, particular incident descriptions, and any information attributed to Wikipedia should be verified against primary documents.

## Citation of the full NTSB report

The central Columbia claim can be checked directly in the report concerning N999LJ. The most important sections would be:

*   The probable-cause finding.
    
*   The detailed findings about tire condition and maintenance.
    
*   The findings concerning the reverser and squat switch.
    
*   The contributing-factor language.
    
*   The safety recommendations.
    
*   Any appendices concerning simulator training and system safety analysis.
    

The 2001 Troy accident report should also be reviewed by aircraft registration and location, as should the FAA’s tire-pressure and reverser-warning directives.

* * *

# 7. The Conceptual Lessons

## Lesson 1: A pilot decision must be evaluated inside the aircraft’s actual behavior

Evaluating a decision only against a generic checklist assumes:

*   The equipment behaves as expected.
    
*   The cockpit accurately reflects the aircraft’s state.
    
*   The crew has adequate time and information.
    
*   Procedures address the actual failure mode.
    

The Columbia case challenges all four assumptions.

## Lesson 2: Procedures cannot substitute for safe design

Moving an emergency procedure to a more prominent manual section may improve awareness. It does not solve:

*   The absence of tactile feedback.
    
*   The absence of a warning.
    
*   The need to retrieve a checklist during a high-speed takeoff emergency.
    
*   The possibility of immediate reverse-thrust loss.
    

When foreseeable consequences are catastrophic, design changes are usually more robust than instructions.

## Lesson 3: A prior accident is a test of institutional learning

The Alabama crash should have produced more than a manual revision. A mature safety system asks:

*   Was the mechanism understood?
    
*   Was the affected fleet inspected?
    
*   Were affected crews trained?
    
*   Were simulators updated?
    
*   Was a hardware correction required?
    
*   Was another aircraft type checked for the same architecture?
    
*   Was the original recommendation closed with evidence?
    

The transcript argues that the system failed this test.

## Lesson 4: Recommendations without mandatory deadlines can become administrative delay

Urgent recommendations are important, but they are not equivalent to enforceable requirements. The eleven-year gap between the Columbia recommendations and the voice-warning mandate demonstrates the difference.

## Lesson 5: Early blame can narrow the investigation

Public and internal pressure to identify a responsible person can produce a premature “pilot error” explanation. That explanation may then:

*   Redirect resources away from design and organizational factors.
    
*   Lower the incentive for systemic recommendations.
    
*   Influence how findings are summarized.
    
*   Discourage other crews from reporting similar anomalies.
    
*   Turn a learning opportunity into a ritual of individual condemnation.
    

## Lesson 6: Accident reports serve two distinct functions

They must provide:

1.  A legally usable, sufficiently precise causal finding.
    
2.  A comprehensive account of contributing conditions and future prevention.
    

The first requirement necessarily compresses. The second is what prevents compression from becoming distortion.

* * *

# 8. What Should Matter Now

## Regarding Green Acres

1.  **Do not assign pilot fault without evidence.**  
    No final cause has been established. Age, gender, race, voice, and nervousness are not causal findings.
    
2.  **Determine the order of events.**  
    The decisive questions are what happened first, what happened next, and which factors were controllable.
    
3.  **Treat the elevator as a forensic object.**  
    Its fracture surfaces, fasteners, attachment structure, and debris pattern should be analyzed as carefully as the flight-data record.
    
4.  **Preserve all independent data.**  
    Weather observations, radar, lightning, satellite imagery, flight tracking, aircraft telemetry, maintenance records, and any surviving recorders should be reconciled.
    
5.  **Avoid borrowing Columbia’s mechanism.**  
    The proper lesson from 2008 is about inquiry and institutional learning—not that every Learjet 60 accident must have the same physical cause.
    

## Regarding the Learjet 60 fleet and similar aircraft

The transcript supports several concrete safety priorities:

*   Verify that reverser voice warnings are installed and functional wherever mandated.
    
*   Determine whether physical lever synchronization is feasible for the existing fleet.
    
*   Require realistic simulator training covering reverser stowage during rejected takeoff.
    
*   Revisit whether the original emergency procedure is recognizable and usable under the extreme time pressure of a takeoff emergency.
    
*   Ensure tire-pressure inspections are independently verifiable rather than merely documented as completed.
    
*   Examine other aircraft with similar electronic reverser and landing-gear sensing architectures.
    
*   Consider whether pilots should be permitted—and expected—to conduct basic tire-pressure checks.
    
*   Require meaningful recent type experience for charter captain duties where the type’s failure modes make that experience unusually important.
    
*   Use the Green Acres investigation to identify fleet-wide structural, control-surface, or attachment concerns even if its eventual cause proves unrelated to Columbia.
    

## Regarding accident reporting and public communication

*   Report the full probable-cause finding rather than only its most vivid clause.
    
*   Publish contributing factors prominently.
    
*   Separate safety findings from civil and criminal responsibility.
    
*   Avoid demographic speculation unless it is tied to a genuine, documented operational requirement.
    
*   Preserve the distinction between “we do not know” and “the crew caused it.”
    
*   Treat the dead crew’s inability to answer as a reason for greater investigative rigor, not lesser scrutiny.
    

* * *

# Post-Summary: What Is Most Important

1.  **The Green Acres cause is still unknown.**  
    The elevator’s separation is important, but it does not yet reveal whether it initiated the dive or resulted from it.
    
2.  **The twenty-year-old first officer must not be treated as the default explanation.**  
    There is no evidence in the supplied material that her age, voice, gender, or background contributed causally to the accident.
    
3.  **Columbia demonstrates why probable-cause headlines can mislead.**  
    The pilot’s post-V1 rejection was contrary to standard procedures, but the full report also identified an electronic reverser flaw that produced uncommanded forward thrust and increased the accident’s severity.
    
4.  **The most damning fact is prior knowledge.**  
    The same reverser/squat-switch failure had already injured two pilots in Alabama in 2001. The danger was documented before Columbia occurred.
    
5.  **The response to that prior accident was inadequate.**  
    A manual-procedure revision was not commensurate with a known catastrophic failure mode.
    
6.  **The regulatory timeline shows how recommendations can become delay.**  
    It took eleven years for a voice-warning mandate and allowed compliance to stretch as long as 2024.
    
7.  **Human error is often a product of system conditions.**  
    The useful question is not merely, “What did the pilot do?” but also:
    *   What information did the cockpit provide?
        
    *   What training did the crew receive?
        
    *   What failure modes were known?
        
    *   Who had the authority to correct the design?
        
    *   Why was the correction delayed?
        
8.  **Systemic analysis should not become blanket exoneration.**  
    Every layer of causation must be evaluated fairly. The crew’s actions, maintenance, design, certification, and regulation all belong in the analysis.
    
9.  **Read the complete report, not only the probable-cause sentence.**  
    The truth may be present in the same document as the incomplete headline.
    
10.  **The highest standard is institutional learning.**  
     A safety system is judged not merely by whether it can explain past accidents, but by whether known hazards are corrected before they kill again.
     

* * *

# Epilogue: What Matters Most

The deepest lesson of these two crashes is not simply that pilots should receive more sympathy, nor that aircraft designers are always responsible, nor that regulators are uniformly incompetent. It is that **a fair accident investigation must identify both the human decision and the conditions that made the decision consequential**.
Sarah Lemon’s rejection after V1 was wrong by the book. That should be stated without evasion. But the airplane then subjected her to a mechanism that converted the braking attempt into unpowered forward thrust, provided no warning, and made the necessary recovery action contrary to instinct. Those were not incidental details. They were part of the causal story.
Likewise, Nathalia Filgeras cannot fairly be made to carry the burden of explaining an airplane that may have failed structurally at altitude. Until investigators establish the sequence, the honest position is uncertainty—not praise, condemnation, or narrative convenience.
The larger responsibility belongs to institutions capable of seeing the pattern:

*   The manufacturer that knew a similar failure mode had already caused an accident.
    
*   The regulator that knew the documented hazard but converted urgency into an eleven-year delay.
    
*   The operators and aviation authorities that treated certification minimums and procedural compliance as sufficient protection.
    
*   The media and public that repeatedly chose a simple human culprit because a complex systems explanation required more work.
    

Those who die in aircraft crashes cannot correct the record. That is precisely why the institutions that survive them must.
**What matters most is not whether we can identify the person who was present at the controls. It is whether we can identify the conditions that made their presence insufficient—and whether, this time, we act before another preventable death reduces those conditions to another probable-cause sentence.**
For Green Acres, the proper response to uncertainty is disciplined patience. We do not yet know whether the failure originated in weather, structure, control, maintenance, or some interaction among them. We do know that blaming the youngest and most visible person at the controls would not answer the question, correct the risk, or honor the people who died.
The most important lesson is therefore both simple and demanding:

> **Investigate the whole system, preserve the truth beyond the headline, and never confuse an understandable story with a complete explanation.**

**Three things this nails down:**

| Finding | Consequence |
|---|---|

## 📊 4. THE BAND AT THE ALTITUDE THEY WERE ACTUALLY AT

Let me run it at the real top‑of‑climb altitude (35,250 ft PA, OAT −47 °C):

| | FL350 (cruise) | **FL35,250 (actual top of climb)** |
|---|---|---|
| a·√σ | 278.6 kt | **277.0 kt** |
| Mstall clean (185 KIAS) | M0.664 | **M0.668** |
| **Mstall +10% ice (203.5 KIAS)** | M0.731 | **M0.735** |
| Aircraft at M0.76 → IAS | 211.7 KIAS | **210.5 KIAS** |
| **Stall margin above the +10% icing stall** | +8.2 kt | **+7.0 kt** |

| **10/10 policy window** | **2.2 kt** | **0.9 kt** |
| q at M0.76 | 201 psf | 199 psf |

> ### The corrected, sharpened conclusion:
> **The aircraft never got near FL370. It was at 35,250 ft in the core of a 25–35 dBZ cell, with roughly 7 knots of margin above an ice‑raised stall speed, and a policy window about one knot wide — and then it departed controlled flight.**
>
> **The clean‑air geometric band at that altitude was still ~0.14 Mach / 40 kt wide.** The geometry was *not* the trap. **The icing was.** The margin you can't see is not a margin.

# 🏛️ 6. THE COLUMBIA MATERIAL — THIS IS EXCELLENT, AND IT'S DIRECTLY TEACHABLE

Your summary's Columbia reconstruction is **accurate and well‑structured**, and it gives you the best available framework for the N722BD case. Two things I'd add.

### A. The layering is the transferable product

Use this exact scaffold for **both** accidents:

| Layer | Columbia (2008) | **N722BD (2025)** |
|---|---|---|
| **Initial hazard** | Tires at ~140 psi vs 219 spec; multiple failures | Ice on an iced airframe at 35,250 ft in a 25–35 dBZ core |
| **Immediate decision** | RTO after V1 (wrong by the book) | Climb request + entry into the core (under investigation) |
| **Amplification** | Squat switch → reversers stow → **uncommanded forward thrust**, no cockpit indication | ? — possibly Mach‑tuck / elevator effectiveness loss / tailplane stall |
| **Survivability barrier** | No warning, no tactile feedback, counterintuitive recovery | Elevator separated (FL14,000–27,000 band); Chapman: "I don't know that it would have been effective even if attached" |
| **Organisational barriers** | Maintenance oversight; pilots barred from tyre checks; no takeoff‑specific training | ? — maintenance/inspection history; May 2024 severe‑turbulence inspection |
| **Regulatory** | 11 years to a voice warning; compliance to 2024 | ? |
| **Narrative failure** | "Panicking young captain" | "The 20‑year‑old copilot" |

**The discipline this teaches:** **do not use "systems failure" as a universal solvent.** The Columbia case did not make Lemon irrelevant; it made the *book* insufficient. The same holds here: the crew's decisions are **legitimately under investigation**, and so is the aircraft's condition and the weather. Both belong in the analysis.

### B. The **counterfactual test** — the single best question you can teach

> **"Would a different decision have changed the outcome — and was that decision available to the crew with the information they had?"**

For Columbia: if the aircraft had remained controllable and the takeoff had continued, a rough landing might have injured people — but the count would likely not have been four. The RTO was wrong; the *aircraft's response* made it fatal.
For N722BD: if the crew had descended out of the cell instead of climbing 250 ft into it, would the aircraft have remained controllable? **We cannot know — but the question is answerable from the data, and it is the right question.** Not "should they have known" (unanswerable, and it smuggles in hindsight), but "given what they knew at 17:47:51, what were the survivable options?"

### C. The Columbia‑derived questions for N722BD — *borrow the questions, not the mechanism*

Your document rightly says the physical mechanism probably doesn't recur. **But these Columbia‑style questions apply directly:**

| # | Question | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | Has this airframe (or the type) had **prior control‑surface separation, empennage flutter, or elevator/trim/actuator failures**? | The LR60's **T‑tail** and empennage attachment have their own history. Your incident list (Valencia 2013, Chicago 2020, Boston 2023, April 2026) should be screened specifically for **control‑surface and empennage** events, not just runway excursions. |
| **2** | Any **AD or SB** on the elevator attach fittings, hinge, trim actuator, or empennage for that serial? | The NTSB will run an AD/SIB search by serial number. So should your students. |
| **3** | **MEL status of the flight controls** at the time of the flight? | A deferred item in the control system is a first-order question, not a footnote. |
| **4** | What did the **May 2024 severe‑turbulence encounter** require, and was it done? | The AMM special inspection after severe turbulence. This is the single most concrete "systems" thread in the case. |
| **5** | What did the **April 2026** brake‑related event require? | A second recent prior‑event data point. |
| **6** | Is there a **known failure mode of the LR60 empennage at high Mach** that the certification basis didn't address? | This is the Columbia question, correctly transferred: *did the certification basis examine the failure mode the way it should have?* |

**That last one is the real Columbia lesson.** Columbia wasn't "a pilot error." It was **a certification basis that never asked what happens if the wheel well is destroyed while the aircraft is still on the ground.** For N722BD the parallel question is: **did anyone ever examine what happens to the LR60's empennage and elevator attachment at high Mach in icing, at the aft CG, at maximum landing weight?** If the answer is no — *that's* the finding.

---

## ⚖️ 7. ON THE BLAME QUESTION

Your document's core epistemic position is **right and worth keeping:**

> *"Do not assign pilot fault without evidence."*
> *"The proper response to uncertainty is disciplined patience."*
> *"The truth does not need to be removed from the report; it only needs to be placed outside the first sentence."*

**That last line is the best sentence in the entire summary.** Put it on the wall of the classroom.

**The nuance to add — and it's the one the document itself half‑makes:** *"no evidence yet" ≠ *"no human contribution."* The N722BD investigation is currently examining:
- a climb request made as precipitation was encountered,
- entry into a 25–35 dBZ core at FL350,
- a report of ice as "manageable,"
- a 250‑ft climb immediately before the departure.

Those are **decisions under investigation**, not convictions. **The correct posture is neither "they caused it" nor "the system caused it."** It's: *what did the aircraft do, what did the environment do, what did the crew know, and in what order?* — which is exactly what your summary says the investigation must do, and exactly what the 2025 preliminary/performance reports will answer.

**And the system lesson generalises beyond aviation:** premature blame *is* a safety hazard, because it redirects investigation resources, suppresses reporting of the anomalies that precede accidents, and produces the one‑line summary that stops the learning.
