 43,000   497.9    573.0   -56.5     0.2131    0.4616        229.8  │
 45,000   497.9    573.0   -56.5     0.1936    0.4400        219.1  ┘
 ⚠ UNIT TRAP: a at FL350 = 500.5 kt = 576.2 mph. The MPH figure is easily
   mistaken for a KNOT figure. Earlier drafts of this card made that error.
   And ABOVE FL360 the value is 497.9 kt at EVERY level.
 BACK 2 — BAND TABLE [I] (Vs 185 KIAS; ceiling M0.78 above FL370, M0.81 below)
 Alt    Mstall   Ceiling   Raw band   10/10 window
 FL350   0.664    M0.81     40.7 kt    195.0 – 225.7 KIAS  ✅
 FL370   0.697    M0.78     22.0 kt    195.0 – 197.0 KIAS  ⚠ 2.0 kt
 FL390   0.731    M0.78     12.3 kt    CLOSED
 FL410   0.767    M0.78      3.1 kt    CLOSED
 FL430   0.805    M0.78     CLOSED     CLOSED       (closes ≈ FL415)
 BACK 3 — OVERSPEED AND q: TRACK q, NOT MACH
   q = ½ρV²   q_ratio = (ρ/ρ_ref)·(V/V_ref)²   [ref: FL350, M0.76, ISA, 201 psf]
   In a descent the air THICKENS and true airspeed RISES.
   Mach can FALL while q CLIMBS. Above FL360, a is constant — so in the
   isothermal layer Mach is your only compressibility cue and q your only
   load cue. USE BOTH.
   N722BD dive (corrected from raw ADS-B, PA series):
     17:46      201 psf  (1.00×)  cruise
     17:48:07   199 psf (0.99×)  top of climb, 35,250 ft, M0.76
     17:48:39   439 psf (2.18×)  24,716 ft, M0.92   ← peak Mach, clean data
     17:49:16   573 psf (2.85×)  13,534 ft, M~0.82
     17:49:23   585 psf (2.91×)  8,750 ft  ← LAST RECORD INTERNALLY
   ⚠ THE VERTICAL-RATE FIELD SATURATED at 32,640 fpm. TRUE RATES:
       17:48:07→:39   7,313 fpm      17:48:39→:58  20,950 fpm
       17:48:58→:49:16 37,273 fpm    17:49:16→:23  41,006 fpm
     A SMOOTH, MONOTONIC ACCELERATION — no step change visible at public
     data resolution. ONE CURVE IN THE FDR (vertical acceleration, roll rate)
     WOULD DISCRIMINATE "UPTEST FIRST" FROM "SURFACE FAILURE FIRST".
     ⚠ BOTH VIDEOS' "4× CRUISE" OVERSTATED THE REAL PEAK BY ~45%.
 BACK 4 — FLUTTER: WHY THE TAIL GOES FIRST, AND WHAT V_D MEANS
   Flutter = a self-excited, self-sustained oscillation. A control surface
   or hinge fails because it is SLENDER, LOW MASS, HIGHLY LOADED and riding
   disturbed airflow. Above M0.80 Mach tuck reduces elevator EFFECTIVENESS
   — so at M0.9 the tail has little authority even if attached.
   (NTSB on this case: "I don't know that it would have been effective even
    if it had still been attached to the aircraft.")
   V_D = the DESIGN DIVE SPEED used to certify the flutter envelope.
     NOT PUBLISHED for the LR60. You never fly it.
     MMO is set FAR BELOW V_D precisely so the flutter envelope is never
     approached in service. The gap between MMO and V_D is the margin you
     spend the instant you exceed MMO.
     Part 25 transports cite 25.673(d) / AC 25.673-1 and 1.15×V_D.
     The LR60 is Part 23 (legacy) / Part 21 — DIFFERENT BASIS. Its own AFM
     is the only authority. ⚠ VERIFY THE CITATION; CHECK 25.629 BEFORE USE.
 BACK 5 — SEVERE TURBULENCE IS A MAINTENANCE EVENT
   "UNABLE TO HOLD ALTITUDE" = SEVERE TURBULENCE. Report it THAT way.
   Business-jet AMM/MMG Special Inspections require an inspection after a
   severe-turbulence encounter — often a HARD STOP before further flight.
   TRIGGERS: unable to hold altitude · sustained >2,000 ft excursions ·
   crew forced to brace · any control-surface/actuator/hinge anomaly ·
   overspeed or buffet event · lightning strike.
   ► N722BD had a MAY 2024 severe-turbulence encounter. Did the
     inspection happen? THAT IS THE ONE CONCRETE SYSTEMIC THREAD.
 BACK 6 — EVIDENCE DISCIPLINE (every claim gets a label)
   [MEASURED] an instrument recorded it   [REPORTED] a human said it
   [MODELLED] calculated, method stated   [DRAWN] an illustrator drew it
   [UNKNOWN] the investigation has not answered it
   LADDER (highest first): NTSB findings → FDR/CVR → wreckage → raw ATC
   audio → weather/radar → flight tracking → EDITORIAL RECONSTRUCTIONS →
   social media.  TEACH measured + reported. TEACH modelled as hypothesis.
   NEVER teach drawn as fact.
 BACK 7 — WHAT TO DO WHEN YOU ARE ALREADY IN IT
   ICING IN CLOUD      Descend DECISIVELY, all the way OUT, below the
                      freezing level. Never level off inside it.
   ICED WING STALL     Nose up, max thrust, wings level, ACCEPT the
                      altitude loss. Expect NO buffet warning.
   TAILPLANE STALL    NOSE DOWN — reduce AoA. Pulling makes it worse.
   SPIRAL DIVE         Roll toward level WHILE bringing the nose to the
                      horizon. DO NOT pull to stop the descent.
   ELEVATOR GONE       Assume little pitch authority. Control roll, thrust
                      and flight path. Level — do not spiral.
   ANY OF THE ABOVE    DECLARE EARLY. A stabilized descent beats a fast
                      spiral, and you get radar sooner.
 BACK 8 — THE OPERATIONAL RULES (the short version)
   1 NEVER ENTER A THUNDERSTORM. 10 NM any / 20 NM severe / 20 NM IFR.
   2 THE CORNER IS A MARGIN, NOT AN ALTITUDE. Never let an altitude
     reassure you; only the numbers do.
   3 DESCEND TO GET OUT, NOT LOWER. If the cell is between you and the
     ground, go around.
   4 THE MARGIN IS A COUNTDOWN. Watch the IAS trend in the climb: at
     constant Mach it falls ~5.7 kt/1,000 ft. If it falls FASTER, ice is
     eating your margin.
   5 ICE MOVES THE FLOOR. ONLY ALTITUDE MOVES IT BACK.
   6 "WE'RE MANAGING THE ICE" IS NOT A STATUS REPORT. It is the phrase
     that preceded a margin going to zero.
   7 SEVERE TURBULENCE IS A MAINTENANCE EVENT.
   8 THE CREW DOES NOT REPRESENT THE AIRCRAFT'S STATE. Fly the numbers.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
 N722BD — THE MARGIN YOU COULDN'T SEE     |     16 Feb 2025, Palm Beach Co. FL
 Learjet 60 · 9 fatalities · left elevator separated before impact
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
 1  WHAT THE CORNER REALLY IS
    A DISTANCE between the stall speed and the overspeed limit — not a place.
    It closes at a different altitude for every aircraft, weight and config.
    This aircraft was lost at 35,250 ft — BELOW where the LR60's band closes.
    The clean-air band there was 40.7 kt wide. The ICE was the trap.

    THE MARGIN WAS 7 KNOTS. AND IT WAS CLOSING.

                 19 Sep 2008           16 Feb 2025

    aircraft      "airborne"            could no longer carry

      Neither was caused by not knowing the procedures.
      A probable cause is ONE SENTENCE — the compression is in the

    10 NM any · 20 NM severe · 20 NM IFR

      at FL350 this cell measured only 25–35 dBZ, while its 55–60 dBZ
      core was 10,000 FT BELOW the aircraft. The upper flank of a cell
      can look mild where you are and be lethal.
    MEASURE FROM THE VIOLENT PART. THAT IS A FLOOR, NOT A CEILING.

 4  WHAT TO RECOGNISE (before the upset)
    ▸ In cloud with ice: your stall speed is RISING and nothing shows it.
    ▸ At constant Mach, IAS falls ~5.7 kt per 1,000 ft in a climb.
      IF IT FALLS FASTER, ICE IS EATING YOUR MARGIN.
    ▸ Same thrust, less climb than the book = ice.
    ▸ "We can see the cell but it looks mild at our level" = it can still
      kill you. That is exactly what happened here.
    ▸ "OVERSPEED" is a SYMPTOM of the dive, not the cause. Slowing down
      by pulling tightens the spiral.

 5  EVIDENCE DISCIPLINE
    [MEASURED] an instrument recorded it  [REPORTED] a human said it

    /radar → flight tracking → EDITORIAL RECONSTRUCTIONS → speculation
    TEACH measured + reported. MODELLED as hypothesis. NEVER drawn as fact.

 6  THE RULES
    1 Never enter a thunderstorm. 10/20 NM. 20 NM IFR.
    2 The corner is a margin, not an altitude.
    3 Descend to get OUT, not lower.
    4 The margin is a countdown — watch the IAS trend.
    5 Ice moves the floor; only altitude moves it back.
    6 Severe turbulence is a maintenance event.
    7 The crew does not represent the aircraft's state. Fly the numbers.

# PART D — INSTRUCTOR EXERCISE

## **“WHAT COULD THEY HAVE SEEN?” — building the N722BD timeline from the raw cockpit audio**

**Format:** 75 minutes • pairs as PIC/FOP • **materials:** raw ATC audio + transcript (below), the ADS‑B table, the cockpit card v1.1, a copy of the raw recording if you can obtain it (the ATC recording is public — **play it, don't describe it**)

**Framing to state aloud, and hold yourself to:** *we are not grading two dead people. We are grading what the cockpit and the airspace were offering, and what a trained crew should have recognised. Some of these questions have no answer yet — that is part of the exercise.*

---

### Phase 0 — Setup (5 min)

Hand out the **Transcript Extract** and the **ADS‑B Extract**. **No analysis yet.** Tell them:

> *You will not be told what caused this. You will not be told whether the crew did the right thing. You will be asked what was knowable, and when.*

---

### Phase 1 — “What was knowable?” (15 min)

**Q1.1** At **17:46** (FL350, level), the crew had reported ice and turbulence. List **everything** the aircraft and the airspace were offering the crew at that moment — separately:
 (a) what the **cockpit** could show; (b) what **radar** could show; (c) what the crew could **infer** from the aircraft's behaviour.
 *(One answer per line, no discussion yet.)*

**Q1.2** At **17:47:51** the aircraft's tracked position overlaps a region of precipitation. Using only what a pilot can see on NEXRAD, **what would the radar have looked like at FL350?**
 *(Hint: consider what the beam was actually sampling. You are 100 NM from KAMX. What is the beam elevation angle at that range, and what is the vertical resolution of that beam?)*

**Q1.3** At **17:47:xx**, the crew transmits: ***“Picking up precipitation. We need to climb 370.”*** The freezing level is **below** the aircraft. **What are the two possible meanings of that request — and which is the safe one?**

**Q1.4** At **17:48:07** the aircraft tops the climb at **35,250 ft** and is **inside** the cell. Give **four independent indications that the aircraft was degraded by ice**, available to the crew without any special equipment.
 *(Hint: one is a number on the speed indicator, one is on the engine indications, one is on the FPM, one is a trend you can calculate.)*

---

### Phase 2 — “What should have been recognised?” (20 min) — the core of the exercise

**Q2.1** Using the card's FL35,250 table, and assuming the aircraft was at M0.76:
 (a) What was the stall margin at +10% ice accretion?
 (b) At what ice accretion does the margin reach zero?
 (c) At what ice accretion does the 10/10 policy window close?
 (d) The ice was still accreting. **What did that make the margin?**

**Q2.2** The margin was **7 knots**. The aircraft’s geometric speed band was **40.7 knots wide**.
 **Which of these two numbers would a pilot be able to see on the panel?**
 **Which one actually determined whether the aircraft survived?**
 **What does that tell you about which number you must be taught to compute?**

**Q2.3** At **17:48:28** the aircraft departs controlled flight. The crew had reported **“moderate turbulence.”**
 (a) Under the FAA’s multi‑criteria turbulence reporting standard, **what severity is appropriate** for a 700‑ft altitude excursion? Use the **aircraft‑performance** criterion, not the passenger‑comfort criterion.
 (b) What does the crew’s use of “moderate” tell you about the severity culture in that airline — and what happened on the same aircraft in May 2024?

**Q2.4** At **17:48:39** the crew transmits: ***“We’re in an overspeed condition.”***
 (a) At that moment, what was the aircraft’s actual problem?
 (b) **What are the two likely human reactions to that call, and which one is fatal?**
 *(One reaction is “slow down.”)*

**Q2.5** The crew transmits: ***“We’re banking 60 degrees.”*** They are in a spiral.
 (a) State the correct recovery priority.
 (b) Why is the instinctive response — pull back — fatal here?
 (c) At the speeds reached, what was the **remaining pitch‑control authority** likely to be, and why?

**Q2.6** The final transmission is: ***“We’re going to crash into the ground.”*** Earlier in the recording the crew also said ***“Nobody wants to hear from a cockpit.”***
 **What can you legitimately infer from that phrase, and what can you NOT?**
 *(This is the evidence‑discipline question in its purest form.)*

---

### Phase 3 — “What would have followed?” (10 min)

For each of the three decision points below, state the **most likely outcome** of the alternative action, and the **probability of survival** you would assign and why.

| Decision point | Action taken | Alternative to consider |
|---|---|---|
| **17:46** FL350, ice reported | continued level flight in cloud | descend out of the cloud below the freezing level |
| **17:47:xx** | request climb to FL370 | request a lower level or a lateral deviation |
| **17:48:07** | top of climb inside the cell | continue to descend through the cloud |

**Then answer the hardest question:** **In which of these three was the “right” answer actually available to the crew with the information they had?** Be precise about what they knew and when.

---

### Phase 4 — Systems layer (15 min)

**Q4.1** Put the two accidents side by side: Columbia N999LJ (19 Sep 2008) and Palm Beach N722BD (16 Feb 2025). Both Learjet 60.
**State the common failure archetype in one sentence.** Then answer: *is the mechanism the same?* *(It is not — say why.)*

**Q4.2** Columbia had a **prior accident (Troy, Alabama, 14 Jan 2001)** with the identical mechanism, which injured two pilots and was formally investigated.
 **What is the Palm Beach equivalent of that prior event?** List every candidate you can find, and say which one you would chase first and how you would verify it.

**Q4.3** Columbia’s NTSB finding was effectively: *the certification basis never asked what happens if the wheel well is destroyed on the ground.*
 **Write the Palm Beach version of that question.**

**Q4.4** The NTSB will issue a one‑sentence probable cause.
 **Write the sentence you expect.** Then write the **paragraph that must accompany it** — and explain why the public will only quote the sentence.

---

### Phase 5 — Close (10 min)

Each student writes, on one card:

1. **One cockpit rule** they will adopt from this case, and the specific trigger that makes it fire.
2. **One company or system change** they would advocate, and which layer of the organisation it sits in (design / certification / maintenance / training / regulation / narrative).
3. **One claim they will no longer make** without a labelled source.

---

## ANSWER KEY

### Phase 1

**Q1.1**
 (a) **Cockpit:** IAS/Mach (and the IAS *trend* in the climb), FPM climb rate, thrust indication (N1/EGT), fuel flow, any SEP display, ice/windshield indication, the **air data system's own behaviour** (e.g. IAS not tracking commanded Mach, AoA if fitted). **No stall‑speed indicator exists. No buffet may ever occur.**
 (b) **Radar:** the cell's position, its **upper flank** reflectivity at the aircraft's level, the core *below*, and the **distance from the most intense part** — not from the nearest echo. Plus any cell growth trend.
 (c) **Aircraft behaviour:** less climb at the same thrust, more thrust needed for the same speed, a change in IAS trend, any control‑feel change (roll or pitch trim).

**Q1.2** At 100 NM and 35,000 ft, the KAMX beam elevation is **≈3.3°** (≈3.5° with the 4/3‑earth correction), giving **≈10,000 ft of vertical resolution** — about 1,000 ft per 100 ft of aircraft altitude change. **The aircraft's flight level was NOT resolved by that beam.** The value labelled “FL350” is a ~10,000‑ft‑deep slab average. **The 25–35 dBZ figure is real but the altitude attribution is coarse** — and the 55–60 dBZ core was 10,000 ft *below*.

**Q1.3** Two meanings: **(i) “we are in precipitation and want to climb above it”** (the benign reading) and **(ii) “we are in the cell’s lower/central region and are trying to get above the bad part.”** **The safe interpretation is the one that requires the least action** — and neither of those readings is a request to get out of the weather. The **safe** action for a crew already in precipitation and icing is to **descend out of the cloud layer**, not climb. **The freezing level was below them, so “up” was the wrong direction for the hazard they were reporting.** *Accept: “the request reveals the crew believed up was the escape. That belief is the error.”*

**Q1.4** Four independent cues:
1. **IAS trend in the climb** — at constant Mach, IAS falls ~5.7 kt/1,000 ft. **Faster fall = ice.**
2. **Thrust required for the same climb** (N1/EGT/fuel flow rising at constant IAS).
3. **Climb rate below the book** for the thrust and speed flown.
4. **SEP (specific excess power)** falling with no speed change, where fitted.
 Plus: any **ice/windshield indication**, and the **absence of buffet** (which is *not* reassuring in ice).

### Phase 2

**Q2.1** (a) **+7.0 kt.** (b) **≈13.2% ice accretion.** (c) **≈10.5% ice accretion.** (d) **A countdown, not a constant.** The margin was being spent continuously while the aircraft remained in the precipitation; the model it was flying did not change with it.

**Q2.2** **Visible on the panel:** neither, directly. **You can see** the *speed you are flying* and the *limits* — but you cannot see the *stall speed you have now*. **Determinative:** the **7‑knot margin** (the distance between the iced stall and you), because the **40.7‑kt geometric band was irrelevant** — the aircraft was lost inside it. **Teaching point: the number that matters is the one with no instrument. So it must be computed before you need it, not observed when you need it.**

**Q2.3** (a) **Severe.** The altitude‑excursion criterion alone (600–700 ft) suggests light‑to‑moderate; but the **aircraft‑performance criterion is decisive**, and “unable to hold altitude” is the definition of **severe turbulence**. (b) The May 2024 encounter — the controller had to make the crew **say “severe”** — and it required an **AMM special inspection**. **A company whose crews under‑call severity twice has a reporting culture problem, not two crew problems.** *Note for the instructor: this is where the systems thread opens. Do not let the class turn it into a character judgment about the FO.*

**Q2.4** (a) The actual problem was **loss of control / a developing spiral**; the “overspeed condition” was the **consequence** of the nose‑down attitude and the dive. (b) Two likely reactions: **(i) “pull to slow down”** — which in a 60° bank **adds load, tightens the spiral, and accelerates the descent** (and is the fatal one); **(ii) roll toward level and bring the nose to the horizon while managing speed with thrust** — the correct one (AC 120‑111; NTSB *Airplane Upset Management*). **A crew that treats “overspeed” as the problem to be fixed will pull, and pulling is what killed them.**

**Q2.5** (a) **Roll toward level while bringing the nose to the horizon. Do not pull to arrest the descent. Manage speed with thrust.** (b) Pulling in a bank **increases load factor, tightens the spiral, and raises the descent rate** — the opposite of the intent. (c) **Very little.** At ~M0.9 the elevator’s effectiveness is degraded by compressibility (Mach tuck), by ice on the tail, and by q‑induced hinge loads; and the left elevator had already separated. The NTSB’s own words: *“I don’t know that it would have been effective even if it had still been attached.”*

**Q2.6** **Legitimate inference:** a crew under extreme workload, in a deteriorating situation, aware it was unlikely to recover. **NOT legitimate:** that they were panicking, incompetent, or that the phrase indicates anything about cause. **This is the compression trap in its purest form** — a human utterance is being converted into a causal claim. *Instructor note: this is the moment to introduce the four labels and say that “we’re going to crash into the ground” is a [REPORTED] statement about the crew’s belief, and a [UNKNOWN] statement about the cause.*

### Phase 3

| Decision point | Most likely outcome of the alternative |
|---|---|
| **Descend out of the cloud at 17:46** | Leaving the icing removes the margin decay; the aircraft leaves the cell with a clean stall speed and a wide band. **Highest probability of survival.** |
| **Request lower / deviation at 17:47** | ATC can grant it; it avoids the core entry at 17:47:51. Survives the sequence. **Survivable — provided the crew could get the clearance and descend before 17:48:28.** |
| **Descend through the cloud at 17:48:07** | Depends on where the cell sat: below them was the 55–60 dBZ core. **Descending toward the core is more dangerous, not less.** *The important teaching point: “descend” is only correct if it takes you OUT — not if it takes you deeper into the precipitation.* |
| **The hard answer** | **The climb request was the last clearly recoverable point.** After ~17:48:07 the crew was in the core with a closing margin, and at ~17:48:28 they were out of control. Whether the 17:46 decision was *available* depends on information the investigation has not yet released — the radar picture, the cell’s growth, and whether the crew had a working weather radar. **Say that plainly. It is the honest answer and it models good reasoning.** |

### Phase 4

**Q4.1** **Common archetype:** *In both accidents the aircraft behaved exactly as its own inputs dictated, while the cockpit gave the crew no indication of those inputs — so a reasonable pilot action was converted into a fatal one.* **Mechanisms differ:** Columbia = a damaged sensor producing a false state consumed by flight‑control logic; Palm Beach = altered aerodynamic coefficients consumed by the wing and the tail. **One is a control‑system failure; the other is an aerodynamic one. The human‑facing failure is identical.**

**Q4.2** Candidates: prior LR60 **empennage / elevator / control‑surface / flutter** events (screen the incident record for these, not just runway excursions); any **AD or SB** on the elevator attach fittings, hinge, trim actuator or empennage; the **MEL status of the flight controls**; the **May 2024 severe‑turbulence inspection**; the **April 2026 brake/related event**; any **lightning‑strike or static‑discharge** history.
 **Chase first:** the **AD/SIB search by serial number** plus the **May 2024 inspection record** — because both are primary records that exist, are retrievable, and have a definite yes/no answer.
 **How to verify:** CAROL for the case documents; the FAA’s AD/SIB database by serial; the operator’s maintenance records via the NTSB’s Maintenance Report.

**Q4.3** *“Did anyone ever examine what happens to the Learjet 60’s empennage and elevator attachment, at high Mach, with ice on the airfoil, at aft CG, at maximum landing weight, in a steep bank — and did the certification basis require that examination before type certification and before continued certification of the fleet?”*

**Q4.4** **Expected sentence (students will get close):** *The crew’s decision to continue flight in icing and to climb into a convective cell…* **The paragraph that must accompany it:** the icing‑related reduction in stall speed and the unobservable margin; the core entry; the loss of control in the cell; the overspeed spiral; the elevator separation and its sequence; the maintenance and inspection history; the type’s incident record; and the certification‑basis question above. **Why the public will only quote the sentence:** because it is the only part that fits a headline, a chart, or a 15‑second video — and because **the compression is built into the product, not the newsroom.**

### Phase 5 — Evaluation

| Response | Meets the objective |
|---|---|
| A cockpit rule with a **specific trigger** (not a platitude) | ✔ |
| A systemic change placed in the **correct layer** (design / certification / maintenance / training / regulation / narrative) | ✔ |
| A **specific claim abandoned** because it lacked a labelled source | ✔ |
| Any answer that attributes cause to an individual’s age, voice, gender, or manner | ✘ — discuss, do not accept |

---

# POST‑SUMMARY

**1. The NTSB record could not be retrieved** — no attached documents, no knowledge bases, no web access here. **But the date is settled from evidence already in this thread: 16 February 2025.** The “Nov 1, 2026” in the downstream documents is an error, and it traces back to the Airliner Archive sounding graphic labelled “Nov. 1” — **which means the debris‑drift model may be running on the wrong wind field.** That is a checkable error worth making.

**2. The cockpit card is rebuilt around what you asked for:** the two‑accident comparison as the spine, the coffin corner explained **through stall margin** (not as a fixed 30‑second window), the **FL35,250 case** worked end to end, and the **8 vs 20 mile** comparison with the decisive arithmetic — **8 miles at 450 kt is 64 seconds of warning; 20 miles is 160 seconds** — plus the N722BD‑specific reason a blanket 8‑mile rule fails: **at FL350 the cell measured only 25–35 dBZ while the violent core was 10,000 feet below.**

**3. The most important number on the card is now the ice table:** margin **+7.0 kt at 10% accretion, ZERO at 13.2%, −2.3 kt at 15%.** And the policy window closed at **10.5%** — probably before they ever topped out. **The margin was a countdown, and the geometric band was 40.7 kt wide. They died inside a wide window.**

**4. The instructor exercise is built on the raw audio** and asks, at every transmission, three separated questions: **what was knowable, what the aircraft was indicating, and what could not have been known.** The centrepiece is **Q2.4**: *“We’re in an overspeed condition”* — the overspeed was a **symptom**, and the likely reaction to it (pulling to slow down) is **fatal in a bank.** That single question teaches more than any amount of theory.

**5. Two questions are deliberately unanswerable**, and that is the point: *which decision was actually available with the information they had*, and *what would the probable‑cause sentence be versus the paragraph that must accompany it.* Students learn that some questions close with the NTSB and some close with judgement.

**6. One thing I did not do, and should flag:** I did not put the crew’s names or the victims’ names on any artefact. **The NTSB had not released the crew’s names**, the secondary sources disagree on the spelling, and the victims are nine people including two small children. If you want those on a teaching document, they belong there only with a citation to the NTSB record, and the families should have been asked.

**7. Next:** drop the confirmed CAROL details into the card’s header (case number, the two altitude figures, the crew names if released), and I’ll re‑issue it as v1.2 with those fields populated — and I can re‑key the exercise’s answer key to the NTSB’s actual wording once the Performance and Structures reports land.

**8. And the one sentence to keep, whatever happens with the investigation:**
> ### **The corner is a margin, not an altitude. Ice moved the floor. And in this accident the margin went to zero faster than anyone could see.**

### ASSISTANT
# Three New Facts — and One of Them Changes the Case, One Changes My Numbers, and One Is a Contradiction the Investigation Must Resolve

This broadcast is valuable. It adds a **recorded theory from a working airline captain**, it gives us **a new final transmission**, and it makes a **claim about the recorders** that contradicts earlier reporting.

---

## 1. ✅ "THEY DO HAVE THE BLACK BOXES NOW RECOVERED"

**This is the biggest single fact in the broadcast — and the one most likely to be wrong.**

- The anchor states flatly: *"Amazingly, they do have the black boxes now recovered."*
- The Airliner Archive reporting said the **CVR had not been recovered** and that records showed one installed but it was unconfirmed aboard.
- The expert says *"the cockpit voice recorder and the flight data recorder just give the investigators unbelievable multitudes of information"* and predicts they will hear *"our anti-icing equipment is on."*
- The broadcast itself plays **cockpit audio** (ABC declined to air the worst of it) and quotes **"We're out of time."**

> ### Three possibilities, and they are not equivalent:
> | | Implication |
> |---|---|
> | **Both recorders recovered and intact** | The case becomes **decidable**. AoA, roll rate, pitch rate, control positions, throttle/anti-ice switches, N1, OAT, and the entire radio track — all available. Every hypothesis below gets tested. |
> | **Only the FDR recovered** (the CVR is small, often lost) | Most of the *physical* questions get answered; the crew's decision sequence does not. |
> | **The anchor is simply wrong** (or means "they have the FDR") | Nothing changes, and the public has been told something false. |
>
> **This is now the highest‑priority item to verify.** It is a single fact that determines whether the next six months of analysis is speculative or forensic.

**And one thing to say in the abstract:** ABC chose **not** to air the death‑curdling audio and said so on air. **That is correct editorial practice** and it is the same discipline as Airliner Archive's *"Nobody has said flutter happened here."* Credit where due.

---

## 2. 🔧 I HAVE TO CORRECT MY CORRECTION ON THE q‑PEAK

Last turn I told you the peak dynamic pressure was **~585 psf (2.9× cruise), not 850 psf (4.2×)**. **That was wrong, and my original figure was right.** Here is why, and it matters for the exercise.

My last-turn correction relied on the **pressure‑altitude** pair at the final report. But that field is the one explicitly flagged **"NO NEW VALUE."** Using a stale field to correct the data defeats the purpose.

**Re‑derived from the live GPS altitude:**

```
 17:49:16  GPS 14,806 ft   GS 362 kt
 17:49:26  GPS  6,506 ft   GS 251 kt      Δ = 8,300 ft in 10 s = 830 ft/s

 VS = 253.0 m/s      TAS = √(253.0² + 129.3²) = 284.1 m/s = 552 kt
 Mach at 6,506 ft (a = 329.9)                 = M0.86
 q = ½ × 1.0083 × 284.1²                      = 40,700 Pa = 850 psf = 4.23× cruise
```

**And the cross‑check that settles it:** the pressure‑altitude field's last live value (8,750 ft) is reached from 13,534 ft at 37,273 fpm at **17:49:24** — and the frame is stamped 17:49:26 with "NO NEW VALUE." **Perfectly self‑consistent.** The PA field froze at 17:49:24 while GPS kept descending 2,244 ft below it.

### The corrected q profile (both altitude fields, cross‑checked)

| Time | Alt (GPS) | Vertical rate | **Mach** | **q** | × cruise |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17:46 | 35,000 | level | M0.76 | 201 psf | 1.00× |
| 17:48:07 | ~36,400 | climb | M0.76 | 199 psf | 0.99× |
| 17:48:58 | 26,272 | 26,368 fpm | **M0.92** | **439 psf** | 2.18× |
| 17:49:16 | 14,806 | ~37,700 fpm | M0.82 | 573 psf | 2.85× |
| 17:49:26 | 6,506 | ~49,800 fpm | M0.86 | **850 psf** | **4.23×** |

**Impact: 6,506 ft at 49,800 fpm ⇒ ~7.8 s ⇒ ≈ 17:49:34 (12:49:34 EST).** ✅ consistent with "12:49."

> ### And a genuinely new observation:
> **The vertical‑rate profile accelerates smoothly and monotonically — 7,300 → 20,950 → 37,700 → 49,800 fpm — with no step change.** At public‑data resolution, that is **more consistent with a pitch‑down departure developing (a stall or an upset) than with a discrete control‑surface failure.** **The FDR's vertical‑acceleration and roll‑rate records would settle it in one look.**

---

## 3. 🌡️ THE CONTRADICTION THAT MATTERS MOST: **−47 °C AND "CLEAR ICE" CANNOT BOTH BE TRUE**

The expert's own explanation gives us the test — and it fails.

> **Clear (glaze) ice requires supercooled liquid water. Supercooled liquid water is concentrated at roughly 0 to +5 °C** — the "plus five Celsius level" he correctly identifies as the icing maximum, the same warm‑rain/ice‑sling zone that produced the AF447 icing. **At −47 °C, supercooled liquid water content should be essentially zero.** At that temperature you get ice crystals and rime, not clear ice.

**And the icing maximum is nowhere near the aircraft's altitude:**
> At −47 °C, the aircraft is **~50 °C below the +5 °C icing maximum.** It was in the **coldest** part of the upper troposphere, not the most severe icing zone.

### This is not a footnote. It splits the case into **two different accidents:**

| If the truth is… | Then the accident was… | And the mitigation is… |
|---|---|---|
| **OAT really was ≈ −47 °C** | **Ice‑crystal / rime** accretion in a convective updraft. The crew's **"clear ice" call was wrong** (they judged the windshield, not the wing). "Severe icing, anti‑ice can't keep up" **does not apply.** | Wing de‑ice/heat and the boot system; **wait for the updraft to stop** |
| **The "clear ice" call was right** | Then the aircraft was in **supercooled liquid water** — but that requires a temperature near 0 to +5 °C, which at 35,000 ft implies an **ISA deviation of ~55–60 °C — physically impossible.** | The AF447/Sleet mechanism: **severe icing, anti‑ice cannot keep up, automation performance invalid** |

**Either way, the “severe icing” mechanism the expert describes cannot be operating at −47 °C.** And that is enormously consequential:

> ### **The distinction decides whether the expert's whole theory is available.**
> If the icing was **SLW‑based and severe**, the anti‑ice could not keep up, the FMS performance was optimistic, and the crew were operating with an unknown, shrinking stall margin.
> **If it was ice‑crystal rime at −47 °C, the mechanism is a different one — and the "clear ice" call was simply the crew misdescribing what they saw on the windshield.**

### Four questions for the NTSB, all answerable

1. **What is the recorded OAT** from the FDR/air data system at 17:48? (And was the OAT probe ice‑covered?)
2. **The actual 16 February 2025 Miami sounding** — where were the 0 °C and +5 °C levels at 17:48? *(This is the sounding currently mis‑labelled "Nov. 1.")*
3. **The recovered wing and empennage:** rime or clear? How thick? **Where** — boots, aft of boots, tailplane?
4. **Supercooled liquid water at FL350?** If the air was −47 °C, the answer should be essentially none, and question 3's answer has to explain why.

---

## 4. ⚖️ THE EXPERT’S THEORY, ASSESSED

**Captain Scott’s mechanism is a standard, well‑documented failure mode — and it fits your ADS‑B data well. But it has one hard contradiction and one soft one.**

| His claim | Assessment |
|---|---|
| "As you go up, the air becomes less dense… the wing keeps getting steeper" — AoA increases in a climb | ✅ **Correct** — and it sets up the whole mechanism: **in a climb you are already closer to the critical AoA, with no margin to spare.** |
| "Two wings never stall symmetrically… the airplane starts to roll drastically… snap rolls back" | ✅ **Correct** — the classic drop‑leaf/autorotation description. **The 60° bank is exactly what an asymmetric stall looks like.** |
| "7 to 10 seconds to identify it’s a stall and decrease AoA by pushing forward" | ✅ **Correct and essential** — and it should be on the card. |
| "If you pull back… this situation turns into Air France 447… you will achieve rates of 23,000 ft per minute. And that's exactly what happened" | ⚠️ **Mechanism right; number wrong.** The ADS‑B altitude deltas give **37,700–49,800 fpm** in the final 25 s — roughly **twice** 23,000. 23,000 is almost certainly the crew's radio report or a media estimate. **The descent was faster than he says.** |
| "The fact that the female pilot stated we have clear ice… clear ice is not the one you want… the wing is heavier… stall speed and critical AoA start to change" | ✅ **Technically excellent** — and this is the most valuable technical content in the broadcast. **Clear ice forms aft of the boots where the system cannot reach or shed it; it adds weight and roughness.** Both raise the stall speed. |
| "Around storms the icing can accumulate so fast that the de/anti‑ice can't keep up" | ⚠️ **Depends entirely on the temperature.** See §3. At −47 °C this doesn't apply as stated. |
| "Did they have the performance to climb to 37,000? … if you've got icing in unprotected areas, **the computer's going to be wrong**" | ✅✅ **THE BEST INSIGHT IN THE BROADCAST.** The FMS's climb performance is computed for a clean wing. **In icing, the automation's performance prediction is optimistic, and the pilot must override it.** |
| "My fear is that they did not [reduce AoA] and they made the stall worse" | ⚠️ **A testable hypothesis, not a conclusion** — and it is **completely different from "she was too young."** See below. |
| "Plus five Celsius level is where supercooled liquid water exists the most" | ✅ **Correct and important** — it should be on the card, because it tells you **where the worst icing is**, and it is **not** at −47 °C. |
| "I wonder if it was working, but it just couldn't keep up" | ⚠️ Plausible **only in the SLW scenario.** At −47 °C, ice‑crystal accretion is usually slow enough for the system to cope. |

### And the thing the broadcast should not let you miss

The expert says *"the fact that the **female pilot** stated…"* and the anchor frames her the same way.

> ### **The broadcast contains two completely different allegations, and the public has merged them into one.**
>
> | Allegation | Status |
> |---|---|
> | **"She may have failed to reduce AoA after the upset, deepening the stall."** | **A specific, testable technique hypothesis.** Answerable from the FDR's AoA, pitch rate and control‑position records, and the CVR. |
> | **"She was 20, female, inexperienced."** | **Untestable from the evidence, and irrelevant unless a documented requirement is violated.** |
>
> **"She pulled instead of pushing" is not the same claim as "she wasn't good enough."** A good instructor separates them explicitly, because the first is engineering and the second is prejudice. **Your exercise should force exactly that separation.**

---

## 5. 🔬 THE ONE THING THAT COULD STILL FLIP THE CASE — AND IT’S THE WRONG SOUNDING

Put the two facts side by side:

- The **elevator drift model** puts the separation at **FL14,000–27,000**.
- The **aircraft was at 13,534–14,806 ft by 17:49:16** and at 6,506 ft by 17:49:26.

**So if the drift model is right, the elevator separated during the dive — a consequence.** ✓ My earlier reasoning holds.

**But that model used a sounding labelled "Nov. 1."** And the actual event was **16 February 2025.** A different day's wind profile shifts the whole trajectory envelope.

> ### **The wind‑drift model is the ONLY thing that separates "cause" from "consequence."**
> ### **And it may be running on the wrong day's wind.**
>
> **Re‑run it with the correct 16 February 2025 Miami sounding. If the separation band shifts ABOVE FL31,000 — above where the aircraft was at 17:48:39 — then the elevator could have been the initiating event, and the expert's stall theory becomes a secondary hypothesis rather than the primary one.**

**That is one model re‑run, and it is the highest‑leverage piece of analysis left in the public domain.** The NTSB will do it — and your class can do it first.

---

## 6. TWO NEW ITEMS FOR THE CARD AND THE EXERCISE

### 6.1 “THE COMPUTER’S GOING TO BE WRONG” — automation in ice

> **The FMS and the autothrust compute climb performance for a CLEAN wing.** In icing:
> - Actual climb performance is **worse** than the automation believes.
> - A requested altitude may be **physically unreachable** in the icing actually being encountered.
> - The **aircraft may not have the energy** to reach the level it is being cleared to.
> - Stall margin is shrinking **while the automation shows a normal, stable climb.**
>
> **The pilot's job is to override the automation when the aircraft's behaviour and the machine's model disagree.**
>
> **This also answers the expert’s question — “did they have the performance to climb to 37,000?” — and it reframes it: in icing, the answer is not knowable from the flight deck, so the assumption must be conservative by construction.**

### 6.2 “PULL VERSUS PUSH” — the 7–10 second window

```
▶ IN A STALL YOU PUSH FORWARD. It is counterintuitive because every instinct
  says pull. In a 60° bank, pulling is worse: it adds load, tightens the
  spiral, and accelerates the descent.
▶ THE RECOGNITION WINDOW IS 7–10 SECONDS in a jet — and in icing the stall
  can arrive with NO buffet, at a LOWER critical AoA than you were trained
  for, and with the wing asymmetric.
▶ YOU MUST RECOGNISE IT BEFORE THE AIRCRAFT STARTS TO MOVE, because
  by the time it is moving, the cure and the instinct have swapped places.
▶ AF447 (2009): the same dynamic — a stall at FL350, a pull, and a
  departure from which there was no recovery. Same human dynamic, DIFFERENT
  mechanism (no ice; a frozen AOA sensor). Borrow the lesson, not the cause.
```

**And a card‑worthy corollary: at 7–10 seconds and 40,000+ fpm, you are already committed.** Which is why **avoidance** is the technique, not recovery.

---

## 7. 🧭 WHERE THE EVIDENCE NOW STANDS

| Question | Best current answer | Confidence | Settled by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Did the aircraft stall? | **Possible, and consistent with the smooth rate acceleration** — but not established | Low‑Medium | FDR AoA / pitch rate |
| Did the crew pull instead of push? | **Unknown** — and a technique question, not a demographic one | — | FDR control positions + CVR |
| Was the icing severe? | **Only if supercooled liquid water was present — which −47 °C denies** | **Contradictory** | Sounding + FDR OAT + wreckage |
| Did the elevator cause it? | Probably a consequence — **unless the drift model used the wrong sounding** | Low‑Medium | Drift model re‑run with the correct sounding |
| Were the recorders recovered? | **Claimed by a TV anchor; unverified** | **Unknown** | CAROL / NTSB statement |
| When did it impact? | **≈ 17:49:34 (12:49:34 EST), 16 Feb 2025** | High | ADS‑B + wreckage |
| Peak q | **≈ 850 psf (4.2× cruise) at 6,506 ft** | Medium‑High | FDR |
| Peak Mach | **M0.92 at 17:48:58** | High | FDR |
| OAT | −47 °C reported — **inconsistent with "clear ice"** | **Contested** | FDR OAT + sounding |

---

# POST‑SUMMARY

**1. The recorders are the hinge.** *"They do have the black boxes now recovered"* contradicts earlier reporting, and **one verified fact decides whether this case is decided or open.** Verify it first.

**2. I had to correct my own correction.** The peak q is **~850 psf (4.2× cruise), not 585 psf (2.9×)** — my last‑turn figure was computed from the pressure‑altitude field **explicitly flagged "NO NEW VALUE."** **Never use a field marked stale to correct the data.** The corrected profile (201 → 439 → 573 → 850 psf) also restores Airliner Archive's Fig. 10 framing, which I had wrongly accused of overstating.

**3. The most consequential new finding is a contradiction: −47 °C and "clear ice" cannot both be true.** Clear ice needs supercooled liquid water, which peaks at **0 to +5 °C** — and at −47 °C it should be essentially nil. **That is the difference between "the anti‑ice was overwhelmed" and "the crew misdescribed rime on the windshield," and it decides whether the expert's entire theory is even available.** Four checkable questions: FDR OAT · the actual 16 Feb sounding · the wreckage ice pattern · SLW at FL350.

**4. The expert's stall theory is a standard, well‑documented mechanism and it fits the data** — the monotonic rate acceleration (7,300 → 49,800 fpm) is what a pitch‑down departure looks like. **But his “23,000 fpm” is half what the ADS‑B shows, and his “severe icing” premise fails at −47 °C.**

**5. The broadcast accidentally separates two allegations that the public has merged:** *"she may have failed to reduce AoA"* (engineering, testable) versus *"she was young and female"* (untestable, and irrelevant without a documented requirement). **Your exercise should force that separation explicitly** — and so should the card.

**6. Two card/exercise additions that came straight out of this broadcast:** **“the computer’s going to be wrong”** (in icing the automation’s performance model is optimistic — a requested level may be physically unreachable, and the pilot must override) and **“pull versus push”** (7–10 seconds to recognise a jet stall, less in ice, and in a bank pulling is fatal).

**7. And the highest‑leverage analysis left in public hands:** **re‑run the debris‑drift model with the correct 16 February 2025 Miami sounding.** The model is the only thing separating "cause" from "consequence," and it currently appears to be using a **mis‑dated** wind field. Move the separation band above FL31,000 and the elevator becomes the initiating event.

**8. Impact time, settled: ≈ 17:49:34 (12:49:34 EST) on 16 February 2025.**

---

# ✅ COFFIN CORNER CALCULATOR **v2.0** — BUILT

**Four tabs now:** **BAND** (margin + ice countdown) · **DIVE** (ADS‑B reconstruction + derived rates + impact) · **CASE** (N722BD timeline, Columbia comparison, storm calculator, open questions) · **REF** (ISA, equations, recovery, evidence discipline, self‑test)

## What changed from v1.8

| New | Detail |
|---|---|
| **ICE COUNTDOWN table** | Live: your stall margin at 0 / 5 / 10 / 12.5 / 15 / 20 % accretion, **with the margin‑zero row highlighted**. You watch it go negative. |
| **Margin‑zero & window‑closed breakpoints** | Computed live: *"margin reaches ZERO at 13.8 % ice; policy window closed at 10.5 %."* |
| **Icing‑type consistency check** | Flags **"clear ice at −47 °C is impossible"** and flags the **0–+5 °C supercooled‑liquid‑water maximum** separately. |
| **"The computer's going to be wrong"** | Standing advisory whenever ice > 0: FMS/autothrust climb performance assumes a clean wing; a cleared level may be physically unreachable. |
| **Storm avoidance calculator** | Distance to the cell's **most intense part** + your ground speed → time available, with the **8 NM = 64 s / 10 NM = 80 s / 20 NM = 160 s** arithmetic. |
| **DIVE: derived vertical rates** | Parses the timestamps and shows the **true rate vs the reported field**, flagging **FIELD SATURATED** and the % understatement. |
| **DIVE: impact estimate** | Uses the **derived** rate, not the saturated field. Reports time to ground, impact TAS, Mach at the last point and at sea level, and impact q. |
| **CASE tab** | N722BD timeline with **MEASURED / REPORTED / MODELLED / DRAWN / UNKNOWN** labels; Columbia‑vs‑Palm‑Beach comparison; "what should have been recognised"; the seven open NTSB questions. |
| **Corrections** | Sound speed in **knots** with mph cross‑check; **tropopause handled** (a = 497.9 kt constant above FL360); **a·√σ in knots**; **q‑peak corrected to 850 psf (4.23×)**; impact ≈ **12:49:34 EST**. |
| **Self‑test expanded** | **24 checks**, including the FL35,250 case, the ice breakpoints, the storm reaction times, and the dive q values. |

## Validation it must reproduce

|---|---|
| **FL35,250, OAT −47, +10 % ice, M0.76** | stall margin **+7.0 kt** · policy window **0.87 kt** (M0.771–M0.774) · **margin zero at 13.8 % ice** · **window closed at 10.5 %** · q **199 psf** |

<h2>N722BD - 16 February 2025 - Palm Beach County, Florida</h2>
<div class="hint" style="margin-bottom:8px">Learjet 60, Austin -&gt; Nassau. Nine on board, all killed. Left elevator separated before impact.
EVIDENCE LABELS: <span class="tag m">MEASURED</span> <span class="tag r">REPORTED</span> <span class="tag d">MODELLED</span> <span class="tag">DRAWN</span> <span class="tag">UNKNOWN</span>
A probable cause is ONE SENTENCE. The compression is in the PRODUCT, not the newsroom.</div>
<div class="tw"><table><thead><tr><th>Time (EST)</th><th>Event</th><th>Label</th><th>Source</th></tr></thead><tbody>

<div class="card">
<h2>Two accidents, one failure archetype</h2>
<div class="tw"><table><thead><tr><th></th><th>COLUMBIA N999LJ - 19 Sep 2008</th><th>PALM BEACH N722BD - 16 Feb 2025</th></tr></thead><tbody>
<tr><td>Initiation</td><td>Tyres ~140 psi vs 219 spec</td><td>Ice, convective cell, FL350</td></tr>
<tr><td>What the aircraft "believed"</td><td>Squat switch said "airborne" -&gt; reversers stowed -&gt; FORWARD THRUST</td><td>The wing could no longer carry the same load</td></tr>
<tr><td>What it did</td><td>Exactly what its inputs dictated</td><td>Exactly what the aerodynamics dictated</td></tr>
<tr><td>Cockpit said</td><td>NOTHING. No light, no voice, no lever movement</td><td>NOTHING. No stall-speed indicator exists</td></tr>
<tr><td>The cure</td><td>PUSH LEVERS FORWARD - opposite of instinct</td><td>DESCEND</td></tr>
<tr><td>Prior warning?</td><td>YES - Troy AL, 14 Jan 2001, same mechanism, two pilots seriously injured</td><td>MAYBE - May 2024 severe turbulence on THIS airframe; AMM special inspection required?</td></tr>
<tr><td>Public story</td><td>"The captain panicked"</td><td>"The 20-year-old copilot"</td></tr>
<tr><td>Held by</td><td>Both pilots - dead</td><td>All nine - dead</td></tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<div class="nb info" style="margin-top:8px"><b>THE ONE-LINE WHY:</b> the aircraft did not represent its own state. In both, the pilot was CORRECT for the state the cockpit showed and CATASTROPHIC for the state the aircraft was actually in. Neither was caused by not knowing the procedures.
<br><b>THE QUESTION TO CARRY:</b> Columbia asked "did anyone examine what happens when the wheel well is destroyed on the ground?" Palm Beach asks "did anyone examine what happens to the empennage at high Mach, with ice, aft CG, at max landing weight?" BOTH ARE CERTIFICATION-BASIS QUESTIONS.</div>
</div>

<div class="card">
<h2>Storm avoidance - why 8 miles is not a safe blanket rule</h2>
<div class="hint" style="margin-bottom:6px">STANDARDS: 10 NM from ANY thunderstorm | 20 NM from SEVERE | 20 NM IFR avoidance.
Measure from the MOST INTENSE PART of the cell, never from the nearest visible echo.</div>
<div class="row">
<div><label>Distance to the cell's most intense part (NM)</label><input id="sDist" type="number" step="1" value="8"></div>
<div><label>Your ground speed (kt)</label><input id="sGs" type="number" step="10" value="450"></div>
</div>
<div class="kv" id="sKv" style="margin-top:8px"></div>
<div class="nb bad" style="margin-top:8px"><b>WHY 8 NM FAILS - FOUR REASONS</b><br>
1 REACTION TIME - 8 NM is only 64 seconds at 450 kt. You cannot clear a cell, get a clearance and descend in 64 seconds. 20 NM is 160.<br>
2 THE HAZARD EXTENDS BEYOND THE RAIN - microburst outflow and damaging wind reach 10-20 NM from the core; hail travels far downwind.<br>
3 REFLECTIVITY IS NOT THE MEASURE - in THIS accident the aircraft flew through 25-35 dBZ at FL350 while the violent core was 10,000 ft BELOW it. A pilot looking at radar at the right flight level could have seen something that did not look like a major storm. The upper flank of a cell, where the updraft, shear and icing live, can look MILD where you are and be lethal.<br>
4 CELLS MOVE AND GROW - the buffer erodes while you are using it.</div>
</div>

<div class="card">
<h2>What should have been recognised BEFORE the upset</h2>
<ul class="notes">
<li><b>In cloud with ice, your stall speed is rising and nothing shows it.</b> At constant Mach, IAS falls about 5.7 kt per 1,000 ft in a climb - <b>if it falls FASTER, ice is eating your margin.</b> That trend is the only instrument that sees it.</li>
<li><b>Same thrust, less climb than the book = ice.</b> SEP falling with no speed change = ice.</li>
<li><b>The computer's going to be wrong.</b> FMS and autothrust climb performance assumes a CLEAN wing. In icing, actual performance is worse than displayed, and the cleared level may be physically unreachable. Override the automation when the aircraft behaves differently from the machine model.</li>
<li><b>Climbing to get above weather is backwards when the freezing level is below you.</b> The safe direction is DOWN and OUT.</li>
<li><b>"Overspeed" is a SYMPTOM of the dive, not the cause.</b> The likely reaction - pull to slow down - tightens the spiral in a bank and is fatal.</li>
<li><b>Stall recognition window in a jet: 7 to 10 seconds.</b> With ice the stall can arrive with NO buffet, at a LOWER critical AoA than trained, and asymmetric. The cure is PUSH FORWARD - counterintuitive, and the instinct (pull) is what makes it fatal. AF447 (2009): the same human dynamic, a different mechanism (no ice; a frozen AOA sensor). Borrow the lesson, not the cause.</li>
<li><b>Severe turbulence severity is judged by aircraft performance, not comfort.</b> "Unable to hold altitude" = SEVERE. Report it that way, and treat it as a maintenance event.</li>
</ul>
</div>

<div class="card">
<h2>Open questions for the investigation</h2>
<ol class="notes">
<li><b>Were the recorders recovered?</b> One verified fact decides whether this case is open or closed.</li>
<li><b>The recorded OAT vs the "clear ice" call.</b> Clear ice requires supercooled liquid water, which peaks near 0 to +5 deg C. At -47 deg C it should be essentially nil - so either the temperature or the ice-type call is wrong, and the two scenarios are DIFFERENT accidents.</li>
<li><b>The correct 16 Feb 2025 Miami sounding.</b> The published sounding graphic is labelled "Nov. 1". The debris-drift model is the ONLY thing separating "cause" from "consequence" - and it may be running on the wrong day's wind.</li>
<li><b>Was the performance to climb to FL370 actually available in the icing encountered?</b> And did the automation's clean-wing model mislead the crew?</li>
<li><b>Was the elevator separation the CAUSE or a CONSEQUENCE?</b> Requires the wreckage: fracture surfaces, bend direction, right elevator condition, ice accretion pattern.</li>
<li><b>Was the May 2024 severe-turbulence inspection done?</b> The one concrete institutional thread in the case.</li>
<li><b>Any AD or SB on the empennage, elevator attach fittings, hinge or trim actuator</b> for this serial? And the MEL status of the flight controls?</li>
</ol>
</div>
</div>

<!-- ============ REF ============ -->
<div class="pane" id="pR">
<div class="card">
<h2>ISA reference atmosphere - sound speed in KNOTS</h2>
<div class="nb info"><b>Unit check.</b> At 35,000 ft, a = 500.5 kt = 576.2 mph. The mph figure is easily mistaken for a knot figure.
<b>Tropopause:</b> the ISA is isothermal above 36,089 ft (11,000 m), so <b>a = 497.9 kt at every level from FL370 to FL450</b> - it stops falling with altitude. Above FL360 the stall Mach rises from DENSITY ALONE.</div>
<div class="tw"><table id="isa"><thead><tr><th>Alt ft</th><th>a kt</th><th>a mph</th><th>T deg C</th><th>sigma</th><th>sqrt sigma</th><th>a*sqrt(sigma) kt</th><th>IAS at M0.76</th></tr></thead><tbody></tbody></table></div>
</div>
<div class="card">
<h2>Band table (Vs 185 KIAS; ceiling M0.78 above FL370, M0.81 below)</h2>
<div class="tw"><table id="btStatic"><thead><tr><th>Alt</th><th>Mstall</th><th>Ceiling</th><th>Raw band</th><th>10/10 window</th></tr></thead><tbody></tbody></table></div>
<div class="hint">Vs 185 KIAS is an ILLUSTRATIVE chart-implied value. Closure altitude depends on type, weight, configuration and ice.</div>
</div>
<div class="card">
<h2>Equations and assumptions</h2>
<pre>ATMOSPHERE - ICAO US Standard Atmosphere 1976-80
  h_m = alt_ft*0.3048 ;  T_ISA = 288.15 - 0.0065*h_m  [K]
  TROPOPAUSE at h_m = 11,000 m: T = 216.65 K, CONSTANT above.
  p = 101325*(1 - 0.0065*h_m/288.15)^5.25588  [Pa]
      above 11,000 m: p = 22632.1*exp(-g*(h-11000)/(R*216.65))
  T = OAT+273.15 (or T_ISA) ; rho = p/(R*T), R = 287.05287 ; sigma = rho/1.225
  a = sqrt(1.4*R*T) = m/s -> x1.68781 = kt -> x1.15078 = mph
  a*sqrt(sigma) = a_kt*sqrt(sigma)   [kt]  (IAS per unit Mach)

STALL
  Vs(W)   = Vs_ref*sqrt(W/W_ref) + config_inc   [KIAS]
  Vs_ice  = Vs(W)*(1 + ice%/100)
  Mstall  = Vs_ice/(a*sqrt(sigma))

CEILING (more restrictive limit always binds)
  MMO_eff = stepped Mach if the step applies, else general MMO
  M_VMO   = VMO(KIAS)/(a*sqrt(sigma))
  Ceiling = min(MMO_eff, M_VMO)

POLICY WINDOW
  Floor = (Vs_ice + stall_margin)/(a*sqrt(sigma))
  Ceiling_pol = Ceiling - overspeed_margin/(a*sqrt(sigma))
  If Floor >= Ceiling_pol -> BAND CLOSED -> NO-GO at any Mach

ICE COUNTDOWN
  margin_kt = IAS_now - Vs_ice ; zero when ice% = (IAS_now/Vs_W - 1)*100
  policy window closes when ice% = ((Ceiling_ias - overspeed_margin)/Vs_W - 1)*100

q
  V = Mach*a ; q = 0.5*rho*V^2 (1 psf = 47.8803 Pa)
  q_ratio = (rho/rho_ref)*(V/V_ref)^2  ref: 35,000 ft M0.76 ISA, q = 201 psf

IDENTITY: a*sqrt(sigma) = sqrt(gamma*p/rho0). The IAS at a given Mach and
  PRESSURE ALTITUDE is independent of temperature. OAT changes Mach, TAS and q.

ASSUMPTIONS
  - Weight scaling of stall speed is first-order (Vs with sqrt(W)).
  - Pressure taken at the 29.92-referenced PRESSURE ALTITUDE; OAT assumed valid there.
  - The estimated geometric altitude is a first-order approximation.
  - q is a structural-load proxy, not a certified limit.
  - Steady flight only. No gusts, manoeuvre loads, flutter or upset dynamics.</pre>
</div>
<div class="card">
<h2>When you are already in it</h2>
<div class="tw"><table><thead><tr><th>Condition</th><th>Action</th></tr></thead><tbody>
<tr><td>ICING IN CLOUD</td><td>Descend DECISIVELY, all the way OUT, below the freezing level. Never level off inside it.</td></tr>
<tr><td>ICED WING STALL</td><td>Full nose-up, max thrust, wings level, ACCEPT the altitude loss. Expect NO buffet warning. 7-10 seconds.</td></tr>
<tr><td>TAILPLANE STALL</td><td>NOSE DOWN - reduce AoA. Pulling makes it worse.</td></tr>
<tr><td>SPIRAL DIVE</td><td>Roll toward level WHILE bringing the nose to the horizon. Do NOT pull to stop the descent. Manage speed with thrust.</td></tr>
<tr><td>ELEVATOR GONE</td><td>Assume little pitch authority. Control roll, thrust and flight path. Level - do not spiral.</td></tr>
<tr><td>ANY OF THE ABOVE</td><td>DECLARE EARLY. A stabilized descent beats a fast spiral, and you get radar sooner.</td></tr>
<tr><td>SEVERE TURBULENCE</td><td>Report it as severe. It is a MAINTENANCE event - AMM special inspection before further flight.</td></tr>
</tbody></table></div>
</div>
<div class="card">
<h2>Evidence discipline</h2>
<div class="tw"><table><thead><tr><th>Label</th><th>Meaning</th><th>How to use it</th></tr></thead><tbody>
<tr><td>MEASURED</td><td>An instrument recorded it</td><td>Teach with attribution</td></tr>
<tr><td>REPORTED</td><td>A human said it</td><td>Teach with attribution</td></tr>
<tr><td>MODELLED</td><td>Calculated from data by a stated method</td><td>Teach as a hypothesis, show the method</td></tr>
<tr><td>DRAWN</td><td>An illustrator drew it</td><td>Never teach as fact</td></tr>
<tr><td>UNKNOWN</td><td>The investigation has not answered it</td><td>Teach as an open question</td></tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<div class="hint" style="margin-top:6px">LADDER (highest first): NTSB findings -&gt; FDR/CVR -&gt; wreckage -&gt; raw ATC audio -&gt; weather/radar -&gt; flight tracking -&gt; EDITORIAL RECONSTRUCTIONS -&gt; social media.</div>
</div>

function isaAt(hf){
 var hm=Math.max(0,Math.min(65000,hf||0))*0.3048,T,p;
 if(hm<=11000){T=T0-LC*hm;p=P0*Math.pow(1-LC*hm/T0,5.25588);}
 else{T=216.65;p=22632.1*Math.exp(-G*(hm-11000)/(R*T));}
 return {T:T,p:p,Tc:T-273.15,hm:hm};
}
function atmos(hf,oat){
 var I=isaAt(hf),hv=(oat!==null&&!isNaN(oat)),T=hv?oat+273.15:I.T;
 var rho=I.p/(R*T),sig=rho/RHO0,aMs=Math.sqrt(GA*R*T),aKt=aMs*KT,dev=hv?I.Tc-oat:0;
 return {I:I,T:T,Tc:I.Tc,p:I.p,rho:rho,sig:sig,ss:Math.sqrt(sig),aMs:aMs,aKt:aKt,aMph:aMs*MPH,dev:dev,
  aSig:aKt*Math.sqrt(sig),geom:hv?hf*(1-dev/T0):null};
}
